With Protests Quieting Down But Not Forgotten, Dealers Return to the Grand Palais for Paris Photo

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

As soft music crooned through the halls of a softly lit, glittering Grand Palais in Paris Tuesday evening, VIPs trickled in for a private dinner hosted by the Prix Pictet as part of the Paris Photo fair, which opens to the public on November 12. Wandering through the fair’s aisles featuring 246 exhibitors this year, it was unsettling to imagine that just three weeks ago, Art Basel Paris had to evacuate the very same venue. Booths were dismantled early, the night before protests planned for October 24. The fair’s closure two days sooner than planned was unprecedented and came as a shock to many, ultimately necessitating Art Basel to offer a 20 percent discount on booth fees to all exhibitors. Related Articles The Art Market Wanted to Go Global--It Didn't Plan for Everything That Means Dealers Scramble to Get Works Out of Paris After Surprise Art Basel Announcement

Though civil unrest in France has not returned to the fever pitch experienced in October, and the government has exerted some greater control over the situation, demonstrations are continuing here, in an effort to bring attention to rising costs of living and under-funded public schools. How then, are Paris Photo organizers and exhibitors navigating it all?

“We are, of course, watching the situation closely to see how things evolve,” Photo Paris director Florence Bourgeois told ARTnews. “At this stage there is nothing new to report, and nothing alarming. In fact, for two weeks the demonstrations have been taking place peacefully, and generally in the east of Paris. So we are not alarmed, and we remain vigilant so that we can respond if necessary.”

Bourgeois said that the fair has closed early once before, after the 2015 terror attacks in Paris; despite lacking appropriate insurance at the time, organizers reimbursed 20 percent of booth fees. “But that’s no indication of what might happen in 2026,” she said. “It was an extremely different situation to that of the current protests.” (She declined to comment further on the fair’s current insurance policy.)

“If we ever do face something along those lines, our decision would be made based on protecting everyone’s safety. That’s the priority,” she added.

For German gallerist Thomas Zander, who exhibited at the shortened Art Basel Paris and is now back for Paris Photo, the city is the biggest draw. “It’s strange in a way, but I love Paris,” he said about returning so soon. He characterized this year’s Art Basel Paris as inspiring a “difficult feeling” in him. The early closure cost him “two good days” of sales, he said, adding, “Now we are in a better situation this week, and there are not so many demonstrations in the center, and it’s quieter than the week of Art Basel Paris.”

At the fair, his booth has on view what he described as “museum-quality works” because “all the museum people come here, to Paris Photo.” This includes a set of six dye transfer unique master prints by William Eggleston from 1968 to 1973, priced together at €1 million ($1.11 million); a rare series of 41 vintage gelatin silver prints of pinball machines by Candida Höfer, which are being sold as a single display for €600,000 ($665,570); and 16 works from Zoe Leonard’s “Analogue” series (1998–2009) about New York’s changing Lower East Side and offered for a total of €140,000 ($155,300).

In terms of price point, Paris Photo is a much more accessible fair than Art Basel Paris, where artworks are frequently offered in the six-, seven-, and even eight-figures. While some exhibitors at Paris Photo might make a sale over $1 million, the prices for other works can start as low as a few hundred dollars, with artist-signed books even costing under $100.

Despite having lower prices than the markets for contemporary painting and sculpture, the photography market has not been immune to the rest of the cooling that the global art market has faced in the past few years, according to Bourgeois, who said that more than ever closing a sale “comes down to a question of quality, which is why we’ve worked hard to represent the best that photography has produced over the last 200 years.” Even still, “for top quality photographic works, prices remain high and are not falling,” she added.

Several dealers said that approachability and a perceived distancing from the billionaire’s club of collectors often play to the favor of Paris Photo, as protestors demand greater social equality. (In October, there were calls to protest outside Art Basel Paris and “take back the Grand Palais” from the wealthy few.)

“By taking part in a fair, we are not ignoring the realities of life around us, nor disconnected from it,” Bourgeois said. “We also represent part of the economy: there are galleries that make a living from this, that represent artists, give them visibility and support them by selling their work. I believe all of this is very important.”

“I’ve been hearing from a lot of people that Paris Photo doesn’t have quite the same image as Art Basel,” Paris dealer Anne-Laure Buffard said. “It’s a fair that has less of a connotation symbolizing a certain concentration of wealth, or an elitism reserved for the extremely rich.” With the recent closure in mind, in light of the protesters who have calmed, Buffard said, “We’re all trying to stay optimistic.”

She is presenting salt-washed and painted, crystalline photographs taken in the Dead Sea, on the border between Israel, Jordan, and the West Bank, by Ilanit Illouz, priced from about $2,000 to $17,750; as well as painted and drawn photographs by Vasantha Yogananthan chronicling the impact of tourism on the northern Indian region of the Himalayas, priced between about $3,300 and $11,100.

Elsewhere, Pace Gallery is offering a jewel of a 1926 vintage gelatin silver print on a treated postcard by André Kertész for $500,000. It is thought to be one of only six or seven in the world. On the more affordable end, medium-sized still life photographs by Kiki Smith have already sold for $8,500 apiece.

“It’s been a very strong first day, with a number of significant sales made in the first hours of the fair, reflecting the strong demand for photography,” Pace senior vice president Lauren Panzo said. “It’s particularly encouraging to see so many new collectors coming through” alongside existing clients.

On Tuesday evening, Panzo told ARTnews that while the gallery has considered the possibility of further interruptions to the fair from ongoing protests, she is “personally not concerned—there are always protests here,” and the current ones seem to be more “under control.” She expects that over the weekend there will be lines to get into the Grand Palais as there have been in years past. “People in Paris love photography. I think there’s such a love for this medium. It’s so accessible,” she said. “Photography is different.”

Greeting visitors upon entering the Grand Palais is a large installation of works spanning the career of German photographer Thomas Ruff, a collaboration by his five galleries: David Zwirner, Konrad Fischer, Mai 36, Lia Rumma, Sprüth Magers. Other highlights include a selection spotlighting women photographers, who now make up 38 percent of artists represented at the fair, up from 20 percent when the program launched in 2018, and around 100 newly acquired and unseen photographs from the Centre Pompidou’s collection. The Prix Pictet also now has a dedicated booth, featuring works by this year’s winner: Valérie Belin’s Still Life, showing an accumulation of plastic goods and memento mori, and Alfredo Jaar’s The End, which documents the receding Great Salt Lake in Utah.

A curated selection of artworks spread throughout the Grand Palais represents every decade since the creation of photography, which is marking its 200th anniversary this year. On one end are Serge Plantureux and Atelier 41, which have on view works by the pioneer photographer, Joseph Nicéphore Niépce, who made what is considered the world’s oldest known existing photograph, View from the Window at Le Gras (1825).

Among contemporary examples in the Voices section is an installation of new works by Alessandro Calabrese in the booth of Milan’s Viasaterna gallery. Priced between $2,440 and $8,430, these dreamy images are made by printing on the “wrong” side of glossy photo paper, resulting in painterly, electric-hued prints. “In the end, it creates a sort of watercolor painting, but [made] automatically,” Calabrese told ARTnews. “All my research is based on looking for the limit of the figurative side and the realism in photography, and trying to challenge it.”

Warby Parker and Gentle Monster Are Launching Smart Glasses. Does Anyone Want Them?

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

This is the November 11, 2026 edition of Fit Check, your front row seat to the power players, peacocks, and deep pockets of fashion—and how they influence the rest of the world.

It’s been a meme-worthy news week: the new Met Gala theme launched endless online discourse, Jensen Huang ignited the internet with news of his affair with an alleged Chinese spy, then fashion netizens had a time memeifying him as a Hedi-boy, and Maybelline changed its slogan with some very confusing punctuation.

IRL, Gentle Monster and Warby Parker are getting into the smart glasses space. Like Meta, they’re working hard to get fashion’s co-sign in order to have tastemakers influence everyone else to wear them, though I have yet to find a fashion person happy to wear these without a check involved. And this week, Alex Consani video-called me to discuss her acting ambitions after her debut in American Horror Story: 13. She’s already filmed two movies coming next year.

Mentioned in this issue: Iron Man, Robert Downey Jr., Kylie Jenner, Timothée Chalamet’s security guard, Steven Meisel, Martine Rose discourse, Luca de Meo, Woke 3.0, Hedi Slimane, Slimaniacs, Sarah Paulson, Ryan Murphy, and many more.

Amelia Gray at the Gentle Monster launch dinner.

Last night Warby Parker and Gentle Monster celebrated the launches of their “intelligent eyewear” lines (not to be confused with Meta’s “smart glasses”), both developed with Google and Samsung. Warby Parker hosted a literary activation with Vogue, and Gentle Monster held a “preview experience” and dinner attended by Alex Consani, Anok Yai, Charles Melton, Joaquin Phoenix, and more.

In June, Meta unveiled a range of smart glasses that built on its existing efforts with Ray-Ban and Oakley. One of the frames was designed with Kylie Jenner, who attended a rollout event in New York alongside an incognito Timothée Chalamet. I got in trouble with the couple’s security guard for trying to snap a pic of the actor in what felt like an apropos parallel to the controversy Meta has faced with this product. Many people don’t want their photo taken without consent, and smart glasses can facilitate this kind of invasion of privacy. In September, Meta released a camera-less pair. That same month, Snap introduced augmented-reality glasses, first seen in June in a campaign by Steven Meisel featuring Kaia Gerber, Jack Harlow, and others. The tech world is working overtime to secure fashion’s buy-in to build hype.

Snap also tapped Robert Downey Jr. to star in videos showcasing its technology, Iron Man–style. Phoenix appeared in Gentle Monster’s launch campaign in a callback to his role in Her, in which he plays a man who falls in love with an AI voiced by Scarlett Johansson. (In 2024, she had a dispute with OpenAI after learning the company had created a voice that sounded similar to hers for its AI, which she had declined to license for ChatGPT. Sam Altman denied any intentional imitation.) Leveraging two movie stars who’ve been the face of this kind of technology in film to bring their products to the masses is a cunning move from Google and Samsung. Yet it’s possible that no co-sign can combat the anxiety around AI and its surrounding gadgets. There is a budding battle in the smart glasses space—but does anyone actually want them?

Martine Rose is officially taking over from Pierpaolo Piccioli at Balenciaga. I still think he could have been right for the brand, but he came at the wrong time. Balenciaga is essentially Kering’s youth vehicle after a decade under Demna, and Piccioli just wasn’t the designer to fulfill that brief. That his collections seemed to try to honor both Demna’s casualwear range and his own couture-like sensibility only muddled the brand’s remit further. But Rose, who worked with Demna on his Balenciaga menswear early on, is a much better fit in this context, and she has the industry’s goodwill—fans have been advocating her ascent to a role like this one for years.

Rose is the first official hire under Luca de Meo’s tenure as Kering’s CEO, and she is walking into a brave new world. De Meo believes Kering has been overly dependent on its creative directors, and is looking to streamline operations with a new strategy dubbed “ReconKering.” If reports are confirmed that Chemena Kamali will head to Saint Laurent and Virginia Burlina to McQueen, four out of five creative directors at Kering will soon be women. Along with Rose and Louise Trotter at Bottega Veneta, these are designers who are better known as prolific workhorses rather than buzzy household names. De Meo is evidently looking to grow beyond the star creative director strategy that defined fashion in the 2010s, with Demna as an exception. What this means for Rose and Balenciaga, and for Saint Laurent and McQueen, which have historically depended on the persona of their creative leads, remains to be seen.

Balenciaga is back in the news this week with the opening of the first of several activations around the 25th anniversary of its Le City bag, arguably the most enduring of It bags after the Birkin. Damien Hirst has hand-painted 25 bags for the occasion, and Nicole Richie is fronting the campaign in a callback to the aughts. This was planned prior to Piccioli’s exit, yet it’s taking place in between creative directors speaks to an undeniable fact that plays right into ReconKering: These brands are bigger than their designers, despite how important these appointments are to industry insiders and fanatics.

A look from Jun Takahashi’s fall-winter 2024 collection for Undercover, which will be included in the upcoming Met Costume Institute exhibition.

In case you missed it: The Met announced on Thursday the replacement exhibition for the John Galliano blockbuster postponed earlier this year. “ExtraORDINARY: Fashion and the Poetics of the Everyday” will focus on how designers have explored familiar, ordinary garments: jeans, T-shirts, workwear, and more. It sounds like both an ode to normcore and to the ingenuity with which designers like Matthieu Blazy, Demna, Jun Takahashi, and more have reimagined the most simple of clothing. Andrew Bolton, the curator in charge of the Costume Institute, told Vogue he “didn’t have a plan B” after Galliano and that he wanted the replacement show to be “challenging and provocative.” The announcement drew both positive and negative reactions. Its main criticism is its simplicity, especially when compared to Galliano’s extravagance. Bolton has a singular way of placing fashion in larger cultural contexts to underscore its historic importance—I’m curious to see what he’ll do when the fashion he’s dealing with already exists in an atmosphere that requires little explanation.

Maybelline has updated its famous slogan, “Maybe she’s born with it. Maybe it’s Maybelline,” which was first introduced in 1991, to a more democratic, inclusive riff: “Maybe. We’re born with it. Maybe. It’s Maybelline.” Confusing punctuation aside, the change feels like it belongs to the late 2010s era of DEI marketing. It’s true that binary pronouns have fallen out of style, particularly at the makeup counter, even if brands shied away from this kind of revamp in the Trump era. But then again, after last week’s blue wave in the midterm elections, maybe Maybelline is just in time.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang waves to the media as he arrives at Gimpo airport in Seoul on June 5, 2026 wearing a Dior jacket. Images from this same outing were modified with AI by fashion netizens.

The news of Jensen Huang’s affair filled X with memes about Chinese spies and mistresses in a variety of configurations. By Monday evening, Huang had reached the fashion world’s stylish netizens: the Instagram account @hidden.ny, an anonymous digital mood board and cultural platform, posted a series of images of Huang in a studded leather jacket. The caption read: “NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang in Hedi Slimane SLP Studded Leather Jacket after admitting to affair with alleged Chinese spy.”

It’s well-known that Huang is a leather jacket enthusiast. But despite Huang’s penchant for a black leather moment, it appears he was not, in fact, wearing the Saint Laurent jacket. A quick run through an AI image detector debunked Huang being a “Slimaniac,” at least in this particular case. SynthID detected that the images posted by Hidden and other users online were generated or edited with OpenAI. To burst the bubble even further, it appears the images used as the base for this edit weren’t taken after the disclosure of Huang’s affair, but during a trip to South Korea in June. Apologies to the menswear crowd for ruining the fantasy.

How To Make Fewer Grocery Trips & Still Eat Well

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Groceries cost a fortune, gas isn’t cheap… every “quick” run to the store somehow costs you 60 bucks or more. Experts share how to shop less and still feed your family well.

It’s a phenomenon that happens more often than not these days: You run into the store for milk, cheese, eggs, and a few other basics, and walk out wondering how in the hell a small handful of grocery bags just set you back upwards of 100 bucks. Worse, you then have to play budget Tetris, moving money around to make sure you have funds for the inevitable grocery run you’ll have to make next week. Add in the gas it takes to get to and from, and you’re spent literally and figuratively.

The reality is groceries are still painfully expensive, and plenty of families are stretched thinner than ever. And, sure, cutting back on trips is probably one of the easiest and most logical ways to save money (and time, and sanity). But it begs the question: How long can you go between grocery runs without your family surviving on cereal benders?

We asked food and budgeting experts how they personally make it work.

Most of the experts we polled said one big shop a week is sort of the sweet spot. “Every extra trip not only takes up valuable time, but is also another chance to grab things that aren’t on the list and drive up your overall grocery bill,” says Laurie Hise, CEO of Passionate Penny Pincher.

Is it possible to stretch it to every two weeks? Maybe. But AJ Prasaguet, head of the iconic Palace bar and restaurant in Miami Beach, points out that biweekly shopping “can work if you have the storage space, freezer capacity, and money available upfront. Those are real considerations, and not every household has all three.”

Amber Hanson, a licensed nutritionist and founder of Whole You Nutrition, emphasizes that there’s no cut-and-dry, one-size-fits-all answer. Your ideal grocery-shopping cadence will ultimately depend on things like how close you live to a store and when your paycheck hits the bank. If you get paid every two weeks, it might make sense to shop on that timeframe too.

Before you make your little grocery list and head to the store, open up your own fridge, freezer, and pantry. “The best way to save money is to use what you have already paid for,” says Hanson.

Jenn Lueke, New York Times-bestselling author of Don’t Think About Dinner, does her inventory every Friday night, then builds the next week’s meals around what’s left. Example: “If I know I have a half carton of broth that needs to be used, I’ll make sure to pick up a grain, like rice or quinoa, that I can cook in it.”

Prasaguet goes one step further. “Give an ingredient its next job before you buy it. If a recipe needs half a container of something, decide where the other half is going.” Take cabbage, for instance. It can be slaw one night, stir-fry the next, and soup filler after that.

Another tip Hise is a fan of is ordering online for pickup or delivery when you can. The simple act of skipping the aisles means you don't have to worry about impulse buys wreaking havoc on your total.

As a busy working mom who regularly feels she has to have everything for the week figured out at all times, this was very liberating to hear! As the experts remind us, life happens. Plans change. A rigid seven-night menu is a fast track to wasted food.

But if it makes you feel better to have the week mostly lined up, you could follow Hise’s example: plan five “fresh” dinners a week, leaving one night for leftovers and one for a meal pulled from what’s already in the pantry, fridge, or freezer. Prasaguet relies on a similar formula: four planned dinners, one leftovers meal, one pantry or freezer dinner, on flex night. “That gives the week some structure without pretending everything will go according to plan,” he says.

Lueke recommends starting with dinners, then exploring how you can make the most of them. “Will the slow-cooker chicken stretch to cover lunches for half the week too? Can I make a double batch of quinoa and bulk up our mid-week sheet pan dinner?” Theme nights like Taco Tuesday or Breakfast for Dinner also help reduce decision fatigue.

The trick to making groceries last, many of the pros explained, is buying foods with different lifespans and eating them in the right order. “You do not want an entire cart of produce that all needs attention at once,” emphasizes Prasaguet.

Andrea Woroch, a consumer savings expert, notes that some hardy produce (think garlic, potatoes, sweet potatoes, winter squash, even whole apples) can last for months depending on storage conditions as well as how they’re handled. For fast-spoilers like berries, resist the urge to buy the giant fresh packs unless you know your family will gobble them down. Otherwise, stock up on frozen.

If you're afraid frozen and canned food won't pack the same nutritional punch as fresh, well, the experts beg to differ.

“One of the biggest misconceptions about healthy eating is that fresh foods are always better than frozen or canned alternatives. That’s simply not the case,” says Jennifer Pallian, a registered dietitian and founder of Foodess. Frozen vegetables are typically flash-frozen shortly after harvest, and research has found they can be just as nutritious as fresh produce that’s been sitting in your fridge for several days. In some cases, they even have more vitamin C.”

Don’t turn your nose up at canned staples, either. Pallian is a big proponent of canned light tuna (a solid source of protein, selenium, and omega-3s) and canned chickpeas, which pack both protein and fiber and are already cooked.

Just opt for low-sodium or no-salt-added canned veggies and beans, and be mindful of foods packed in juices, syrups, or sauces.

Adopting a few little habits can go a long way toward making food last until your next grocery run:

However, Prasaguet reminds us, “Stretching groceries should never mean stretching food safety.” That means refrigerating leftovers within two hours, eating them within three to four days, or sticking them in the freezer.

You should know that eating well doesn’t mean you have to buy a bunch of fancy superfoods. “Some of the most nutrient-dense foods in the grocery store are also the cheapest,” Hanson says.

Here’s what our experts say they keep stocked:

And while many of us think shopping in bulk must save money, Prasaguet notes that it’s “only better value if you can afford it, store it, and finish it.” Hise’s workaround is to grab a few extra cans whenever they’re on sale. “A few extra cans a month can add up to a pantry that carries you through a tight month.”

If you feel like a failure every time you serve your family dinner out of a bag or can, it’s high time you cut yourself some slack.

“Frozen and canned foods that you actually eat beat fresh vegetables you throw away every time,” says Hise. Link agrees, adding, “I’d much rather see a family use convenient foods they enjoy than buy fresh ingredients that become a science experiment in the crisper drawer.”

“A fed family is a win, and ‘fresh’ isn’t the measure of a good dinner," Lueke puts it simply. A balanced meal is made up of a protein, a plant or two, and something filling. It truly does not matter if those came from the produce section or the freezer aisle.

But maybe Prasaguet sums it up best. “You do not have to prove your love through a complicated dinner,” he reassures. “Feeding your family with the resources you have is already an act of care.”

1967 Ford Ranchero For Sale With 3,100 Miles Shown Is 351 V8 Swapped, Bright Red And Ready To Roll

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

I'm known around these parts as a ute fan. Any car you can dream up, I've probably come across it built into a ute, and I've almost certainly liked that version better than the original. Once in a while, though, we have to pay respects to the originators of the ute life — the El Caminos, the Holdens, and of course the ever-gorgeous Ford Rancheros. Take this one, for instance: A 351-swapped 1967 Fairlane Ranchero that's not a hideous restomod.

This Ranchero, while modified and maintained over the course of its life, is gloriously free from any terrible four-spoke steering wheels or gaudy blue LED gauges. It does have an air conditioning system of questionable provenance (the control/vent unit doesn't appear to be either the factory Ford in-dash option available in 1967 nor the period-appropriate round-vented dealer accessory) as well as a modern single-DIN radio, but those are welcome and unobtrusive changes — they don't distract from the classic character of the car. The wheels certainly do, but some five-spoke American Classics will fix that handily.

Under the hood, though, is the mod that really matters: The Ranchero's factory 289ci V8 engine and its associated three-speed manual gearbox have been replaced with a 351 ci V8 and a four on the floor (with a Hurst shifter, even). It's not technically a period-correct swap, as the 351 postdates this Ranchero by a couple of years, but it's close enough that I'll count it. Better than putting a Coyote under there and pretending that the Ford badging maintains the car's character.

This OEM-plus Ranchero is on Bring a Trailer right now at a bidding price of just $2,500, which falls firmly into "Amber wants this" territory. I'm going to need you all to bump that number up until this ute starts to look like more of a bad idea to me, or Jalopnik's local kei car and motorcycle girlie might just end up with a giant hunk of American muscle street-parked outside her apartment. Surely you'll save me from that fate, right?

6 Native Alternatives to Butterfly Bush

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Butterfly bush (Buddleja davidii, Zones 5–9) might seem like an easy choice for a pollinator bed, given the name, but is it the right shrub for your garden? There are a couple of reasons you might want to think twice and consider native butterfly bush alternatives instead.

Originally from Asia, butterfly bush is a prolific bloomer that attracts pollinators like no tomorrow. Unfortunately, it is also a prolific seeder, easily spreading past its intended area and potentially outcompeting other plants. Even after the shrub is removed, its seeds can remain viable in the soil for years until conditions are favorable for germination.

Butterfly bush is listed as an invasive plant by several state and regional invasive-plant organizations across the Southeast. Although some cultivars on the market are advertised as sterile or seedless, their fertility varies, and some can still produce small amounts of viable seed. While low-fertility cultivars can reduce the risk of spreading, butterfly bush also lacks an important benefit of many native plants: It does not serve as a host plant for native butterfly caterpillars.

The good news is that if you’re open to planting butterfly bush alternatives, there are plenty of beautiful flowering shrubs native to the Southeast that have both high visual appeal and benefits for native pollinators, including butterflies. Many also provide food and habitat beyond their flowering season. Here are just a few to get started:

Blooming in late spring to early summer, false indigo bush produces distinctive spikes of purple flowers that attract bees, butterflies, and other pollinators. A wonderful nectar source for long-tongued pollinators and a host plant for several butterflies, including the silver-spotted skipper and gray hairstreak, it offers benefits besides its gorgeous blooms. Its tolerance of a range of moisture conditions makes it a versatile option for sunny garden spaces.

Virginia sweetspire produces fragrant white flower spikes in late spring, followed by colorful foliage in fall. Its adaptability to moist soils makes it an especially useful shrub for rain gardens and other sites where drainage can be a challenge. A nectar source for many bees and butterflies, it is also reported as a host plant for the American holly azure butterfly in the coastal Southeast.

Its fragrant flowers attract butterflies, hummingbirds, and long-tongued bees. Blooming in late summer, when some other shrubs have finished flowering, summersweet offers an additional nectar source later in the growing season. Summersweet is also worth thinking about for gardens with consistently moist soil. Although it tolerates full sun, it generally needs more moisture in sunnier locations.

This deciduous native azalea brings soft pink to white flowers to woodland gardens in mid-spring, often before its foliage has fully emerged. An excellent nectar source for many pollinators, including hummingbirds and butterflies, pinxterbloom azalea is particularly well suited to partially shaded garden beds with acidic soil.

Spicebush blooms in early spring, producing small yellow flowers before its leaves emerge. Its early flowers also provide nectar for pollinators, while the bright red fall fruits on female plants, when a male plant is nearby for pollination, offer food for birds. A host plant for the spicebush swallowtail, this native shrub also brings pretty yellow foliage to the fall landscape.

Flame azalea earns its name from its showy flowers, which bloom in shades of yellow, orange, and red from late spring to early summer. A terrific general nectar source for many pollinators, particularly butterflies, this deciduous shrub adds striking seasonal color to woodland gardens and other partially shaded spaces.

If you’re only interested in the “butterfly” aspect of butterfly bush and not the “bush” aspect, choosing a great plant becomes even easier. There is an entire world of pollinator plants to explore, from host plants for the Southeast to nectar-rich native perennials and annuals. By incorporating both nectar plants for adults and host plants for caterpillars, you can create a garden that supports butterflies throughout their life cycle.

How to Make Sure an 'Onion' Site on the Dark Web Is Legitimate

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

The dark web isn't just for illicit activities. In fact, many legitimate online platforms maintain a ".onion" domain on the dark web for privacy reasons, including media outlets like ProPublica and BBC News, social media sites like Facebook, and privacy-conscious communication platforms like Proton Mail and SecureDrop.

But this also provides perfect cover for criminals and hackers who create clones of legitimate dark web sites to steal sensitive data and cryptocurrency from privacy-conscious users. And given that onion links are long and jumbled, it’s easy for users to accidentally click on the wrong link and end up on a fake website instead of the real one. When the FBI seized 276 onion addresses during 2014's Operation Onymous, they found that 153 of them were phishing sites.

Thankfully, Tor Browser already has a cryptography-backed feature that can verify the authenticity of an onion site, by confirming whether it has end-to-end encryption and extended validation certificates. Here's how this feature works, where it falters, as well as what other red flags you should look for.

While phishing on the dark web works as it does on the clear web, scammers have a few unique advantages due to the secretive nature of the environment. Most importantly, you can’t just glance at an onion link to tell if the site is genuine. Normal website URLs tend to be short and memorable, while onion links are 56-character strings of alphanumeric text that look mostly like gibberish.

When you try to connect, Tor Browser verifies the URL against the originating website’s cryptographic key, which is how you know nobody has hijacked the address or tampered with it midway. But since this has the unwanted effect of making domains harder to recall, scammers have a much easier time generating lookalike addresses or creating website clones.

For example, many popular websites use “vanity prefixes” to make their onion addresses more easily identifiable or to add some branding appeal. That means their 56-character onion link starts with something like “facebook,” “bbcnews,” or “ciadotgov.” But scammers can use a free tool like mkp224o to generate lookalike addresses with the same vanity prefix as the legitimate website, which gives users a false sense of security.

Of course, most dark web users don’t type addresses directly into Tor Browser; they use dark web directories like the Hidden Wiki to find useful onion sites relevant to their search. Scammers will create fake search engines or link directories to point users to their fake websites, like when the Ahmia search engine discovered that a copy of its platform was directing users to 255 cloned services back in 2015.

Of course, it’s possible to orchestrate phishing attempts that don’t involve cloning sites at all. Researchers at Freie Universität Berlin describe a sophisticated technique where attackers serve the real website through their own reverse proxy, which lets them manipulate parts of the site or log your online activity without your awareness. In 2019, ESET even found a trojanized version of Tor Browser that replaced Bitcoin addresses on websites to direct payments to hackers. So both genuine websites and your browser itself are also vulnerable to manipulation if you download or access them from an unverified source.

There are a few best practices to avoid phishing attacks when using the dark web. None of them are foolproof on their own, but together, they considerably lower the risk of ending up on a fake or manipulated website.

The Tor Project has a feature called Onion-Location, which lets clear web websites advertise that they have a dark web version available for users who prefer more privacy. If opening a regular TLS-backed website using the Tor Browser reveals a purple “onion available” button in the address bar, you can click on it and be redirected to the official onion link associated with that site.

On the desktop version of Tor Browser, you can access an onion site using a shortened version of its URL called an Onion Name. For now, this feature only works with SecureDrop, so newsrooms can establish tip lines with addresses like mysitename.securedrop.tor.onion, which would then redirect to the 56-character original onion address. This feature is in active development, so more platforms might support it in the future.

When you load up an onion site using the Tor Browser, you’ll see an onion icon on the address bar, which confirms that your connection is end-to-end encrypted. But you can also click on the onion icon to check if the site has an Extended Validation (EV) certificate, which tells you that its authenticity has been verified by a trusted certificate authority like DigiCert. While not every legitimate onion site will have an EV certificate, famous ones like Facebook tend to.

On the other hand, if you see a caution sign next to the onion icon, that means the site is using a self-signed or expired certificate that isn’t guaranteed by a trusted authority. It may also imply that the site is being served over an insecure URL. This doesn’t definitely tell you it’s a phishing site, but it’s a red flag to watch out for nevertheless.

Don’t rely on search engines, link directories, Reddit threads, or Telegram channels too heavily on the dark web. Once you’re sure you’ve found the onion address for a genuine site, bookmark it so you can access it directly the next time you want to visit.

If you realized that you’ve been the victim of a phishing attempt, the first step is to close the browser window with the fake site and access the real onion address to reset any account passwords you may have accidentally divulged.

Also, generally try to enable two-factor authentication and PGP logins on any dark web account that supports it for added security. And if you downloaded Tor Browser from anywhere other than the official Tor Project site, uninstall it now and grab a fresh copy from the official address.

AI voice company WellSaid retools for agents while keeping its commitment to human actors

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Chronicling the Seattle and Pacific Northwest startup scene. SEE MORE

by Todd Bishop on Nov 11, 2026 at 2:56 pmNovember 11, 2026 at 3:19 pm

WellSaid, the AI voice company that spun out of Seattle’s Allen Institute for Artificial Intelligence in 2019, is reinventing itself for the era of AI agents.

The company this week launched WellSaid Producer, which connects its voiceover direction tools directly to AI assistants such as Claude and Microsoft Copilot.

Instead of logging into WellSaid’s app, corporate users can ask the AI assistant for a voiceover to accompany a training or marketing video, for example. The assistant then sends the script to WellSaid, which generates the audio using its licensed voices.

It’s part of a broader overhaul and refocusing of WellSaid’s business. The company has spent the past two years moving away from individual users to focus on businesses. It now has about 30 employees, down from more than 70 in late 2024.

Along the way, WellSaid has turned profitable, CEO Benjamin Dorr told GeekWire.

“We’ve been calling this adventure Act II, so I guess Producer is Scene I,” Dorr said. Extending the metaphor, he acknowledged that “the cast is a lot smaller,” and noted that many of the company’s leaders are new, recruited from his own network.

Dorr joined WellSaid as CFO in 2024 and became CEO in spring 2025. He was previously chief operating officer of Cordial, a San Diego marketing software company, and earlier worked in tech investing and banking at The Carlyle Group, Morgan Stanley and Citi.

He succeeded Brian Cook, the former Nintex CEO who led WellSaid for about 16 months. Co-founder Matt Hocking, who was CEO until early 2024, remains on WellSaid’s board and is now chief product officer at Type, an AI startup for creative writers.

Dorr’s leadership team includes three executives who also worked at Cordial and joined WellSaid since last fall: Natalia Holts, senior vice president of finance and accounting; Jeni Bishop, vice president of marketing; and Bryan Keefer, vice president of product.

Chris Johnson, the chief product and technology officer, and co-founder Mixail Petrochuk, the chief technology officer, remain from the earlier leadership team.

The company is maintaining its longstanding practice of building its AI voices from recordings of professional voice actors, who are paid royalties when businesses use their voices to narrate training courses, marketing and other videos.

WellSaid drew widespread attention in 2023 when it created a spot-on synthetic version of former NPR “Planet Money” host Robert Smith’s voice, with his permission, as part of a special series by the popular radio show and podcast.

Its customers include nearly half of the Fortune 100, according to WellSaid. The company’s licensing approach stands out in a sector that remains a wild west, with models trained on audio scraped from the internet and tools used to fake the voices of public figures.

“We view [Model Context Protocol], and agents in general, to be as disruptive to software as the Web once was twenty years ago.”

How it works: Producer uses Model Context Protocol, or MCP, a standard way for AI assistants to connect to other software. It works with Claude, ChatGPT, Copilot and Atlassian’s Rovo, and Dorr said AI agents that work on their own will be able to use it by the end of the year.

Companies using WellSaid Producer can set up style guides so brand names, acronyms and technical terms are pronounced the same way in every file. When a product name or script changes, Producer can update every affected voiceover in one pass, and each finished file carries a record of its license.

WellSaid also says it’s the first voiceover platform to generate audio natively at 48 kHz, the standard for film and video. WellSaid will continue to offer its Studio app for customers who want to work there directly.

Producer is sold as a platform, with customers committing to buy credits for minutes of finished, licensed audio. WellSaid’s licenses today are mostly one-time buyouts, but Dorr said the company wants to price more like the traditional voiceover industry, where an ad voiceover is priced by its length, reach and how long it runs.

The strategy: Dorr said voiceover is just one step in a larger production process, and many creative tools now include basic AI speech that is sometimes good enough. He sees AI agents letting companies combine specialized tools from vendors they trust, instead of settling for one all-in-one app.

“We view MCP, and agents in general, to be as disruptive to software as the Web once was twenty years ago,” he said.

Voice actors: Actors’ revenue share rates won’t change when their voices are used through AI assistants, Dorr said. One of his first moves as CEO was to open the company’s stock option pool to its top 20 voice actors, and WellSaid is building a voice actor advisory board, with more details expected to be released in December. Dorr said WellSaid plans to add custom royalty structures, “potentially all the way up to union rate cards.”

Customers will also be able to use voice models from other companies alongside WellSaid’s own, for features its models lack, such as one voice speaking several languages. The voices still come from WellSaid’s actors under its licensing terms. “We’re not moving from our position on commercial licensing,” Dorr said.

The business: WellSaid has about 1,000 business customers, and its top 25 customers each spend more than $100,000 a year, Dorr said. Moving away from individual users “did flatten our growth compared to a lot of AI companies growing at astronomical rates,” he said.

By comparison, rival ElevenLabs, which has offered voice generation through Claude since 2025, was valued at $22 billion in a recent employee tender offer.

WellSaid raised venture debt from Multiplier Capital in March but didn’t disclose the amount. Its most recent disclosed equity funding was a $10 million Series A round in 2021 that valued the company at $65 million, according to PitchBook.

Dorr said debt was a better fit than equity for a “modestly growing and, now, profitable company,” at a time when investors’ growth expectations “have become extreme.” He said WellSaid will consider other financing in 2027 “if our ambitions outstrip our ability to reinvest.”

<i><em>VisionQuest </em></i>Reveals Wanda Maximoff’s Return in <i><em>Avengers: Doomsday </em></i>and Hallelujah for That

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Elizabeth Olsen’s return is a major revelation.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

The following story contains spoilers for VisionQuest.

LIKE WandaVision AND Agatha All Along before it, VisionQuest turned out to be among the best Marvel projects to be released in the post-Endgame era. And given that the series was billed as the conclusion to the trilogy that those prior two shows set up, that should come as no surprise. While many Marvel Cinematic Universe movies and shows in the last five years have struggled to figure out what works and what doesn’t—and what manages to resonate with audiences over time—these shows, released over the course of a nearly six-year span, have clearly understood that the recipe for success is pretty simple: a slightly raised concept, and some characters people really love.

VisionQuest took a formula that felt like something of a hybrid of what made the other two shows successful and rode it. Where WandaVision had a super high concept (Wanda turning her world into a surreal series of sitcom references), returning stars (both Elizabeth Olsen and Paul Bettany led the show), and new characters people became obsessed with (Kathryn Hahn’s Agatha), Agatha All Along then introduced the idea of bringing in a vital new character in Wanda and Vision’s son Billy (Joe Locke) to go along with the returning Agatha.

VisionQuest splits the atom by pairing Bettany with a returning James Spader as Ultron (truly fantastic throughout) and introducing Ruaridh Mollica as Tommy, Wanda and Vision’s other superpowered son. The show’s consistently strong, surprising, and well-paced writing from showrunner Terry Matalas—to go along with fun new characters like the Paladin (Todd Stashwick) and E.D.I.T.H. (Emily Hampshire)—made it an easy, fun, watchable hit. And it helped that in the end, it came with a punch.

The show continued to expand on Vision becoming human, something Bettany has proven to be brilliant at during the course of his time in the MCU, but it also gave layers to Spader’s Ultron, proving that the franchise can allow some of its iconic villains to stick around and play in shades of moral gray.

By the end of the show, Vision, Tommy, and Billy have made it through the adventure, discovered themselves, and reunited as the family unit we’ve long known they were meant to be. There was just one lingering question: Where’s mom?

VisionQuest asks the question near the end. Elizabeth Olsen’s Wanda Maximoff was the most popular character (and briefly became one of the best villains) in the MCU in the aftermath of Avengers: Endgame; WandaVision was a massive smash, hitting during the peak of the COVID-19 pandemic and becoming the closest thing to water cooler talk material that anyone could get at the time. Olsen’s performance, nominated for an Emmy, was one of the most important reasons why.

She took a more sinister arc in the following year’s Doctor Strange in the Multiverse of Madness, and—spoiler alert—appeared to be “dead” at the end of the movie. Which puts a serious wrinkle into the possibility of her reuniting with her robotic husband and immaculately conceived sons.

But this is the Marvel Cinematic Universe, and we’re talking about stories based off of comic books. There’s always a way.

Of course she is! Wanda Maximoff is one of the Marvel Cinematic Universe’s most beloved characters, and Elizabeth Olsen’s performance as her is truly fantastic. You didn’t think Kevin Feige would leave her alone beneath a mountain, did you?

Sure, it’s been more than four years since we last saw the Scarlet Witch at the end of Doctor Strange in the Multiverse of Madness, but as the credits scene for VisionQuest (likely a moment taken right out of next month’s Avengers: Doomsday) shows us: She’s back. Doctor Doom (Robert Downey Jr.) picks up the bit of mountain she was crushed underneath, and her eyes rocket open in a Jon Snow–esque awakening. “Scarlet Witch, awaken,” Doom says. “Your power will be mine.”

It’s not hard to read into what’s going on here. It’s clear that in Doomsday, Doom is recruiting from some of our existing and most powerful Marvel heroes in an attempt to fuel his own takeover of the world. In the 2015 Secret Wars comic, Doom saves the universe from destruction after incursions (the collision of parallel universes) threaten to destroy everything; the result finds unexpected allyships as Doom takes his place as God Emperor. In that story, Doom’s second-in-command is Doctor Strange… someone we’ve already seen have quite the experience with Wanda (in Multiverse of Madness). Will they wind up allied once again in Doomsday?

The news of Wanda’s return also comes as we learn that her on-screen hubby will also be in Doomsday. “I got to hang out with Paul Bettany, who I love,” Anthony Mackie said in a recent interview, spilling some of the beans.

If VisionQuest was the conclusion of the WandaVision trilogy—and a darn good one—it sounds like Doomsday could feature a full-on Wanda and Vision reunion. And what Marvel fan who’s been along for this ride (and stuck it through to this point) wouldn’t be excited for that? Let’s get the family back together, shall we?

Lila Bio lands $3.65M grant to fight pulmonary hypertension with AI-designed proteins

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

by Lisa Stiffler on Nov 11, 2026 at 2:31 pmNovember 11, 2026 at 2:31 pm

Lila Bio, a startup spun out of Nobel laureate David Baker’s lab at the University of Washington, has received a $3.65 million grant from the National Institutes of Health to develop a treatment for pulmonary hypertension.

The condition causes high blood pressure in the arteries that carry blood from the heart to the lungs. It forces the heart to work harder and can lead to heart failure.

The Small Business Innovation Research fast-track award supports Lila’s efforts to develop a protein-based treatment for pulmonary hypertension that could be given by injection a few times a year.

“Most approved therapies only manage symptoms, such as vasodilators,” Lila CEO Jake Kraft said in a LinkedIn post, referring to drugs that widen blood vessels. “Lila Bio is pursuing clinically-validated, disease-modifying mechanisms that treat the underlying disease.”

The funding comes from the NIH’s National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute and is divided into two phases. The initial $605,981 will support early testing in mice, rats and non-human primates. The second phase, totaling $3,049,462, will support further animal testing and studies using lung tissue from patients.

Kraft and chief scientist Anindya Roy co-founded Lila in 2023. Both worked as postdoctoral fellows at UW’s Institute for Protein Design, which Baker directs. Lila raised $10 million in seed funding the year it was founded. Last year, it announced a collaboration with pharmaceutical giant Eli Lilly to develop treatments for solid tumors.

The startup uses AI and machine learning to design therapeutic proteins through two platforms. One supports the pulmonary hypertension project, creating long-acting injectable drugs designed to remain active in the body for weeks or months. The other focuses on targeted radiotherapy, using proteins to deliver radioactive isotopes to tumors.

“We want to bring medicines to patients,” Kraft told GeekWire in an earlier interview. “We don’t want to just say we’ve designed a protein, but we actually want to be able to say we’ve saved lives.”

Lauren Graham's 2026 Colleen Hoover Movie Is Officially Netflix's Biggest Streaming Hit

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Is there anyone more representative of the recent romance novel adaptation trend than Colleen Hoover? Hollywood loves a BookTok phenomenon, and Hoover's novels seem designed in a lab to appeal to the "crying, screaming, throwing up" crowds on social media, who simply cannot resist what The Guardian called Hoover's "normcore thrillers."

But the film also has some other things working in its favor. For one it stars the talented Maika Monroe, and it also features Lauren Graham, who's best known for playing Lorelai Gilmore on "Gilmore Girls." Graham does a decent job with the material in "Reminders of Him," and regardless of how you feel about the film as a whole, it's nice to see her in a modern-day melodrama.

"Reminders of Him" is directed by British director Vanessa Caswill, who oversaw Netflix's 2023 romantic comedy "Love at First Sight," after honing her chops on BBC series such as "Little Women," "Gold Digger," and "Thirteen." This was another romance movie based on a popular book, and wouldn't you know, the movie went straight to the top of the Netflix charts upon its release. Now, Caswill has done it again with "Reminders of Him," though this one had a successful box office run under its belt before taking over Netflix.

The film stars Maika Monroe as Kenna Rowan, who made a tragic mistake years earlier that landed her in prison. After serving her sentence, Kenna returns to her Wyoming hometown, where she hopes to finally meet her young daughter, Diem Landry (Zoe Kosovic). Sadly, Diem's grandparents Grace and Patrick Landry (Lauren Graham and Bradley Whitford) are completely opposed to the idea. But there's also a strapping young bar owner standing between her and her daughter in the form of Ledger Ward (Tyriq Withers). Turns out Ledger has been helping take care of Diem, and he's similarly concerned about Kenna's return. But as the two get to know each other, romance blossoms, and Kenna finally gets a chance at redemption.

"Reminders of Him" hit Netflix U.S. on November 10, 2026, and according to streaming viewership tracker FlixPatrol, became an instant hit. The film shot to number one on November 11, 2026, proving these Colleen Hoover adaptations remain perennially popular. No doubt we'll see plenty more Hoover books being adapted in the near future.

There are plenty of movies to avoid on Netflix, and "Reminders of Him" is right on the borderline. The film managed a 56% critic score on Rotten Tomatoes, which is higher than any other Colleen Hoover adaptation. But it also has an 87% audience score, which ties it with "It Ends With Us" for most popular among viewers on the site. Now it has managed to earn the endorsement of Netflixers, does this mean you should watch it?

Well, the movie has a few plus points. For one, it stars the Maika Monroe, who's able to shift from scream queen to swoon queen with ease. Meanwhile, Lauren Graham is given some of the more emotional scenes and does a solid job of bringing them to life. Otherwise, it's sort of okay for the first half? Monroe and her co-star, Tyriq Withers, manage to wring some believable emotion out of the script. Once they throw caution to the wind and just go for it, despite their previous reluctance to besmirch Monroe's deceased husband, everything starts to get a little ridiculous.

Joey Shapiro summed things up quite aptly in his review for the Chicago Reader, when he called "Reminders of Him" a "corny TV movie" that's "made with so much sincerity and conviction by all involved that it becomes watchable, if rarely 'good' by any stretch." If that appeals, or if you simply can't get enough of these romance movies that verge on providing some sort of interesting insight into human emotion only to fall short, then why not give this lesser-known Hoover adaptation a go.

Talking Zen 6 with Mike Clark at AMD In-Depth

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Hello you fine Internet folks,I sat down with AMD’s Mike Clark to talk about Zen 6, the evolution from Zen 5 and what some of the differences are between Zen 6 and Zen 6c and what drove some of those changes.

The transcript below has been edited for readability and conciseness.

George Cozma: Hello, you fine Internet folks. We’re here at AMD In-Depth, where we’re talking about Venice. With me, I have a returning guest, Mike Clark. Would you like to tell the audience who you are?

Mike Clark: Hi, I’m Mike Clark, the original architect of Zen. Some people refer to me as “Zen Daddy.” I’ve been working on cores here at AMD for 33 years, and we’ve got another great one coming, so I’m very proud of the design team I get to represent here today.

George Cozma: I’ve had some hands-on time with Zen 6c. Let’s talk about the change that’s happened with Zen 6 and Zen 6c, what I refer to in my piece as the Zen 6 family. Why is it different from previous generations?

Mike Clark: Right. Starting with Zen 4, we’ve been going through this evolution of having two core families. Before, we were really just using the denser technology as an area play. In this generation, having learned a lot, as we were filling out that denser version of the core, we wanted to leverage it to add some extra IPC within the core. So if you look more in-depth at what we have, what used to be our classic design, we now think of as our high-frequency design. And our dense technology is now our top-of-the-line, no-questions-asked performance core, because we’ve also enabled the full L3 in that implementation, at 4MB per core. So we have 32 of those cores, 128MB of L3, and the highest IPC you can get in that core.

George Cozma: Awesome. So far, one thing I’ve noticed is that the vector scheduler is a bit bigger on Zen 6c. What was the reasoning for increasing that on Zen 6c?

Mike Clark: Well, that wasn’t the only thing we increased. One principle we follow is that we don’t want to create a delta between the two architectures that software has to be optimized for. We want them to have the same fit and feel, so the same optimizations apply to both. But because of its reduced frequency target, Zen 6c can have a little more capacity in the queues, since it doesn’t have to reach the maximum frequency the high-frequency core has to hit. The floating-point scheduler is just one example. There are other queues throughout the core that are larger in Zen 6c, to pull out a little more IPC and get a little more leverage in that SMT environment as well.

George Cozma: Interesting. Both Zen 6 and Zen 6c build on the design you had in Zen 5, and one of the interesting things with Zen 5 was the dual decode setup. Has the front end changed much to let a single thread make better use of both decode clusters?

Mike Clark: Yes. As you said, Zen 5 was a new foundation, with the ability to fetch two basic blocks and get them down the line. But it gets quite complicated with one thread running larger-footprint workloads, where some of it might be hitting the I-cache and some of it might be hitting the op cache, and you’re trying to get everything synced up. So we spent a lot of time optimizing that and making it more efficient from both a delivery and a power perspective: less power wasted, and more efficient delivery from that new front end.

George Cozma: Awesome. Going down into the execution engine, I also noticed that the number of integer multiply units has gone down from three to two. Could you talk a bit about what drove that choice?

Mike Clark: It really came out of the Zen 5 design, where we had a more unified scheduler. To make a scheduler simpler, you want the units it schedules into to be very uniform, and the way we had set up those schedulers, adding a third multiplier to the group it was scheduling into was easy and didn’t add complication. In Zen 6, we realized that to really scale out the architecture, we needed to go back to more distributed schedulers, which now schedule into the ALUs. We hadn’t seen much uptick from that extra multiplier on Zen 5, so to optimize the area and the schedulers, we went back to two multipliers.

George Cozma: Speaking of integer multiply, on the vector side you’ve added two more VNNI units. What was the impetus for adding them, and at what point in the design did you add them?

Mike Clark: As I said in the talk, these are long design programs. We weren’t designing Zen 6 to be an agentic core. When we started, no one was really even using that term. But Zen 5 was a whole new foundation for AI workloads, and we’ve always believed the core has a place in AI: orchestration, data type manipulation, smaller models. So we were always focused on making the core good at AI. It’s not going to beat a GPU, and that’s not the point. We realized we probably should have some more VNNI, given where we were seeing inference go, and the team rallied around that. It was kind of late, since these are long-lead projects and the floor plan was set, but the team rallied, we were able to squeeze a couple more VNNI pipes in there, and it was a big success.

George Cozma: Again on the vector side, one of the oddities of Zen 5 was that although the wires were all there, the load unit could only do two vector loads at a time. What changed within the Zen 6 core to allow four vector loads, assuming they’re either 128 or 256 bits?

Mike Clark: We can do the 256-bit loads. Since we were able to do two 512-bit loads, we already had 1,024 wires headed over there. Obviously, using them piecemeal versus as one contiguous set complicates the design. I think it’s something we always wanted to do on Zen 5, but with all the change and getting it working, we left that for the Zen 6 team, and they were awesome. We’re all really one team at AMD anyway, and sometimes things intersect at different points in the roadmap. This was the time to do it.

George Cozma: Speaking of bandwidth into the caches, the L2 in Zen 5 had some oddities where it couldn’t always hit 64 bytes per cycle on reads. What changed in Zen 6 to allow that? Because I’ve been able to measure it, and it does hit that.

Mike Clark: Honestly, sometimes you just don’t see some of these things. A lot of it was that we would be over-prefetching, and prefetches that hit in the L2 would be robbing L2 bandwidth. So we made sure we could sustain those prefetches alongside demand requests, reduce the conflicts, and deliver the full 64 bytes per cycle that we want.

George Cozma: Awesome. Circling all the way back to the front: the all-important branch predictor. There seems to have been a reduction in branch misprediction latency, so when you miss a branch, the number of cycles it takes to recover has gone down. What I also noticed, which is really neat, is that there wasn’t any reduction in accuracy. So how did you pull off not only a faster branch predictor, but one that’s even slightly more accurate than Zen 5’s already very accurate predictor?

Mike Clark: The branch prediction team, as you know, is very algorithmic. There’s lots of design space and lots of exploration, and AI has even been getting involved in helping us figure out how to optimize it. It’s a great optimization problem: the number of TAGE tables, how much information they hold, accuracy. So we set out to make a more efficient one. That was an area where we didn’t really want to grow; we thought we could be more efficient in the way we lay out the tables for prediction. And we’re always focused on our pipeline length. No matter how good a branch predictor you build, there are always hard-to-predict branches, and you’re always going to mispredict. So each generation, we’re monomaniacally focused on getting going again as fast as we can when we’re wrong. There are lots of unique tricks we pull to get that redirect back into the front end and get it flowing as fast as possible. And you’ve observed it, which is great.

George Cozma: Yeah. I haven’t had time on Zen 6 yet, but I have had time on Zen 6c, and in SPECint 2026 it matches your fastest Zen 5 server CPU. That’s very impressive, especially considering the clock deficit. Wrapping this all up and moving outside the core into the L3, there are obviously topology differences between the 12-core and 32-core CCDs. How much were you able to mitigate those differences inside the core, building up structures to tolerate latency, to compensate for the slightly increased L3 latency?

Mike Clark: As I always talk about, the design has to be balanced. As we grow the capacity of a structure, the new technology helps it shrink, so the physical distance doesn’t grow linearly, but unfortunately the distance does grow. Inside the L3, there are a lot of tricks I can’t talk about...

Mike Clark: [In order to] maintain bandwidth within the L3 mesh so we can keep delivering to the cores. And in the cores, we do grow the queues to handle those higher latencies.

George Cozma: Awesome. My penultimate question: Verano is adding LPDDR5X to the EPYC ecosystem. Were there any design considerations between LPDDR5X and DDR5 that you had to think about when designing the core?

Mike Clark: At the CCX level, we focus a lot on bandwidth per core. I think Mahesh talked a lot about the SDP ports, with 32-byte reads per port, so we try to make sure we have enough bandwidth between the CCD and the IOD. But we were already well provisioned for bandwidth with MRDIMMs, so that construction, the winglets and choosing a different memory technology, didn’t really have a major impact on how we built the core.

George Cozma: Awesome. Usually this is where I’d ask what your favorite type of cheese is, but since we already know that: have you tried any new cheeses lately?

Mike Clark: I have. My new favorite is Cotija cheese.

Mike Clark: I’ve been eating a lot of tacos.

Mike Clark: Thanks for having me, George. I always look forward to coming and talking with you about what this awesome AMD CPU design team has created, and you always have so many good questions. I wish I could answer more, since we do have to protect our secret sauce, but I really enjoy helping you understand some of our thought processes, the challenges we face, and how we’ve been able to overcome them.

George Cozma: Well, it’s always fun to sit down with you and talk, not just about what you’re doing, but also what the team is doing and what you’re looking forward to in the future. So thank you so much.

George Cozma: I’ll see you in the next one. Have a good one, folks!

BYD shows what its new ‘Cloud Carriage’ intelligent body control system can really do

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

BYD’s new EV may be about the size of a Toyota Camry, but it’s playing in a whole different league when it comes to technology.

BYD launched the second-generation Seal 07 last week, calling it “the new choice for a class-leading luxury experience.”

It’s available in battery electric (EV) and plug-in hybrid (PHEV) powertrains, with prices starting at 139,900 yuan (about $21,000) for the plug-in hybrid and 149,900 yuan (around $22,500) for the fully electric version.

Both versions measure 5,080 mm long, 1,960 mm wide, and 1,495 mm high, with a 2,960 mm wheelbase, which is slightly longer than the Toyota Camry.

Don’t let the price fool you, however, the vehicle is packed with BYD’s latest breakthrough technology.

The new Seal 07 EV uses a 76.7 kWh second-generation BYD Blade LFP battery that provides a CLTC range of up to 481 miles (775 km).

Thanks to the second-gen Blade Batteries and BYD’s Flash Charging technology, the vehicle’s battery can charge from 10% to 70% in just five minutes. A full recharge, or going from 10% to 97%, takes just nine minutes.

The Seal 07 is also BYD’s first vehicle to debut with its new Disus-M Intelligent Magnetorheological Body Control System.

The setup features a front double-wishbone suspension and an enhanced rear five-link suspension. It works alongside BYD’s road-surface sensors that use LiDAR, plus its iTAC 2.0 Intelligent Torque Adaptation System.

Zhang Zhuo, the general manager of BYD’s Ocean sales division, offered a better look at how the system works in real life in the video shown above.

BYD said the system provides ultra-fast response times down to the millisecond. The advanced suspension system adjusts damping force across a wide range of 100 to 10,000 Nm.

It’s equipped on the all-electric Flagship Seal 07 variant, while lower-priced versions have the Disus-C intelligent body control system. The top-spec EV model with the Disus-M setup starts at 172,900 yuan ($26,000). Still, not too bad for what you get.

Inside, the new Seal 07 adopts BYD’s new interior design, including a 15.6″ floating center infotainment screen, a 10.25″ instrument cluster, and two rear passenger entertainment screens.

BYD added a few premium extras, including a built-in 6.8L smart refrigerator, zero gravity seats, and driver headrest audio.

Quite impressive for sure. What's more impressive is that GM/Cadillac first offered magnetic ride control back in 2002 (a full 24 years ago!!). It may not have been quite as advanced as this system in 2026, but still one of the most advanced suspension systems offered to date for a line of vehicles.

I really want this car!! I truly hope BYD will be able to sell this in the USA soon.

The Seal 07 also debuts BYD’s Smart Ecosystem 2.0, including “Diva,” a smartwatch that lets drivers interact with the vehicle and manage functions remotely.

Higher-priced versions are available with BYD’s God’s Eye B intelligent driving system, which includes a roof-mounted LiDAR. The setup provides safety and convenience features like Navigation on Autopilot in the city and on highways, plus full-scenario intelligent parking.

For a limited time, BYD is offering a “Super trade-in price” special, starting at 145,900 yuan ($22,000) for the new Seal 07 EV and 135,900 yuan ($20,500) for the PHEV version.

Scoop: Frontier Airlines Plans First Class Service—Not Just Bigger Seats

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Frontier Airlines is planning more than a bigger seat for first class. A new memo tells flight attendants that first class service standards are coming for flights beginning March 5, 2027, the same day its new front cabin launches. This follows my original scoop, since confirmed by the airline, that Frontier’s first class rolls out March 5, 2027. Under “Onboard service,” the airline now says: Our standard onboard services apply through March 4, 2027. New First Class service standards will be released for flights on and after March 5, 2027. This is a move toward a real first class service, and away from the Spirit Airlines ‘Big Front Seat’ approach to just selling more space. This hasn’t been announced publicly yet. The airline’s first class frequently asked questions, updated just yesterday, still says food and beverages are available for purchase. Specific standards are still to come. Frontier Has Confirmed The March 5 Launch Frontier originally announced first class in December 2024 for late 2025. Last month I reported that aircraft conversions were beginning, with the bigger seats sold as UpFront Plus during the transition. So getting the seat before the March 5 official launch doesn’t mean getting new service with it. Frontier publicly announced first class reservations on November 10, confirming March 5 travel and targeting that date for completion of the seating conversion: Eight first class seats: two rows in a 2-by-2 configuration, with up to 37 inches of pitch. More extra legroom: rows three through eight will offer up to 34 inches of pitch. UpFront Plus, with its blocked middle seat, continues in select rows. A First bundle advertised from $129 one way: first class seating, a carry-on, two checked bags and priority boarding. A Premium bundle advertised from $90 one way: extra legroom seating, a carry-on and priority boarding. The introductory offer requires buying the flight and bundle together by November 16, for select nonstop domestic departures March 5–April 5, 2027. And these seats still don’t recline. Frontier describes them as “pre-reclined,” which means you can’t adjust the seatback. Power And Wi-Fi Are Coming Frontier now says USB-C charging will be offered in the first eight rows, not throughout the full coach cabin. Both power and Starlink wi-fi will roll out during 2027. Frontier’s new renderings show the charging setup: I’ve said that the lack of inflight internet has kept me from flying Frontier. Giving up hours of work can make the cheapest ticket actually more expensive. A bigger seat, power and working wi-fi make a big difference in my willingness to buy travel from them. Their operational reliability, however, is still a caution. Including drinks and food makes sense as it tries to have a product customers interested in a better experience will buy. They’ve had largely the cheap offerings but not the revenue opportunities from further upsells. It’s still not clear what the service standards will mean or translate into in the cabin. Unfortunately, for bookings made starting January 1, 2027, elite frequent flyers will only be eligible for complimentary upgrades to first class if they buy a bundle. And top tier Diamonds only qualify 24 hours prior to departure (18 for Platinum, 8 for Gold, and 4 for Silver). N'Guessan buteur contre la Norvège

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Sous contrat avec l'ASSE jusqu'en 2028 et prêté actuellement au Stade Brestois, Djylian N'Guessan (18 ans) n'a joué que 28 minutes (hors temps additionnel) cette saison de Ligue 1 avec les Ti Zefs.

Mais le natif de Saint-Nazaire a retrouvé du temps de jeu en début de soirée à Pabu (Côtes d'Armor) lors du 2e tour éliminatoore de l'Euro U19. Titularisé par Jean-Luc Vannuchi contre la Norvège pour sa première apparition en équipe de France U19, il a marqué d'une tête piquée le 5e des 6 buts de l'écrasant succès (6-0) de sa sélection avant de céder sa place à l'entame du dernier quart d'heure.

C'est son 24e pion claqué sous le maillot bleu en 33 matches joués pour son pays (il avait scoré 12 fois en U16 et 11 fois en U17). S'il n'a pas encore ouvert son compteur buts contre un club pro (il n'a trompé que le gardien de Quetigny), le joueur valorisé à 5 M€ par Transfermarkt aura l'occasion de faire gonfler ses stats en sélection samedi contre la République d'Irlande et mardi prochain contre la Grèce.
