Yes, My Toddler Sleeps In Our Room. No, We Haven't Stopped Having Sex.

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Before you judge me, I asked experts what young kids actually understand and if our obsession with sexual privacy is more cultural and modern than we think.

My 3-year-old still sleeps in bed with my husband and me. I like cuddling him all night, and I’ve come to accept that it’s just the kind of mom I am: the clingy co-sleeping type. When he was littler, we took safe-sleep precautions with a bassinet and all that jazz, but these days, he sleeps right up next to me. Most non-co-sleeping families cringe at this. And then they immediately want to know: So how do you get intimate with your partner?

To which I say: We manage just fine, thanks. But lately, I’ve been wondering about the elephant in the room. Or, should I say, the toddler.

My husband and I NEVER have sex in the bed with our toddler. But we do sneak into the bathroom. Or sometimes, once he’s deeply asleep, we pull out a cot on the other side of the room and get it on in the dark. I’m embarrassed even admitting that.

I also don’t believe we’re the only parents who have ever found themselves in this predicament.

Here’s the thing... our house isn’t huge. And between the 3-year-old sleeping soundly in our blacked-out bedroom and one of our much more aware older kids wandering out of their rooms, we’ve chosen the former.

Which brings me to something else we really need to address. What about the “olden days”? Ma and Pa from Little House on the Prairie managed to have four babies while living in one-room log cabins and shanties. I guarantee they weren’t heading out to the barn or the outhouse every time they wanted to have sex, especially when bears and wolves were roaming around, and temperatures dipped below zero.

Besides, many modern families around the world still live and sleep in shared spaces.

Lately I’ve wondered if being horrified by the idea of parents having sex in the same room as a sleeping child is a cultural thing. Is it so awful if they’re still very little? At what point does “they have no idea what’s happening” turn into “Mom and Dad need to find somewhere else to do this”?

We reached out to experts to answer all the questions you’ve probably been dying to know but have been way too embarrassed to ask.

Here’s the short answer: Parents don’t need to feel guilty about having sex in the same room as a newborn or young infant. An infant doesn’t have the “cognitive capacity to understand what sex is,” and they won’t remember or understand what’s going on, says Kimberly Resnick Anderson, LCSW, an AASECT-certified sex therapist.

Anderson does add one caveat, though. Having a baby nearby is likely to make parents more reserved and overall less expressive during sex. Over time, she says, that could potentially lead to a “more permanent shift” in how they express themselves sexually.

When does she recommend finding some privacy? Around age 1.

Once a child develops language and becomes mobile, she says, it becomes harder to get it on, knowing the child could wake up in the middle of it.

But Samantha Ridout, M.Phil., BCBA, a child development and behavioral expert, cautions against treating any birthday as a universal cutoff. No two children develop at exactly the same rate, she says, so there isn’t a magical age when a child suddenly becomes aware of what’s happening around them. Instead, parents should watch for their child’s growing alertness and awareness and set appropriate boundaries.

I’ve found this to be true in my own family. We moved our older two kids out of our room quicker because they were definitely more aware than our last, who just feels more “babyish.”

But both experts made one thing very clear: During sex, infants need to be in a separate, safe sleep space.

Let’s go back to my Ma and Pa observation for a minute, shall we?

The idea of children and adults having separate bedrooms is a relatively new phenomenon, says Macall Gordon, a researcher and sleep coach with an undergraduate degree in anthropology who studies children’s sleep through a sociohistorical lens. She says before the lightbulb was invented, family life revolved around sunrise and sunset, with families often sleeping in shared spaces.

But with electricity, adults could suddenly stay up after children went to bed, she adds. Now, children needed a “separate space to sleep” while adults stayed up. So the nursery and the crib came along. Then, she says, the layout of homes started to change, too, to include hallways connecting separate rooms. Before that, rooms were all connected to each other, so you had to go through one to get to another.

To this day, however, a separate bedroom is still something of a privilege. Many families simply don’t have “extra, separate rooms,” Gordon points out, especially in big cities, where studios and lofts are common, or in cultures where extended family members may occupy additional bedrooms. “While we think of a separate room for children as the norm, it really wasn’t for most of our history,” Gordon shares.

Tiffany Belanger, cosleeping expert and founder of Cosleepy, says the topic tends to divide her followers into two camps: those who will have sex in the room with a kid and those who won’t. When I described my scenario to her, she responded that floor sex while young kids are still sleeping in their parents’ bed is actually pretty common among the co-sleeping families she hears from.

You want to know if mine has woken up before.

But rarely. He’s a really deep sleeper, and it’s only happened a few times. It’s dark, and when he did wake up, he immediately called out for me in that disoriented, half-asleep way toddlers do. We stopped immediately. The cot is also on the far side of the bed, outside his line of sight. As far as I know, he never saw or heard anything.

Will we emotionally damage our kid if he were to catch us in the act? Unlikely, Ridout says, if parents stay calm, stop immediately, tell the child they are safe, and answer their questions in a simple, non-panicky way.

But all of this still leaves me with the extremely practical question that got us into this mess in the first place.

Parents have to get creative, flexible, and spontaneous, says Anderson. That might mean asking family or friends to babysit when you can, doing it in other rooms, or using whatever short pockets of privacy you can find.

And while Belanger reassures me that my floor-sex solution isn't unheard of among co-sleeping parents, the other experts think it’s not a great option when kids become mobile and verbal. So, I suppose it might be time to retire the cot. Where to next?

The Ice Giants' Magnetic Fields are Very Strange Compared to Earth's

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Most planets appear to have magnetic fields, even if they're only weak, remnant fields. Earth has a strong field generated by its internal geodynamo. The other rocky planets have magnetic fields of varying strengths, with the possible exception of Venus. The outer gas planets, Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, and Neptune, all have strong dynamos.

But the magnetic fields generated by the ice giants Uranus and Neptune have some strange properties.

Earth's molten core and internal convection generate its magnetic fields. But the ice giants' magnetic fields are likely generated by fluids that are electrically conductive. These are water, ammonia, and methane ices under extreme pressure and temperatures that alter them and turn them into ionic and superionic phases. Not only is their electrical conductivity at play, but so is convection and the planets' rotations.

But beyond what generates them, they also have other differences. Earth's magnetic field has well-defined north and south poles that drift a little over time, and even flip every couple of hundred thousand years or so. But Voyager 2 showed us how different Uranus and Neptune are. Their fields are in disarray compared to Earth's. They're tilted and multi-peaked.

Uranus' magnetic field doesn't originate in its center, and is tilted almost 60 degrees from its axis of rotation. It's overall geometry is strange, and the magnetic field across its surface is asymmetrical in strength.

Neptune's magnetic field is similarly strange, but weaker. Like Uranus' it's tilted from its rotational axis, by about 47 degrees. It's also offset from the planet's physical center like Uranus'.

The most critical difference between the magnetic fields of Earth and those of Uranus and Neptune is that the fields around the ice giants experience shorter-term variability. In short, they're a weird mess.

While planetary scientists know that these fields are generated inside the planets, they've had to make some guesses as to why they're so strange. New research in Nature Communications Physics has made some progress understanding the pair of ice giants and their strange magnetic fields. It's titled "Electrical conductivity structure drives anomalous and potentially time-varying magnetic fields at Uranus and Neptune," and the lead author is Chi Yan. Yan is a research associate at the University of Texax Jackson School of Geosciences.

"Uranus and Neptune exhibit magnetic fields that are non-dipolar and non-axisymmetric, unlike those of any other known planet," the researchers write. "These fields are generated by fluid motions in electrically conducting planetary interiors via dynamo action, offering a window into otherwise inaccessible deep processes."

As we often say at Universe Today, understanding the oddballs and outliers in a group of objects sheds light on how processes operate across the broader group they're a part of. This is true of the ice giants and their magnetic fields. "Understanding why Uranus and Neptune have such unusual magnetic fields provides critical insight into dynamo processes across planets and stars," the authors explain.

This work is based on modelled dynamos that are controlled by interior composition and temperature. They assumed that electrical conductivity increases with depth. The authors say they "produce large-scale magnetic features consistent with the observed fields of Uranus and Neptune..."

This schematic shows the different interior layouts used in the models. a through c show radially variable electrical conductivity profiles for dynamo models with convective shell geometries. d through f show the associated shell geometry for each of a through c. Image Credit: Yan, C., Soderlund, K.M., Aurnou, J.M. et al. Electrical conductivity structure drives anomalous and potentially time-varying magnetic fields at Uranus and Neptune. Commun Phys 9, 336 (2026).

âDeep inside Uranus and Neptune, pressures and temperatures become extremely high,â lead author Yan said in a press release. âUnder these conditions, which can cause water molecules to break apart into ions, the material may become increasingly electrically conductive with depth.â

Voyager 2 gave us our only good close-up look at Uranus and Neptune. It likely caught those fields in a snapshot in time, and the model shows both planets' magnetic fields could shift and change markedly in only decades. The fields are most likely configured much differently than when Voyager 2 visited. This is much different than on Earth, where the magnetic north pole drifts between about 5 and 60 km per year, or about one degree every few decades.

"These non-dipolar dynamos exhibit substantial temporal variation, although the rate fluctuates and can be transiently slow," the authors write. They explain that changes in both intensity and orientation are a possibility in the decades since Voyager 2 flew past Uranus and Neptune, "though the extent of any such variation remains poorly constrained observationally as each planet has been measured in situ only once."

âEarthâs magnetic tilt drifts by only a degree or so over 50 years,â said Yan. âOur models show the ice giants can behave very differently. In some stretches the tilt swings by tens of degrees within decades. In others it shifts only slightly over the same span.â

This figure compares observations of Uranus' and Neptune's surface magnetic fields with the fields from models. The figure "demonstrates that the most Uranus-like snapshot from Model 6 (panel c) and the most Neptune-like snapshot from Model 11 (panel d) can change substantially on decadal timescales, with their close morphological resemblance to the respective observed fields (panels a and b) deteriorating markedly after 32 years (panels e and f)," the authors explain. Image Credit: Yan, C., Soderlund, K.M., Aurnou, J.M. et al. Electrical conductivity structure drives anomalous and potentially time-varying magnetic fields at Uranus and Neptune. Commun Phys 9, 336 (2026).

"We demonstrate that dynamo models incorporating an electrical conductivity that increases with depth can promote non-dipolar, non-axisymmetric magnetic fields," the authors write, including the M1 peak observed at Uranus and Neptune.

The M1 peak is an important, maybe the most important part, of Voyager 2's magnetic field observations. M=1 represents the lop-sided parts of a magnetic field. On Earth and other planets like Jupiter and Saturn, the magnetic field's power is dominated by symmetric or M=0 components. So for those planets, the M-1 components are at least an order of magnitude weaker than their M-0 components.

But that's not true on Uranus and Neptune. They show a stark contrast. They both have a very strong M-1 peak, the part that represents asymmetric fields. This means that they have dipoles, quadrupoles, and octupoles that are messy and skewed from the axis of rotation.

"Despite current observational and computational limitations, our results indicate that ice-giant-like dynamos can, in some intervals, undergo substantial magnetic field variations on decadal timescales that, if confirmed by future in situ or auroral measurements, could have significant impacts on the surrounding planetary magnetospheres," the authors write.

The JWST has observed the ice giant aurorae and the charged particles involved. These particles respond to the magnetic fields, and those observations could confirm if the magnetic fields have indeed shifted since Voyager 2 visited. Researchers are still working on those observations.

The image on the left is an enhanced-color image of Neptune from NASAâs Hubble Space Telescope. On the right, that image is combined with data from NASAâs James Webb Space Telescope. The cyan splotches are auroral activity captured with the JWST's Near-Infrared Spectrograph (NIRSpec). Image Credit: NASA, ESA, CSA, STScI, Heidi Hammel (AURA), Henrik Melin (Northumbria University), Leigh Fletcher (University of Leicester), Stefanie Milam (NASA-GSFC)

âWe are busy working on whatâs contained within these observations, but the shape and structure of the aurora, as well as other weird patterns in the ionosphere promises to provide us with a way of understanding how the magnetic fields have changed, an important stepping stone,â said Tom Stallard, a professor at Northumbria University who led the JWST observation campaign.

The researchers will combine the JWST data with their models and data from Voyager 2 and take their results as far as they can to try to determine the state of the magnetic fields today. But they're up against some limitations, limitations that can only be lifted by another mission to the ice giants.

There are no firm plans for a mission to the ice giants, but there are concepts. One is NASA's Uranus Orbiter & Probe that would study the planet and its moons, and even send a probe into Uranus' atmosphere. The Neptune Odyssey is another NASA concept. It would also consist of an orbiter and an atmospheric probe. The science community has recognized the importance of these missions, but they're just concepts at this point.

This research may extend to the issue of habitability, though probably only tangentially. Uranus' four major moons could all potentially have subsurface oceans under icy covers, though this is far from confirmed. If they do, then Uranus' magnetic field and related radiation belts could affect these bodies of water, their longevity, and their potential habitability.

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

After a Historic Electoral Rout, Trump Again Asks the Supreme Court to Greenlight His Ballroom

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

One day after the historic rout by the Democrats nationwide on Election Day a week ago, Trump took to the construction site of the sprawling ballroom he is constructing on the grave of the East Wing of the White House, which he abruptly razed in October 2025. “Trump invited reporters into a cavernous construction site and talked about the building project for nearly 20 minutes,” reported PBS, “without mentioning sweeping Republican defeats around the country the night before.”

The next day, Solicitor General D. John Sauer filed a petition asking the Supreme Court to greenlight the 90,000-square-foot construction project, including the 22,000-square-foot ballroom-cum-military complex. He asked the justices to rule on whether a district court, at the behest of the National Trust for Historic Preservation, erred in August in enjoining the ongoing “renovation” of the East Wing. The $400 million project has been the subject of heated debate and lawsuits since its inception. Sauer asks that the court reverse a lower court’s ruling outright or hear oral arguments, explains SCOTUSblog. Related Articles President Trump Moves to Make Oval Office a Public Monument Former Presidents Obama, Clinton Mock Trump's White House Interiors With Comparisons to 'Saddam Hussein' and 'Liberace's Bathroom'

The petition starts off by claiming the injunction was granted “at the behest of a single passerby claiming she will dislike how the new building looks.” That passerby was Alison K. Hoagland, a member of the board of the National Trust for Historic Preservation. The question of whether she has legal standing to sue is a central issue in the ongoing argument.

Sauer claims she has no standing, because the court has ruled that “mere offense, disagreement, or distaste does not qualify as a concrete and particularized injury.” He further calls the ballroom a “vital national-security effort taking place on the White House grounds with the President’s personal involvement,” arguing that it is necessary because the president faces “an onslaught of assassination attempts.” (There have been, counting generously, three.)

A consultation with the Secret Service and the National Parks Service, says the petition, determined that demolition and rebuilding would be the most cost-effective course of action and would be best “to ensure that the East Wing remains minimally visible from public spaces.”

The old structure couldn’t support the ballroom’s necessary security features, says the White House, including blast-proof windows, fortified walls, and missile-resistant columns. Nor could the underground military installation be built under the existing structure.

In April 2026, US District Court judge Richard J. Leon wrote a searing 35-page opinion, employing some 18 exclamation marks, rejecting Trump’s plan for the ballroom, saying that “the President of the United States is the steward of the White House for future generations of First Familes. He is not, however, the owner!”

The US Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia froze Leon’s order to allow the court time to consider the government’s appeal, outlines SCOTUSblog. A three-judge panel upheld Leon’s ruling in August but pushed its effective date back two weeks to allow the federal government to go to the Supreme Court. The majority wrote that only Congress has the power “to regulate the construction and demolition of White House structures.” A dissenting judge said Hoagland had no standing.

The Trump administration called Leon’s ruling “extraordinary and unlawful,” and Sauer asked the justices to pause the ruling, saying construction was “vitally required by national security” and that Hoagland’s “dislike” for the new construction doesn’t constitute a legal injury.

In August, a federal court ruled that Trump lacked the authority to build without Congressional approval, but the Supreme Court greenlighted the construction project in a stay issued by Chief Justice John Roberts; the Court ruled 5–4 for the White House on August 31. Roberts dissented and, along with Ketanji Brown Jackson, Elena Kagan, and Sonia Sotomayor, ruled that the construction “is likely unlawful” and that the majority decision was “no victory for the separation of powers” and that Hoagland did have standing.

In any event, there’s no turning back now, says Sauer, writing that the project is 70% complete, “and, in turn (sic), there remains no sound way now to redesign or unwind the massive superstructure that is currently standing at the White House.”

NASA kick-starts a fresh push to bring Mars samples home

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

China is making progress on its Tianwen-3 Mars Sample Return mission, which

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A long-term goal at NASA has been the ability to robotically haul back to Earth specimens from the Red Planet.

NASA is kickstarting a new initiative for its troubled Mars Sample Return (MSR) program, called the Perseverance Sample Retrieval Architecture, or PSRA in space shorthand. The plan is to establish a fresh NASA strategy for gathering Mars samples collected by the Perseverance rover and launching them back to Earth.

The PSRA initiative was noted on Nov. 9 by Tiffany Morgan, director of the NASA Mars Exploration Program (MEP), during the virtual meeting of the Mars Exploration Program Analysis Group (MEPAG).

Morgan noted that there will be a PSRA strategy team kickoff in January 2027 with a final report due in winter 2027-2028.

The backdrop to the PSRA activity is that there's one busy rover already on the Red Planet snagging and bagging samples.

NASA's Perseverance Mars rover remains on the prowl within Jezero Crater following its touchdown in February 2021. That car-sized robot has been grabbing select specimens from across the area and gingerly dropping the sealed pick-me-ups on the surface of Mars, as well as stuffing them inside the rover itself.

Those collectibles may well hold signs of past life on the enigmatic, dusty and foreboding Red Planet.

NASA and the European Space Agency have for years been intently plotting out plans to send future spacecraft to Mars and haul those Perseverance-plucked bits, pieces, and sniffs of atmosphere to Earth for rigorous inspection here on Earth using state-of-the-art equipment.

However, over the years, multiple reviews of a NASA Mars Sample Return (MSR) project by independent groups have reported sticker shock findings. Indeed, one last price tag came in at about $11 billion, with samples being returned to Earth in 2040. That finding was deemed by NASA itself as too costly and not doable within an acceptable time frame.

Yet another appraisal group ended up finding it feasible to return Mars samples as early as 2035 for a less costly ship and shoot cost of some $8 billion.

How the PSRA review deals with MSR cost and a multitude of other concerns is in the to-be-determined column.

In the first place, why are samples from Mars delivered by robotic means? Why not let a human expedition bring back the goods?

Also speaking at MEPAG was Bruce Jakosky of the Laboratory for Atmospheric and Space Physics at the University of Colorado Boulder and Department of Earth and Space Sciences at the University of Washington.

Jakosky called for an integrated approach to planning human missions to Mars. He highlighted recommendations stemming from a workshop in which he participated, held last May and organized by Explore Mars, Inc.

That report focused on the impact of MSR on reducing risk for human missions, rather than focusing on the science, Jakosky told Space.com.

"The purpose is to understand the risk to astronauts and to hardware. The risk includes that from compounds that are present in the soil that are hazardous to astronauts if breathed in," said Jakosky. "This includes perchlorates and chloromethanes that have been identified already, and detection of other species that might be present. It also includes dust that can be breathed in by astronauts and that can affect hardware wear and tear," he said.

During the MEPAG gathering, Jakosky flagged the importance of returning samples prior to human missions to Mars, such as:

Yet one more forcing factor for NASA in yet another relook at MSR is that China is making progress on its Tianwen-3 Mars Sample Return mission.

According to the China National Space Administration, the country's Tianwen-3 probe will have a lander, an ascender, a service capsule, an orbiter, and an Earth reentry module.

Tianwen-3 appears on track getting the trek underway around 2028 on two Long March-5 heavy-lift carrier rockets from the Wenchang Space Launch Site in China's southernmost island province of Hainan.

China's Tianwen-3 mission is dedicated to bring back to Earth roughly 500 grams of Red Planet collectibles in 2031.

He also wrote "Mars: Our Future on the Red Planet" released in 2016 by National Geographic. Leonard  has served as a correspondent for SpaceNews, Scientific American and Aerospace America for the AIAA. He has received many awards, including the first Ordway Award for Sustained Excellence in Spaceflight History in 2015 at the AAS Wernher von Braun Memorial Symposium. The Best Gifts Under $100 for Design Enthusiasts

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

A $100 budget opens up plenty of possibilities for thoughtful gifting, especially when you know where to look. It’s enough to find something genuinely well-designed, whether that’s a sculptural object for the home, an everyday essential with an unexpected twist, or a small indulgence someone might not think to buy for themselves.

The trick is finding gifts that offer something beyond their price tag. We’re drawn to pieces with a distinctive point of view, clever functionality, and materials or details that make them memorable. With that in mind, we’ve rounded up our favorite gifts under $100, from playful accessories and colorful tabletop finds to practical objects that make everyday routines a little more interesting.

Le Creuset brings a little holiday magic to its classic cocotte with an embossed Christmas tree lid and a gold-finished star knob. Crafted from durable glazed stoneware, the 24-ounce vessel moves easily from oven to table, making it ideal for individual pot pies, baked pasta, or warm desserts. Available in six colors, it’s a festive gift that’s just as practical as it is charming.

The Noël Mini Cocotte is available at williams-sonoma.com for $62.

Inspired by the pleated designs of Issey Miyake, Wooj’s Kinoko Lamp borrows the delicate look of folded paper without its fragility. Each mushroom-shaped shade is 3D printed from recycled plastic in Brooklyn, creating an accordion-like texture that softly diffuses light. Available in Amber, Smoke, Olive Oil, and Natural, it’s a small but striking addition to a desk, bookshelf, or bedside table.

The Kinoko Lamp is available at wooj.design for $95.

With its puffy, quilted silhouette and compact proportions, Calpak’s Luka Mini Duffel brings a playful edge to an everyday essential. The lightweight, water-resistant bag packs plenty of functionality into its design, including padded shoulder straps, dedicated bottle pockets, and a luggage sleeve with a hidden security compartment. Available in 18 colors, from understated neutrals to shimmering metallics, it’s a practical gift with plenty of personality.

The Luka Mini Duffel is available in select colors at calpaktravel.com for $98.

Chess has always been a game of strategy, but Printworks makes a case for keeping the board on display between matches. This contemporary take on the classic features wooden playing pieces, a sleek acrylic-topped board, and a coordinated storage box designed to look at home on a coffee table. Available in multiple colorways, it’s a gift that brings people together while doubling as decor.

The Classic Chess Set is available at printworksmarket.com for $79.

A little whimsy goes a long way when it comes to travel accessories. Antler’s Heritage Leather Aeroplane AirTag Holder takes the shape of a miniature airplane, crafted from scratch-resistant green leather with subtle stitched details. A discreet compartment keeps an Apple AirTag securely tucked away, while a leather lanyard attaches easily to luggage or a favorite travel bag. It’s a charming way to keep track of belongings without sacrificing style.

The Heritage Leather Aeroplane AirTag Holder is available at ⁠antler.com for $75.

Belgian jewelry designers Wouters & Hendrix bring a touch of surrealism to the tabletop with this trio of sculptural candleholders. Inspired by 1920s surrealist art, the hand-painted stoneware pieces combine graphic checkerboard patterns, contrasting colors, and three playful silhouettes. Arrange them together for an unexpected centerpiece or scatter them around a room for little moments of visual interest.

The Les Objets Mouleversants Candle Holder Set is available at lumens.com for $85.30.

Photography courtesy of Present & Correct

Originally created as teaching tools for Spanish classrooms, these solid beechwood geometric shapes have been in production since 1934. The 15-piece set comes in a wooden box with a hinged lid, inviting recipients to explore form, proportion, and composition through hands-on experimentation. Part educational tool, part sculptural desk accessory, it’s an unexpected gift for anyone fascinated by architecture, mathematics, or design.

The Wood Polyhedra Set is available at presentandcorrect.com in two sizes: large box ($82) and small box ($41).

What happens when a cardboard prototype becomes the finished product? Dutch designer Annebet Philips brings her playful, sketch-like aesthetic to this porcelain teapot, preserving the uneven edges and fold lines of the original cardboard model. Hand-painted black outlines give it an almost cartoonish quality, turning an everyday kitchen essential into a conversation piece.

The Carte Blanche Teapot is available at lumens.com for $98.

Designed with birds’ feeding habits in mind, this sculptural feeder takes its cues from traditional grain silos. Its cylindrical form accommodates birds that cling vertically while eating, while a clever filling mechanism helps keep seeds clean and dry. Made from upcycled plastic, the Finnish design comes in three earthy colors and brings a modern touch to outdoor spaces.

The Silo Bird Feeder is available at goodeeworld.com for $75.

Disguised as colorful coffee table books, Printworks’ jewelry boxes bring a little personality to everyday organization. Cloth-covered exteriors feature bold typography and playful color combinations, while padded interiors keep rings, earrings, necklaces, and other treasures neatly arranged. Available in a range of designs and sizes, they’re made to be displayed rather than tucked away in a drawer.

The Jewelry Boxes are available at printworksmarket.com starting at $49.

Inspired by traditional French bedside drinkware, Maison Balzac’s colorful glass carafe turns a practical necessity into a small design moment. The matching cup sits neatly over the opening when not in use, creating a sculptural silhouette that’s equally at home on a nightstand or desk. Made from dishwasher-safe borosilicate glass, the set comes in six colors and can even double as a vase.

The Bedside Carafe is available at comingsoonnewyork.com for $90.

German artist Marc David Spengler brings his graffiti-inspired compositions to the sofa with this colorful collaboration for Slowdown Studio. Playful abstract shapes give the 20-inch cotton pillow cover an artistic quality, while a soft, neutral reverse offers a quieter alternative. It’s a thoughtful gift for someone who appreciates original artwork in unexpected places. Pillow insert sold separately.

The Bessie Throw Pillow Cover is available at slowdownstudio.com for $80.

A rolling pin might not seem like an exciting gift, but this one makes a convincing case otherwise. Most Space Heaters Oscillate Side-to-Side. This Dreo Moves Air Upward for Better Coverage.

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

The powerful fan and rotating body poured out warmth in every direction.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

It’s been a bit chilly as the cold weather has begun to set in. In that chilliness, I’ve broken out the cable-knit sweaters and undershirts, but I can’t very well type in gloves. In order to beat the freeze in the cavernously large bullpen with high ceilings and large windows that is our office, I needed a space heater built for the moment. That space heater is the Dreo Whole Room.

The Dreo Whole Room space heater oscillates both horizontally and vertically to cover larger areas of space and better distribute heat throughout a room. At the same time, the device is also extremely powerful, blasting heat at three different temperature levels and fan speeds. Particularly for drafty rooms or larger spaces, this is a space heater that not only got my attention but helped get my blood flowing.

I loved the Dreo both for quick, on-demand heat as well as for more sustained use over several hours throughout the day. Even in the drafty office, there was a notable difference in temperature almost immediately, and it spread well beyond just my desk when oscillating.

The most notable feature on the Dreo Whole Room Heater is the dual oscillation. With both horizontal and vertical oscillation, the space heater is capable of spreading heat in more directions and bringing the temperature of a space up more holistically. This design choice prevents cold spots in a room and provides a more overall warmth so you don’t have to be in direct line of the heater in order to feel its effects.

In my own use, the feeling is noticeable. When left on and oscillating for a few minutes in a side room in our office, I walked back into a noticeable shift in temperature. The room was nearly as warm in the far corners of the space as it was within a three-foot radius around the heater.

While the oscillation is great for heating up full rooms, there’s still a lot of additional customization available to tinker with. The space heater comes with three different heat levels as well as three fan speeds so you can choose the intensity and strength of the air. While this is great for short bursts of time, I preferred using the temperature setting.

This setting allowed me to choose a specific temperature, and the Dreo would automatically run, slowing down as it neared it, and occasionally turning back on to maintain it. If you expect to use the space heater for extended periods of time, this mode makes it easier to both not overheat and save a bit of energy. That said, the Eco mode is your best bet for cutting down on energy costs.

Once I know that the heating power is powerful enough to be worth the money, the next concern is always safety (despite it probably deserving to be first). On this front, the Dreo Whole Room Heater is a solid choice.

The space heater not only has tip-over protection and a safety plug but also automatically shuts off if overheated and has a three-second child lock to prevent them from accidentally switching settings inadvertently. My only real issue with the child lock is that the three-second time might not be enough time to prevent a kid from unlocking the features and making changes.

As far as a space heater goes, I really only want to make sure it heats well and is safe to use. On those fronts, the Dreo Whole Room Space Heater gets the job done.

However, the Dreo goes far beyond what’s expected. The dual oscillation helps warm up larger spaces with greater speed and efficiency, and the safety features make me feel comfortable leaving it on when I’m out of the room for periods of time.

If you have larger rooms you want to warm up without turning on your central heating, or simply want to get a fuller warmth as opposed to an incredibly localized one, the Dreo Whole Room is a good horse to back.

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

The Vikings made Kyler Murray their quarterback choice to open the 2026 season, but it didn’t take long for their plans to go awry.

O’Connell said that he told Murray that he knows it has been a “less than ideal” series of events, but that underscores the possibility that the Vikings will have to turn back to him with an important game on the line at some point later this year.

Rand Paul chooses ranking member slot at Senate HELP

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Sen. Rand Paul, R-Ky., announced Wednesday he plans to serve as the top Republican on the Health, Education, Labor and Pensions Committee when Democrats take control of Congress next year.

The move clears the way for Senate Ethics Chairman James Lankford, R-Okla., to use his seniority to ascend to ranking Republican on the Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs Committee, which Paul currently chairs, and touches off several other shifts at the top of other committees.

The move fit in with Sen. Lisa Murkowski’s bid to use her seniority to succeed Senate Appropriations Chair Susan Collins, R-Maine, as top Republican on the Appropriations panel. If Paul did not go to the top spot on HELP, Murkowski, R-Alaska, would be next in line for that committee in the next Congress.

Murkowski is second in seniority on Appropriations to Collins, who was defeated for reelection. But she faces a potential challenge from Sen. Jerry Moran, R-Kan., who is next in line behind her.

Paul said in a press release that he plans to bring the same approach to the HELP Committee as he did as chairman of Homeland, where he said he “challenged entrenched bureaucracies, forced federal officials to answer for their actions, and pulled back the curtain on what Washington tried to keep hidden.”

He cited calling Dr. Anthony Fauci before the committee to probe the former federal health official’s role in pandemic-related shutdowns and the origin of the COVID-19 virus, and releasing thousands of pages of pandemic-related documents. The panel voted in August to hold Fauci in criminal contempt of Congress for declining to answer questions about the pandemic during a highly charged hearing.

Related: Fauci could face criminal contempt charge following panel vote

Paul, an ophthalmologist, said at HELP he will “stand against efforts to put Washington between Americans and their doctors, their children’s schools, and their paychecks.” He predicted “a rush toward socialism unlike anything we have seen” when the Democrat-controlled Congress convenes in January.

Lankford, in a separate statement, confirmed his interest in becoming the ranking member of the Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs Committee. In many areas, he said, the panel can work in a bipartisan manner, and over the past decade he “worked across the aisle to improve emergency communications in federal buildings, strengthen the United States Postal Service, increase federal cybersecurity preparedness, government transparency, and the use of artificial intelligence.”

But he added that his job also would be to “push back on a [Democratic] majority that goes too far . . . Oversight is responsible, but obstruction is unacceptable.”

Lankford said he would work together with the likely committee chair, Sen. Maggie Hassan, D-N.H., “when we can agree and be a fierce opposition if there is an attempt to undercut our nation’s security.”

‘The Gilded Age’ Star Ben Ahlers and Todd Snyder Know the Power of a Good Uniform

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

For the label’s latest campaign, the 30-year-old actor takes fall style to Scotland’s Isle of Islay.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

TODD SNYDER makes some of our favorite menswear, and for its latest collaboration, the New York brand crossed the pond to Scotland. The draw? Lagavulin, the distillery that’s been making smoky Scotch whisky on the island of Islay for more than 200 years.

The partnership marks Snyder’s 15th anniversary, with clothes that borrow from Islay’s rugged landscape. Standouts include a brown suede jacket lined in Lagavulin’s own tartan and Fair Isle zip-ups in the earthy shades of the Scottish moors. Pretty great reasons to look forward to sweater weather.

Fronting the campaign is Ben Ahlers. As footman-turned-businessman Jack Trotter on The Gilded Age, the 30-year-old actor knows his way around heritage menswear. Still, these relaxed layers are pretty easy to picture on the streets of New York. We caught up with him about what playing Jack taught him about getting dressed—and which piece he’d bring home.

MEN’S HEALTH: You spend a lot of time in period costumes on The Gilded Age. Has anything from Jack’s wardrobe made its way into how you dress off-screen?

BEN AHLERS: Jack has a consistency to the way that he dresses. As a footman, he had a uniform, and as a young businessman, he has a pretty steady throughline through his looks—a base structure with really well-made clothing and then accents like a tie or a tie pin or a ring that can allow for a sense of creative expression. I find that I like a level of consistency in my own personal dress as well.

More than anything, the greatest benefit of living inside of the clothing that Kasia [Walicka Maimone, the show’s costume designer] and her incredible team make for us every year is this awareness of the way clothes not only make you present to the world but also how they make you feel within them.

MH: Is there a menswear trend from that era you wish more guys would embrace today?

BA: I would love to see some of the creativity come out in menswear and in the accent work, like a tie pin. Some of the tie pins we have on this show are just so well-crafted and specific. The heads of canes the men wore back then also have such an artistry to them, and there aren’t a lot of avenues for that sort of expression in modern menswear. It’d be nice to see that creative voice or risk-taking come through in the subtle accents that we wear in our day-to-day life.

MH: Your role in Death of a Salesman got people talking about your physique. How did you prepare?

BA: For Death of a Salesman, I knew earlier on in the development process that they wanted to go the direction that they did for the top of the show, and so I knew that I was going to be kind of exposed every night. I really tried to go to the gym during the rehearsal process because I knew once we opened the show that it was going to be hard to stay as active—the show was just so draining.

The couple of months up to opening, I really made a practice of five days a week of cross-training, lifting, running, and yoga, and trying to keep my body as nimble and buoyant as possible, because the subject matter and the daily grind of showing up to the theater every day can really take a toll.

During the run, a lot of my focus was on rest, recovery, sauna, cold plunge, diet, stretching, because I’d already built the foundation of the physicality. Throughout the five-month run, it came down to being sure that I was healthy and recovering as best as I could.

MH: What happened between takes on the Todd Snyder shoot that we’d never know from looking at the campaign photos?

BA: Between takes, I had a blast with Boo George, our photographer. He is such a character and one of the best energies on set, and really delivered in the work. The whole crew from Todd Snyder and Lagavulin were so supportive, and it was such a fun little escape for all of us to exist on this island in the most beautiful landscape imaginable.

I really enjoyed getting to know a lot of the locals there. Many of the people there worked at the distillery, and I got to see firsthand how the whisky was actually made. People were so proud to share their culture as well as their craft with the world.

MH: Which piece from the collection would you wear back home in New York, and what would you pair it with?

BA: My favorite piece from the collection is the Todd Snyder x Lagavulin suede stable jacket, inspired by the texture and color of the mossy peat that coats Islay. It’s a perfect fall jacket, and I’d pair it with Todd Snyder’s relaxed barrel jean in khaki, which I think would really pop.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

MCR’s Tyler Morse Wants to Ban AI Bots From Booking His Hotels

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Photo Credit: Tyler Morse, chairman and CEO of MCR Hotels, talking to Skift's Seth Borko at the Skift Commerce Summit in New York City on November 11, 2026. Skift

MCR Hotels CEO and Chairman Tyler Morse dislikes AI personal assistants because they "make more rational decisions." He prefers human customers, who can more easily be persuaded to make impulse purchases.

Tyler Morse wants to stop personal AI agents from booking rooms at his hotels. The reason isn't privacy or regulation, according to the chairman and CEO of MCR Hotels. It's that the software can't be talked into a splurge.

"Everyone is going to ban the agents because it's in their interest to do so," Morse said Wednesday at the Skift Commerce Summit in New York.

Morse's objection to AI personal assistants is that they could be tougher to sell to than a human. That was different from United Airlines, which cited data privacy and regulatory risk for justifying its ban on AI bots announced the same day.

Morse explained that travel merchandising is built to trigger impulse purchases, with sites and apps full of strategical

Tags: artificial intelligence, hotel owners, mcr, mcr development, online travel, online travel agencies, skift commerce summit, tyler morse

Skift provides the insights. You make the decisions.

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

MCR Hotels Chairman and CEO Tyler Morse said he wants to block personal AI agents from booking at his hotels, and his stated reason is commercial rather than legal: software doesn't make impulse purchases. Speaking Wednesday at the Skift Commerce Summit in New York, Morse said travel merchandising is built around upsell offers and emotional triggers, and that AI assistants "make more rational decisions." That stance differs from United Airlines, which cited data privacy and regulatory risk when it announced its own ban on AI bots the same day. Morse acknowledged he has no way to carry out the block — "I can't figure out how to do it" — and Skift's review of robots.txt files found only 10 of 50 brand sites explicitly address AI crawlers. He expects suppliers to buy placement in AI recommendations much as they bid on sponsored links in Google search today, with a dominant AI intermediary such as Anthropic or OpenAI likely to emerge. Skift Research estimated global hotel distribution costs at roughly $105 billion in 2023, including about $75 billion through indirect channels. Morse said online travel agencies won't surrender easily, and that hotel operators lack comparable research departments and technology budgets in an industry split across 42,000 owners of 65,000 U.S. hotels.

2,200-year-old gold bear figurine and surprising number of bones found in massive burial mound in Kazakhstan

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Archaeologists have found a 2,200-year-old golden bear in an Iron Age burial in Kazakhstan that may have held seven people.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The gold bear would have been worn on a headdress.

Archaeologists in Kazakhstan have discovered a unique gold bear figurine in a 2,200-year-old burial mound that looters had hit but failed to empty completely.

The sculpted bear stands on its hind limbs and holds a pot-like container between its front paws. The artifact would have been worn as part of a headdress, and holes at its base indicate that it could have been sewn onto the garment, according to a translated statement from the Kazakhstan government.

The 2,200-year-old gold bear is a unique find in Kazakhstan.

The gold bear was unearthed at an Early Iron Age burial mound at the Karagaily burial ground in the southern city of Almaty, along with a surprisingly high number of human bones for a single burial mound.

Although other gold-crafted animals have been found in the region, the newly unearthed bear is the first of its kind.

"The sculpted bear figurine is unique to Kazakhstan. No similar finds have been discovered previously," Lyazzat Sagyndykova, director of the Almaty Museums Association, which conducted the excavation, said in the statement. "This discovery allows us to trace cultural parallels across the whole of Eurasia, from the Black Sea region to Altai."

Archaeologists in Kazakhstan excavated a 2,200-year-old burial mound.

The figurine dates to the Saka period, named for a nomadic horseback-riding people from Iran who lived on the Eurasian Steppe and Tarim Basin of what is now western China. Archaeologists have found comparable gold objects at other excavations in Kazakhstan, according to the statement. For example, a headdress element shaped like a small leopard previously came to light at the Almaty Kok-Kainar burial ground, which dates to the fourth century B.C. Also, the headdress of Kazakhstan's famous "Golden Man," a young Iron Age warrior buried in an extraordinarily lavish grave, also has a gold, sheep-shaped decorative element.

It's unclear which bear species the figurine depicts, but today Kazakhstan is home to two subspecies of brown bear: the Tien Shan or Himalayan brown bear (Ursus arctos isabellinus), which lives in the Tien Shan Mountains in the southeast, and the East Siberian brown bear (Ursus arctos collaris) in the Altai Mountains in the east, according to a study published earlier this year.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

Preliminary analyses date the recently excavated site to the fourth to third centuries B.C., according to the statement. Based on the number of bones they excavated, experts suspect that the mound, which is around 8 feet (2.4 meters) tall and roughly 85 feet (26 m) wide, may have been the final resting place for four adults and three children.

Archaeologists found a surprising number of human bones in the burial mound.

"Of particular interest is the unusually large number of human remains in a single burial mound, as such burials are atypical for Kazakhstan," Marat Mugalovich Nurpeisov, a senior research fellow at the Almaty Museums Association, said in the statement. "As a rule, burial mounds of a comparable size were intended for the burial of one or two people."

Archaeologists also found pieces of Saka pottery, most of which had been strewn about after the burial had been looted in ancient times, according to the statement. The thieves left behind a 10-foot (3 m) looting pit.

"Research into the Karagaily burial ground is ongoing," Nurpeisov said in the statement. "To clarify the circumstances of the burial and determine the exact number of individuals interred, further analyses are planned, including the involvement of anthropologists."

After all of the investigations are finished, some of the artifacts will be put on display at the Almaty Museum, according to the statement.

Piecing Together Romare Bearden’s Life

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

The best part of the new documentary about the famed artist is watching people interact with his work.

A new documentary about Romare Bearden embraces a collage-like sensibility, fitting well with the artist’s most influential work. Romare Bearden: A Life in Collage, soon to screen at DOC NYC, eschews the familiar structure of a linear biography, jumping around to find thematic rhymes through different periods of Bearden’s life.

Early on, for instance, the film alludes to Bearden’s family’s participation in the Great Migration, but rather than dwell on his biography, it turns to the recurring motif of trains in his work.

The film benefits from generous access to recordings of Bearden himself, who speaks with remarkable elegance about his memories, artistic process, and influences. Shaped by living in the South, Harlem, and Pittsburgh, his studies at the Sorbonne, and his time in the military and semiprofessional baseball during the first half of the 20th century, Bearden synthesized them into an oeuvre now seen as emblematic of the broader Black experience of the era.

28 Christmas Baby Names That Aren't Noel

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Whether your baby will be born in December, or you just love the holidays that much.

Of course when parents are thinking about what to name their new baby, they look toward the things they value most. Whether it’s a lifelong love of literature, a sentimental meaning, or simply a certain vibe that fits your lifestyle, choosing a name you really adore often starts with thinking about what already brings you happiness. And for some parents, that means Christmas. It’s a holiday all about togetherness, giving, joy, tradition — all wonderful values on their own. For many, Christmastime is also a source of some of their best memories of a magical childhood. It’s easy to see why parents might choose a Christmas-adjacent baby name, and Noel is not the one and only option. Here are some of our favorites:

This beautiful, classic girl’s name means “bright” or “clear.” And yes, Christmas is all about being merry and bright, but the real Christmas connection here is that Clara is the main character of the iconic Christmas ballet, The Nutcracker.

Douglas is a Scottish name meaning “dark stream.” But if you’re a Christmas lover, you’ll know that Douglas firs are an American evergreen tree species. This name is perfect if you love traditional boy names and would prefer one with a built-in nod to your favorite holiday.

Like the North Pole, of course! Kim Kardashian made the name popular when she gave it to her eldest child back in 2013, but now it feels like enough of a name that it wouldn’t be automatically associated with the young celeb.

If the Christmas story holds meaning for you, but naming your baby Mary or Joseph is a little too on the nose, consider Shepherd. It’s an occupation name (very trendy in recent years), and kind of doubles as a last name baby name too. But really, the shepherds in the fields were the first to hear the news of baby Jesus’s birth.

Do any of these names feel like the perfect pick for your little cherub?

How to Hunt the Best Day of the Rut No. 5: November 13

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

If you suffer from friggatriskaidekaphobia (fear of Friday the 13th), this Best Day of the Rut could come with a little apprehension. But for most whitetail hunters, a mid-November Friday should stir up anything but dread. After all, this is the peak of the rut, and almost anything can happen.

Several years ago, on this very day, I shot a mature buck on the edge of a cornfield just 15 minutes into my hunt as he stole a quick sip of water from a mud puddle and continued cruising along the stalks. As I got down and checked my arrow for blood a few moments later, an even larger buck charged in and almost ran me over. Intoxicated by rut hormones and trying to decipher if I was a threat or his next mate, he locked eyes with me for what seemed like minutes before crashing off through the corn.

As wild and exciting as the peak of the whitetail rut can be, it can also be taxing on both deer and hunters. Bucks have now been either searching for or chasing does for weeks, and many hunters have been working just as hard in pursuit of the. But if you haven’t found success yet, don’t lose heart. November 13th is my pick for the No. 1, overall, best day of the entire 2026 rut. So, lock in for a great hunt on this Friday, because the frenzy of the whitetail rut is in full swing.

Related: The 7 Best Days of the 2026 Whitetail Rut

The estrus bell curve is now at its height, meaning the largest portion of does on the landscape are currently in estrus and ready to breed. The action should be on fire, with one caveat—so long as you can find it. Scrapes have largely been abandoned for some time, exchanged for full-out seeking and chasing of does by testosterone-driven bucks. And peak-breeding is, of course, when the highest percentage bucks catch up to those does. Referred to as “the lockdown,” this is a period when bucks sequester does in attempts to guard and breed them, which makes seeing them from stand near impossible if you’re not in just the right spot.

On top of this, factors like weather and local herd dynamics also determine how intense the rut is perceived by hunters. Warm temperatures can drive activity to the nighttime, putting a damper on daylight activity, while areas with a skewed buck-to-doe ratios can see decreased chasing due to the vast breeding options bucks have. But rest assured, one thing is true—the rut is happening and is at this apex. So, here’s how you can break through the lockdown and put yourself in the middle of the action on this challenging, yet most promising Best Day of the 2026 Whitetail Rut.

Two factors push deer into heavy cover more than any others: the peak rut and hunting pressure. Since the first is definitely happening now, and the second is happening in many areas, picking an isolated bedding pocket for an all-day sit is a prime rut strategy. If you have a sanctuary on private land, now is one of the few times to consider entering this sanctum. For the public-land junkies, your pre-scouted, thick-cover bedding areas are by far your best bet for daylight action. Plany your Eentry and exit routes carefully and try to get settled an hour or more before light shooting light. This helps ensure that you’ll beat all deer back to these secluded haunts, and allows them to filter back to your bedding ambush totally alerted. One often-overlooked detail is the color of light you use when entering in the dark. Whitetail studies at the University of Georgia show white light appears 18 times brighter to deer than to humans, but red is next to impossible for them to see. So, if you need it, use a red headlamp, and put extra effort into planning an ultra-silent setup—two things that can make or break an early-morning entry.

What exactly should you be looking for in a prime rut bedding area? According to renown public-land whitetail expert John Eberhart, you should focus on “thick, hard-to-access, secluded areas where multiple trails converge and with ample shooting lanes. Bucks will push does back into these areas and cruise within them at midday, which is exactly where you need to be,” he says. Eberhart adds that the majority of bucks he’s killed have come during this stage of the rut, in these thick-cover areas, and often during midday hours.

If you’re not seeing deer in range, try a calling sequence to mimic a chase. An estrus doe bleat followed by a few excited grunts can bring any buck within earshot right to your tree. And don’t worry if the hours drag on and movement. This is exactly why you need to have ample food, water, and patience to wait it out, because a rut hunt in a bedding pocket can go from boring to big-buck-down in a matter of seconds.

Related: How to Rattle In Whitetail Bucks

If the prospect of sitting in the same spot all day doesn’t appeal to you, an good alternate play is to get down at midday and move to a transition zone outside of bedding cover or between bedding cover and evening food. Setup on the edge of thick cover to catch deer traffic and leverage the nightly deer movement toward a larger food source. This is exactly what I did when I hunted the edge of that standing corn several years ago. I was able to setup my sticks, platform, and saddle as several deer meandered among the rows just 60 yards from me. It’s also what brought the bigger buck running to me. Bucks are keyed up now, and when they cannot see far in thick cover, they must rely on their ears and can be easily fooled. Use this to your advantage by calling or rattling in these conditions to pull a buck out of nearby cover and into one of your shooting lanes.

I caught some of this intense rut behavior in a similar transition zone on public land a few years ago. Sitting in a pinch point surrounded by walls of thick slash, I watched several bucks chase a doe in and out of a small opening and ultimately had a front-row seat to the largest buck breeding her. Afterward, he looked around, blinked a couple times, and then bolted in back into the cover to find another doe. Right now, bucks know their job and don’t waste a minute getting to it, which could bring him right to you if you are in one of these right places at the just right time—which should be this Friday.

I would never suggest blowing up a hunting area on a Hail Mary if you think a little patience will pay off later. However, if you’re working with limited time, having eyes on a shooter buck is information you may want to act on. We’ve all been there. You spot a buck locked down and bedded with a doe through our binoculars while sitting in a tree well out of range. But if you have a few factors in your favor, you absolutely can make a move. A visual barrier like a hill or dense cover and some cover sound, such as an afternoon wind, can disguise your movements, and turn these situations into big opportunities. Just make sure to take your time and keep an eye peeled on those deer, especially the doe—the savior of many a brain-dead rutting buck in mid-November.

Feds arrest top official of healthcare company, alleging illegal drug importation

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

U.S. authorities are accusing the two top officials of a Canadian company of operating a long-running scheme to illegally import prescription drugs into the U.S., according to court records filed Tuesday.

John Thomas Howard, treasurer and a director of the company, CANARX, was arrested Sunday on smuggling and conspiracy charges, according to the records.

Bob Howard, his brother, who is president and a director of CANARX, is also wanted in the federal investigation, according to an affidavit from Dustin Dobbs, a special agent with the Food and Drug Administration's Office of Criminal Investigations. He has not yet been taken into custody.

Besides conspiracy and smuggling, the arrest warrant said John Thomas Howard was charged with introducing misbranded drugs into interstate commerce and illegal drug importation. No plea has been filed.

CANARX was featured in a CNBC investigation last year that revealed how the company and others known as alternative funding programs, or AFPs, are becoming more pervasive around the U.S. as drug costs skyrocket. The investigation found these companies have penetrated the country's healthcare system by offering high-cost specialty drugs at more affordable prices through private and public employers. In some cases, employers are requiring their staff to use an AFP.

AFPs often obtain the medications overseas and ship them directly to patients, a practice that federal authorities have said is illegal and poses safety risks. CNBC traced the trail of drugs from abroad to the U.S. via a supply chain that the pharmaceutical companies producing the medications have not authorized.

On its website, CANARX, founded in 2002 and based in Windsor, Ontario, Canada, described its business as the first international prescription provider for the U.S. and the safest such program in the industry. The company said it sources its medications from Canada, the United Kingdom, Australia and New Zealand.

But the affidavit said that the FDA's investigation found "there is probable cause to believe that (CANARX) illegally imported or caused the illegal importation of prescription drugs into the United States …."

The affidavit does not specify which medications the agency believes were illegally imported. The criminal complaint covers the period from Feb. 22, 2017, through April 15, 2026.

The company's general counsel, Joseph Morris, told CNBC in a statement: "The Howards deny all the charges against them, will defend vigorously, and expect to be vindicated in court."

The company has been under FDA scrutiny for several years. In 2023, the agency seized medication being shipped by CANARX that it says was potentially misbranded and counterfeit, according to an email sent to a patient that was obtained by CNBC.

And in 2019, the FDA sent a warning letter to the company, saying it was operating "in a manner that substitutes the FDA-approved drugs prescribed by the U.S. healthcare provider with unapproved drugs." Morris, in response to that warning, argued CANARX was not misleading patients, but agreed for the company to stop importing drugs that need to be administered by a doctor.

In an interview as part of the CNBC investigation, Morris said last year that the medicines shipped to U.S. patients come from highly regulated countries. He said CANARX was operating legally.

The central question for AFPs is the legality of importing prescription drugs into the U.S. for personal use. The FDA allows Americans to import medications for individual use in some cases if the drug is not available domestically. That is the basis on which the AFPs built their models for shipping medicines directly to patients.

But an FDA spokesperson told CNBC last year that "in most circumstances, a person cannot import prescription drugs from other countries into the U.S. ... as substitutes for FDA-approved drugs."

In a separate civil lawsuit that is pending, Gilead Sciences accused CANARX and other companies of illegally importing Biktarvy, its medication to treat HIV.

In a statement to CNBC on Wednesday, a Gilead spokesperson said the company "has taken strong legal action to protect patients and the integrity of the U.S. medicines supply chain. We welcome the government's efforts to hold accountable those whose conduct may put patients at risk."

CANARX has not responded to the Gilead lawsuit.

— CNBC's Paige Tortorelli and Melissa Lee contributed to this report

The Ultimate Guide to the Rolex Daytona, the World’s Most Wanted Watch

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

From the hand-wound watches dealers struggled to sell to the steel model that commands an impressive premium today.

In September, four Rolex Daytonas sold at a charity auction in London for a combined £37.3 million (about $49.5 million). The most expensive, at £10.3 ($13.7) million, had a turquoise dial and a ring of pink sapphires. That same month, Rolex unveiled a steel-and-platinum Daytona with a white enamel dial and a speed scale that recalled the original from 1963.

The regular steel Daytona with a white dial costs $16,900 from Rolex, if you can get one. In September, WatchCharts put its secondary-market value around $35,000. It wasn’t always like this. In its early years, according to Sotheby’s, retailers discounted Daytonas to clear stock.

Rolex launched the Cosmograph in 1963. An early advert called it the “Le Mans,” after the French endurance race. The name that stuck came from Daytona, Florida, where cars raced on hard-packed sand before the city had a speedway.

The first model, reference 6239, had a movement you wound by hand and two buttons to operate the stopwatch. Rolex put the speed scale on the steel bezel, where a driver could read it at a glance. The three small counters stood out against the dial, black on a light face or light on a black one.

By the 1970s, two models had emerged that collectors still prize today. The 6263 had a black acrylic bezel, and the 6265 had a metal one. Both had screw-down collars around the stopwatch buttons. On some, the word Daytona curves in red above the bottom counter. Collectors call these “Big Reds.”

Joanne Woodward gave Newman a 6239 around the time he made Winning, the 1969 film that sparked his interest in racing. She had “DRIVE CAREFULLY ME” engraved on the back. “There’s something almost magical about it,” James Cox, who later owned the watch, told Phillips. In 2017 it sold for $17.75 million, then an auction record for a wristwatch.

A “Paul Newman” usually refers to a type of dial. Look for the unusual numerals and small square-ended markers in the counters. The dials were unpopular when new. By 2013, Christie’s could sell 50 Daytonas in a single Geneva auction for $13.2 million. The top lot was a steel watch with a Paul Newman dial. It fetched $1.09 million, four years before the actor’s own watch went to auction.

On one particularly rare black-dial 6263, the word Oyster appears below Rolex Cosmograph. Collectors call it the Oyster Sotto, from the Italian for under. Phillips sold one in 2025 for 1.39 million Swiss francs (approximately $1.67 million). “One mistake people make is that they expect everything to fit neatly into a specific set of rules,” Geoff Hess, global head of watches at Sotheby’s, once explained. A dial can be genuine Rolex and still have come from a different watch.

In 1988 the steel Daytona became the 16520. It grew to 40mm, gained raised gold hour markers and a glossier dial, and wound itself as you wore it. Rolex extensively reworked Zenith’s El Primero movement for the watch. It was the first automatic Daytona and the model that established the basic dimensions of the watch Rolex still makes today.

On some early examples, a gap makes the word Cosmograph appear to float beneath the rest of the lettering. On certain black dials, the rings around the counters have turned brown with age. Those are known as Patrizzi dials, after the auctioneer Osvaldo Patrizzi, who noticed the change.

Three steel references are worth knowing. The 116520, introduced in 2000, has a steel bezel and the first chronograph movement Rolex developed and made itself. The 116500LN, from 2016, brought back the look of the black acrylic bezel in ceramic. The current 126500LN, introduced in 2023, has a slim steel rim around that ceramic bezel, a revised dial, and a new movement that runs for about three days when fully wound. The two ceramic models can look remarkably similar in listing photographs.

The unicorn is a hand-wound 6265 that Phillips described in 2018 as the only known vintage Daytona in white gold. It sold for 5.94 million Swiss francs (about $7.1 million). The Rainbow gets its name from the graduated sapphires around its bezel. A white-gold example made around 1994, nearly two decades before the production versions appeared, sold in 2024 for 5.505 million Swiss francs (approximately $6.1 million).

Sixty years after that early advertisement, Rolex returned to Le Mans. Its 2023 watch for the race’s centenary has a stopwatch that counts the full 24 hours. The regular Daytona counts to 12.

An official Rolex retailer is the place to start if you want a new one at list price. Retailers manage their own allocations, so availability varies. Rolex addressed the shortage in 2021. “The scarcity of our products is not a strategy on our part,” it said, explaining that it could not meet demand without compromising quality.

On the secondary market, compare the same reference and dial. The WatchCharts September estimate for the current black-dial steel model was about $29,000, roughly $6,000 less than the white. If you’re buying vintage, ask a specialist dealer or auction house for a condition report. Find out whether the case has been heavily polished, which parts have been replaced, and whether the dial is correct for that particular watch. On a Daytona, those details can explain more about the price than the reference number.

Jaden Smith Is Taking Louboutin into the Future
