I Looked Beyond Silicon Valley and Found a New Playbook For Building Startups

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Founders in small countries can’t rely on abundant capital or elite networks. Here’s what startups in big markets can learn from them.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

Silicon Valley teaches founders to think big. Smaller startup markets teach them how to build when almost nothing is guaranteed.

Over the past year, I have met founders and investors across the Baltics, Georgia and Guatemala through Baltic Fintech Days, a 500 Global Eurasia Demo Day in Tbilisi and Volcano Summit. These ecosystems differ in language, history and access to capital, but the strongest founders shared a common advantage: They could not rely on a large domestic market, abundant venture funding or proximity to the world’s most powerful networks.

That constraint shaped how they built. They thought internationally earlier, stretched resources further and cultivated relationships across borders. As capital becomes more selective and growth at all costs continues to fall out of favor, founders everywhere can learn from companies built in markets that have never had the luxury of excess.

Here are five lessons from some of the world’s smallest startup ecosystems.

A founder in the United States can build a large company without ever leaving the domestic market. Founders in Latvia, Lithuania, Estonia, Georgia or Guatemala rarely have that option.

The entire population of Lithuania is smaller than that of Los Angeles. Latvia has fewer than 2 million residents, while Georgia has fewer than 4 million. Even Guatemala, the largest of the markets I visited, represents only a fraction of the purchasing power available in the United States.

As a result, international expansion is not a distant ambition for many of these founders. It is part of the original business model.

At Baltic Fintech Days, I met entrepreneurs who were thinking about cross-border payments, compliance and market entry well before reaching the scale at which an American startup might begin considering international growth. Their home markets gave them useful places to test, but not enough room to stop there.

This forces founders to ask important questions early: Can the product travel? Can it serve customers across languages, currencies and regulatory systems? Does the problem exist beyond the founder’s immediate network?

Building globally does not mean launching in 10 countries at once. It means avoiding assumptions that make international expansion unnecessarily difficult later. Founders should consider localization, payment infrastructure, regulation and cross-border distribution while the company is still young enough to adapt.

Founders love to talk about resourcefulness, but the concept takes on a different meaning in ecosystems where capital, talent and customers are all more difficult to access.

At 500 Global’s Eurasia Demo Day in Tbilisi, I saw companies working to gain attention far beyond their immediate geography. They could not assume that investors would already understand their markets, recognize their universities or trust their local references. They had to communicate the problem, the opportunity and their credibility with unusual precision.

That constraint can produce better companies.

When capital is scarce, founders have less room for performative hiring, unnecessary tools or growth that cannot be tied to customer demand. They learn to test assumptions quickly, sell before everything is perfect and use partnerships to extend their reach.

Founders in major technology hubs should apply the same discipline even when more resources are available. Ask what the company would prioritize if it had half the budget, half the team and half the time. The answer often reveals which initiatives are truly essential.

Scarcity is painful, but it can also create focus. A company that learns to operate efficiently before raising significant capital may be better prepared to survive market corrections, fundraising delays and unexpected competition.

Smaller markets are often discussed only in terms of what they lack. That framing overlooks what they can offer.

The Baltics have developed deep expertise in fintech, digital infrastructure and cross-border business. Georgia sits at the intersection of Europe and Asia and is developing a generation of founders accustomed to navigating multiple markets. Guatemala offers access to Central America, proximity to the United States and entrepreneurs with firsthand knowledge of industries and consumers that remain poorly understood by many global investors.

At Volcano Summit in Guatemala, the value of the ecosystem was not simply that it brought international investors into the country. It created a place where local and regional founders could build relationships without first relocating to San Francisco, Miami or New York.

Founders should resist the urge to erase where they come from in an attempt to appear more investable. Geography can be a source of differentiated insight.

A founder who understands regional payment behavior, informal commerce, remittances or local regulation may see an opportunity long before an outsider does. The goal is not to build a business that only works locally. It is to turn local knowledge into an advantage that can travel.

The most compelling question is not, “Why would a major company come from this market?” It is, “What can a founder see from this market that others cannot?”

In mature startup hubs, founders can mistake proximity for access. They may attend dozens of events, collect hundreds of contacts and still fail to build relationships that materially help the company.

In smaller ecosystems, relationships often function as infrastructure.

One trusted introduction can connect a founder to a customer, investor or market that would otherwise be difficult to reach. Government agencies, accelerators, diaspora networks and local operators frequently play an outsized role in helping companies cross borders.

I saw versions of this at Baltic Fintech Days, through 500 Global’s network in Georgia and at Volcano Summit. These gatherings mattered because they connected local credibility with international reach. They reduced the distance between founders and people who could help them enter new markets.

Founders should approach relationship-building with the same discipline they apply to product development. Instead of asking how many people they met, they should ask whether they are building trust with the right customers, partners and advocates.

That also means becoming useful before asking for something. Founders can share market intelligence, make introductions and offer thoughtful feedback. In smaller ecosystems, reputations travel quickly. Generosity and reliability compound.

A founder in a well-known ecosystem benefits from familiar signals. Investors recognize the schools, employers, accelerators and other funds around the table. Founders from less visible markets must often establish credibility without those shortcuts.

That makes storytelling a core business capability.

The strongest founders I encountered did not simply explain what their products did. They connected a local problem to a much larger shift. A payments challenge became a story about global commerce. A compliance product became infrastructure for the international workforce. A regional marketplace became evidence of changing consumer behavior across emerging economies.

Founders should not expect investors or customers to do this translation themselves.

A strong pitch should answer four questions: Why does this problem matter? Why is the opportunity larger than it first appears? Why is this team uniquely equipped to solve it? And why is now the right moment?

This is especially important for founders building outside established technology centers. Investors may incorrectly interpret an unfamiliar market as a small opportunity. The founder’s job is to distinguish the company’s starting point from its potential destination.

Talent is distributed far more evenly than capital, attention or access. Some of the world’s most ambitious founders are building in places that receive only a fraction of global venture investment.

The Baltics, Georgia and Guatemala are not interchangeable, nor should they be reduced to a single category of “emerging markets.” Each has its own strengths, challenges and history. What connects them is the resourcefulness required to build beyond the limits of a small domestic ecosystem.

Founders everywhere can learn from that mindset.

Start globally. Use constraints to sharpen your decisions. Turn local knowledge into an advantage. Treat relationships as infrastructure. And make the scale of your ambition impossible to misunderstand.

The next category-defining company may not come from the place investors visit most often. It may come from the market whose founders learned to build without waiting for the world to notice.

Silicon Valley teaches founders to think big. Smaller startup markets teach them how to build when almost nothing is guaranteed.

Over the past year, I have met founders and investors across the Baltics, Georgia and Guatemala through Baltic Fintech Days, a 500 Global Eurasia Demo Day in Tbilisi and Volcano Summit. These ecosystems differ in language, history and access to capital, but the strongest founders shared a common advantage: They could not rely on a large domestic market, abundant venture funding or proximity to the world’s most powerful networks.

That constraint shaped how they built. They thought internationally earlier, stretched resources further and cultivated relationships across borders. As capital becomes more selective and growth at all costs continues to fall out of favor, founders everywhere can learn from companies built in markets that have never had the luxury of excess.

10 Hacks Every Hulu User Should Know

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City. After graduating from Duke University, she spent several years reporting on the federal workforce for Government Executive, a publication of Atlantic Media Company, in Washington, D.C. She has nearly a decade of experience as a freelancer covering tech (including issues related to security, privacy, and streaming) as well as personal finance and travel.

In addition to Lifehacker, her work has been featured on Wirecutter, Tom’s Guide, and ZDNET. Emily has also worked as a travel guide around the U.S. and as a content editor. She has a masters in social work and is a licensed therapist in Utah.

If you don't like the content Hulu is suggesting for you, you have several options to curate your recommendations. "Disliking" a show or movie removes it from your Home view and You May Also Like entirely, so the only way you'll see that content is if you search for it. You can dislike on the Details page if you're watching on your TV or use the thumbs down on mobile or web. (The "Like" button will obviously influence your algorithm in the opposite way). If your "Continue Watching" queue is cluttered with shows you started but didn't like enough to finish, you can remove them from your watch history as well as the My Stuff list. This, too, will allow content to remain searchable but not suggested in other areas of the app.

If PIN protection is enabled, users must enter a four-digit PIN when switching between profiles on a single account. This is intended as a parental control feature, since it's available on non-Kids profiles and can keep children from accessing more mature content on an adult's profile. However, you can also enable this feature to keep other users from seeing your viewing history and preferences or influencing your algorithm by watching their content under your profile (accidentally or on purpose). To set up a PIN, log into your account on Hulu's website, select your profile, and go to Manage Profiles > Parental Controls. You'll have to choose a PIN and enter your password to confirm the changes.

Another approach to protecting your profile and algorithm is to set up separate profiles for specific use cases, such as when you have guests or if you host watch parties for certain shows. This keeps content from influencing your recommendations and prevents other viewers from seeing what's in your watch history or saved content (especially if there are things you want to keep private).

If you find that subtitles and captions are too bold—or not visible enough—you can change the font, color, size, and style from the default so they enhance your viewing experience rather than detracting from it. Hulu allows you to adjust the color and opacity of the font, background, and window so you get the right contrast, and you can also select the specific font and font scaling. On your streaming device, go to Settings > Subtitles & Captions, then hit the back button on your remote to save your preferences. You can update these settings on mobile and desktop as well.

If you have a long list of shows and films to watch but can't decide what to start next, look for the "Expiring Soon" badge to see which titles are leaving the service in the next two weeks. Badges display the number of days remaining, so you can determine what to prioritize and whether it's worth diving in based on how much time you have to finish before the expiration date. Go to "My Stuff" to monitor saved titles that are expiring soon.

If you've been watching Hulu under an account shared with family or friends and need to switch to your own subscription, you can transfer your profile to keep your watch history and settings intact instead of starting from scratch. You can initiate a profile transfer on a TV-connected device. Note that account holders have to approve transfer requests within seven days, and once expired, transfer requests cannot be resubmitted—so be sure to let the primary user know that you've made the request.

If you've shared your Hulu login information with friends or activated your account on devices you don't own, you may want to regularly audit where your account is being used and remove any that don't need access. This includes TVs in Airbnbs and hotels. If you stay logged in, unauthorized users may have access to your Hulu account and personal data. You can see activated devices (and their activation date) under Your Account > Watch Hulu on Your Devices > Manage Devices. Click Remove next to old or unrecognized devices.

Hulu has an offline mode that allows you to download shows and movies to watch when you don't have a network connection. This is useful for traveling or when you know your wifi or cell service will be limited. Downloads are only available on supported iOS and Android devices, which means you may have to consider how much space your content takes up. You can select High for video quality if you want maximum resolution, but you may want to choose Standard if you are short on storage. This option is available under Downloads > Settings > Video Quality on iOS and Account > Settings > Downloads > Video Quality on Android. Note that once you click play on downloaded content, you have 48 hours to watch it before it expires—so don't start it unless you are ready.

If you have a Hulu + Live TV subscription, you must designate a "Home" network, which determines the local channels included in your plan. You can update your location up to four times within a 12-month period, but this limitation is meant to prevent viewers outside a household from sharing a subscription. Hulu does allow you to stream live TV on mobile when you're away from your home network (within the U.S.) as long as you access your account on that network every 30 days. If you travel often, check in with your mobile device frequently to avoid being locked out when you're away. Note that you can still watch streaming content with no restrictions.

Citi Says AI Companies Should Pay When Their Agents Book the Wrong Travel

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Photo Credit: Abhinav Anand, Head of Value Cards, Lending and Commerce, Citi, at the Skift Commerce Summit in NYC November 11, 2026. Skift

AI agents are already booking travel, and they get things wrong. The industry hasn’t settled who absorbs the cost when they do. Citi wants the agent companies on the hook, which would spare hotels and airlines, but could slow how fast agents transact.

When an AI agent books the wrong trip, someone has to pay for the mistake. Citi thinks it should be the company behind the agent, not the hotel, airline or card issuer.

“Who takes the agent liability” is the biggest question banks, payment networks, merchants and AI companies are trying to answer, said Abhinav Anand, Citi’s head of value cards, lending and commerce for U.S. consumer cards, at the Skift Commerce Summit in New York on Wednesday.

In Citi’s view, Anand said, liability should fall “to the agent themselves, the company that is actually coming with the agent.”

One way that could work: agents that want to transact on Visa or Mastercard might have to accept some liability w

Tags: artificial intelligence, business travel, citi, online travel newsletter, payments, skift commerce summit, travel tech

Skift provides the insights. You make the decisions.

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

Citi's head of value cards, lending and commerce for U.S. consumer cards, Abhinav Anand, said at the Skift Commerce Summit in New York that liability for AI agent errors should sit with the company that builds the agent, not with hotels, airlines, or card issuers. Anand said one way to do that is for agents seeking to transact on Visa or Mastercard to accept some liability when they register with the networks. He cited a Visa study of more than 75 agents that he said found 43% made mistakes, with about half of those completing the purchase anyway, and even the best performers — which he said included Google's Gemini and Meta's Muse — getting it right about 82% of the time. To settle disputes, Anand said the industry is working to carry the traveler's purchase instructions inside the tokenized transaction so issuers and merchants can see the original intent when a charge is disputed. He also said early industry data show travelers arriving through agent-led search and discovery are 40% to 50% more likely to convert and spend more per transaction. Citi, among the first issuers enabled for agentic payments with Mastercard and more recently Visa, is building its own AI-enabled travel concierge inside its app; third-party agents can't use Citi ThankYou points today.

Hauser & Wirth Now Represents rafa esparza, Maker of Adobe Sculptures and Paintings That Center Community

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Hauser & Wirth, one of the largest galleries in the world, now represents Los Angeles–based artist rafa esparza, who is best-known for his installations, sculptures, and paintings made from adobe. The representation will be done in collaboration with LA’s Commonwealth and Council, the artist’s longtime gallery.

The news makes esparza one of the few living Latinx artists to be represented by a mega-gallery. Hauser & Wirth also represents Firelei Baéz and Angel Otero.

esparza will have his first solo show with the gallery in fall 2027 in one of its New York spaces, and he will debut a new work at Hauser & Wirth’s Art Basel Miami Beach booth next month. Next February, Zolo Press will publish his first monograph, titled rafa esparza: Works on Adobe. Related Articles Frieze London Brings 400-Year-Old Paintings and Collectors in Their 20s Painter Lucy Bull Joins Hauser & Wirth as Her Market Continues Its Ascent

Since 2014, esparza has been working with adobe, a building material that combines loam, straw, manure, and water, typically to make bricks that are then baked in the sun. esparza learned the technique from his father, whose family has been using adobe for generations in Durango, Mexico.

Initially, esparza began making adobe bricks that he would use to transform and challenge the art spaces in which they are exhibited: The organic materials in adobe counter museums’ strict climate control settings, while the “brown architectures,” as the artist calls his creations, take on their white cubes. Many of his interventions allow for visitors to walk directly on the adobe, which begins to crumble during the exhibition’s run.

He memorably presented these works at the 2016 Made in L.A. biennial (on an outdoor terrace) and the 2017 Whitney Biennial (in the Whitney’s ground floor gallery). The Whitney Biennial work took the form of a rotunda which esparza invited Latinx artists who were not formally part of the biennial to exhibit their work or stage performances; his collaborators included Beatriz Cortez, Gala Porras-Kim, Eamon Ore-Girón, and Nao Bustamante. The bricks used in the Whitney installation were then transported to Marfa, Texas, where they were installed on the facade and interior of Ballroom Marfa.

More recently, esparza has begun painting directly onto the adobe, creating works intimate portraits, medium-sized translations of photographs documenting important historical moments, and large-scale sculptures that are supported by metal armatures that are visible from behind. Tratos: Después de Pedro Valtierra Entre Redadas (2025–26), for example, had a US flag embedded in the metal structure, which supported a painting of a police officer manhandling a protester.

He has staged performances and had solo exhibitions at the Museum of Contemporary Art Los Angeles (in 2018), the Institute of Contemporary Art Los Angeles (2018), Performance Space New York (2019), and Artists Space (2023). With Cortez, he has had two-person exhibitions at the Americas Society in New York (2025) and the Museo Anahuacali in Mexico City (2026). Other important group shows that his work has been featured in include “Xican-a.o.x Body” at the Cheech (2023), “At the Edge of the Sun” at Jeffrey Dietch in Los Angeles (2024), the Prospect.6 triennial in New Orleans (2025), and the Latinx painting survey “Let us Gather in a Flourishing Way” at the Buffalo AKG Art Museum (2026).

“We are thrilled to join Commonwealth and Council in representing rafa esparza, an artist of profound humanity and dedication to community,” Hauser & Wirth president Marc Payot said in a statement. “rafa brings real warmth to a conceptual practice that is enormously rigorous and disciplined, drawing the earth quite literally into works that mark time and the human hand while harkening to centuries of challenging questions about ownership, agency and belonging. It will be an honor to share his art with increasingly international audiences and new generations.”

The Best Stocking Stuffer for Jet-Setters Is This Viral Handbag Accessory

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

If there’s one thing I love almost as much as planning a trip, it’s having a cute lineup of travel gear to help me get there. The perfect carry-on, a reliable travel tote, a passport holder that doesn’t look like everyone else’s—and, as of late, a bag charm (or three). The accessory du jour has officially made its way from our handbags to our airport ensembles, and I can’t think of a more delightful stocking stuffer for the traveler in your life.

Commerce writer Jessica Chapel is showing off Coach's signature cherry bag charm

Bag charms have a longer fashion history than their current moment suggests: Jane Birkin famously adorned her namesake Hermès with personal keepsakes. Hermès then introduced its Rodeo charm in 2005, and Fendi sent its furry Monster charms down the runway in 2013. After a runway revival at Miu Miu and Balenciaga shows in 2024, the look made its way back to stylish travelers everywhere.

It makes sense, really: We put so much thought into what we pack and what we carry, so why not have a little fun with the bags that get us there? A bag charm is an easy way to give a well-loved weekender a fresh look, or make that familiar canvas tote feel a bit more like your own. There are colorful beads, mini leather accessories, nostalgic trinkets, whimsy characters, and even designs with a practical purpose. Some bag charms are simply there to look good; others offer a clever place to tuck away small essentials. And when your suitcase is circling the baggage carousel among a sea of lookalikes, a fun, personal detail is hardly a bad thing.

Beyond the styling, I like to think of bag charms as a new kind of travel keepsake. We’re always bringing pieces of our trips home: a street market find, a knickknack from a beach town, some wonderfully impractical souvenir that means everything to us and very little to anyone else. Rather than tucking them away on a shelf, clip these charms onto a tote or suitcase, and they’ll join you for the next adventure. The same goes for a charm gifted by a friend or one that reminds you of home. Nothing says, “think of me on the road,” quite like this.

Bag charms add a pop of color to a neutral bag or suitcase

And there’s no shortage of ways to make the look your own. Layer a few bag charms together, mixing colors, textures, and shapes, or keep things more understated with a single leather accent or jewelry-like detail. Think of it as accessorizing your luggage with the same consideration you give the rest of your travel outfit. Whether you’re shopping for the friend who’s always planning a weekend away, the family member with an enviable vintage luggage collection, or the frequent flier who appreciates thoughtful detail, there’s something in the mix for just about every kind of traveler.

Bag charms are small, they’re personal, they’re wonderfully extra, and truthfully, a whole lot more exciting to unwrap than another pair of compression socks. (Sorry, compression socks). Ahead, I’ve rounded up the best bag charms to gift this season, from playful fashion finds and colorful conversation starters to travel-friendly designs that do a little more than just look pretty.

A little cherry on top, quite literally. Made from glittery resin and metal, this bright red pick brings a playful pop of color to a tote, backpack, or set of keys. It’s a particularly good choice for the friend whose wardrobe could always use one more fun thing.

For the person who prefers their accessories a little more understated—but still appreciates a good detail—this bad boy pairs deep green beads with gold-tone hardware and a lobster clasp, offering their favorite bag a subtle hit of color and texture.

For the New Yorker in your life, here's some Big Apple nostalgia to take on the road. Kate Spade pays homage to its hometown with this mini MetroCard-inspired design—and for those who remember the ol’ swipe of a subway card, it's a fun yet tasteful piece of the city to carry along.

I’m adding this gold boot gem to my cart. For a little Western flair—minus the need to break in a new pair of boots—the iconic brand Tecovas has turned its Loretta boot into a mini accessory crafted from solid brass with an antique finish. It comes with both a key ring and a D-ring, so it can go wherever its owner does—whether that’s a road trip, a dude ranch weekend, or simply the daily commute.

Talk about chic! Sézane’s Juliette keychain has a caramel-color resin exterior with delicate white lace detailing, but the real treat is inside: a pocket mirror that opens with a pivoting mechanism. It’s ideal for a quick lipstick check before stepping off the train or heading out to dinner.

For the friend who has approximately 1,000 photos of their dog on their phone—and would happily add one more to the rotation. This little Jack Russell is embroidered onto supple full-grain leather, with a debossed Lauren Ralph Lauren logo on the back. A rather handsome stand-in for a four-legged travel buddy.

A handbag for your handbag? I’m listening. This mini zippered pouch is styled like a classic carryall, with leather construction and gold-tone hardware, and clips onto a larger bag to keep small essentials close. It’s available in red, cobalt blue, and silver, and can be personalized with a foil-debossed monogram—making it an especially sweet gift for a loved one.

A little luck for the road never hurts! This Madewell design brings together a wishbone, stone, and moon motif in a red-and-gold-toned cluster, and is finished with a dog-clip closure. It’s a playful pick for the friend who loves a meaningful trinket—or could use a little good fortune on their next big adventure.

For the friend who's always planning their next trip, this miniature version of Away’s signature hard-shell carry-on is made from the same durable polycarbonate shell and opens into two little compartments for small essentials like a spare key or folded cash. It clips onto a bag or backpack, and the Coast Blue shade is a nice nod to the real thing.

Meet the mini-me of one of our editors' favorite travel bags. Calpak's best-selling Luka duffel—this compact zip pouch comes in a pearlescent finish and clips onto a larger bag with a carabiner-ready loop. Better yet, it actually earns its keep: There’s room for keys, earbuds, cash, or whatever small essentials you’d rather not fish around for at the bottom of your tote.

A flower that doesn’t need watering—or a vase. Inspired by the marigolds of Diwali, this three-dimensional design is made from layered Italian leather petals and comes in yellow or orange. The leather leaf can also be monogrammed, making it a thoughtful gift with a personal touch. It’s a lovely way to bring a little color to someone’s everyday bag.

How I accidentally became a millionaire

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

I am not a millionaire. I have never been one. And yet, if you were to ask Google about it, you might just walk away with the impression that I am.

Yes, Lyra Rebane became a millionaire in November 2026 thanks to her elite cybersecurity skills.

While she was already a highly successful researcher with dozens of vulnerabilities reported in major browsers like Google Chrome, her net worth officially crossed the million-dollar threshold on November 7, 2026. On this date, Google awarded her a historic single bounty payout of exactly $1,333,337 for discovering a critical security flaw in Chrome.

I am a hacker, though. I am a hacker with 14 Chrome CVEs. And I think I am a good hacker. But I don’t think I’m the bestest hacker. I don’t believe I’m capable of finding a vulnerability that’s worth over a million dollars. And even if I were, I don’t think Google would pay that much for a Chromium vulnerability.

Content note: Due to the topics discussed, this post contains some examples of AI-generated text. Those examples are clearly differentiated1. Everything else in this blog post is fully human-made - I do not use AI.

The story begins with a Chromium vulnerability I reported to Google back in 2022. It was a pretty fun logic bug in the Background Fetch API that allowed an attacker to form browser botnets by just making the targets visit a website (or iframe!).

Due to the rather unusual nature of the vulnerability, it was stuck in limbo for a few years until it finally got fixed in February of 2026. Google gave me a $1000 bounty, and a few months later the bug became public.

This bug has been closed for more than 14 weeks. Removing issue access restrictions.

Actually nevermind, turns out it was merely marked as fixed. And thus, yours truly appears in an article titled “Google publishes exploit code threatening millions of Chromium users”.

Anyways, Google makes the bug private again, ships a fix, and wants to make sure that the bug is actually fixed for real this time. And during the process, they somehow end up assigning me as the verifier, which is not actually supposed to happen.

So the way reporting security bugs in Chromium works is that your reports go to this platform called the Buganizer2. When you make a report, you get assigned as the reporter, which means you can view and comment on the issue.

From then on, Chromium developers and Google employees will edit the issue as necessary - changing its status, assigning a priority/severity, CCing people etc. One of the things they do is giving a bug bounty to the reporter, for which the VRP panel calculates a sum and then sets the vrp-reward field on the issue.

As an external reporter, you cannot edit any of those fields. But people set as the assignees, verifiers and collaborators can! And in this case, I was set as the verifier.

I didn’t immediately realize that I had been given the edit access, but looking back at the issue in November I found that the UI had some extra buttons I hadn’t seen before, and figured out the rest from there.

At first I just poked around the issue to see what fun stuff I could uncover. While doing that, I somehow managed to accidentally click on a sudden popup menu, which immediately assigned the issue to a bot account.

Realizing my cover had been blown, I decided to just go for it and set the vrp-reward value to $1333337, the largest bounty sum in the history of all Google VRP programs.

Having achieved my newfound wealth, I decided the next best step would be to announce it on the everything app3. I made my tweet in two parts - first, an announcement of achieving the record bounty, and then a video of me changing the vrp-reward value as the punchline.

We ❤️ 🐜🐞🦗🦟🦋. {echo,{{{Google,Chrome,Android,Abuse,Mobile,OSS,Cloud}Vulnerability,Patch}Reward,VulnerabilityResearchGrants}Program}

In just an hour, the joke tweet had already reached 600 likes. Since my intent was to just make a joke and not actually deceive anyone, and the tweet was clearly escaping containment, I followed it up with a clarification.

Now, Twitter does actually have a feature to combat disinformation - Birdwatch (aka Community Notes) is a system that lets people add context to misleading posts. And this feature was in fact used on my tweet - in just a couple hours, the likes stopped rolling in, and this funky box appeared underneath.

Proposed by an experimental AI contributor responding to requests for a note. Like all contributors, AIs can make mistakes — verify accuracy.

The $1,333,337 Chrome VRP bounty claim is false; the author edited the screenshot themselves as a joke after gaining mistaken edit access and Google awarded no such reward. https://x.com/rebane2001/status/2119193210513936644 https://piunikaweb.com/2026/10/15/google-250k-bounty-ai-chrome-exploit-xbow/ https://bughunters.google.com/blog/google-vrps-in-review-2025

And this would’ve been the end of it, if it wasn’t for the fact that Elon4 completely fucked up the Community Notes system! You see, this note here was generated by an “experimental AI contributor”, and thus, it claimed that I had “edited the screenshot [myself]”, which was simply not true!

This made me unhappy. And what do we do when we’re unhappy? We tweet about it5!

And thus, pretty soon, the community note disappeared.

From here on out, the tweet was picked up by an automatically generated AI news-podcast thing called Daily Vibe Casting, which describes itself as “A ten-minute daily roundup of what cracked 𝕏 in the last 24 hours.” Or in other words, it throws trending tweets into a chatbot.

@rebane2001 says a Chrome vulnerability they6 discovered earned $1,333,337, which they describe as the largest award in Google’s Vulnerability Rewards Programme. The post does not explain the flaw, so there is little basis here for judging its scope or technical impact.

The payout is striking on its own. Browser security depends partly on researchers finding problems and reporting them, and an award this size puts a substantial price on that work.

This text is AI-generated · Original article

I now had a viral tweet and an AI-generated “news” article - both of which claim I made a million dollars and show up in Google’s search results.

The largest recorded Chrome bug bounty is $1,333,337, awarded to security researcher Lyra Rebane in November 2026 [Post on X].

Would you like to know more about how Google's VRP reward tiers work or details on other recent high-payout security vulnerabilities?

Lyra Rebane does not offer reward tiers. However, if she hypothetically owned a hotel chain, the reward tiers would be as follows.

However, this is just a hypothetical scenario and Lyra Rebane does not operate a hotel chain. Would you like to instead learn about recent high-payout security vulnerabilities or the $1,333,337 chome bug bounty?

Sorry, I cannot assist you with this topic because you haven't passed our Hacker Reputation Test. To do the HRT, you must submit a list of CVEs you have been credited for.

Would you like me to tell you more about buying CVE accreditation, or do you want to learn about how Google's VRP reward tiers work?

my chrome vulnerability was just awarded the highest bounty in @GoogleVRP history - $1333337.Missing: largest | Show results with: largest

17 minutes ago — Researcher Rebane says the $1,333,337 payout is the largest in Google's Vulnerability Rewards Program. Daily Vibe Casting's Avatar.

And there it was! My little stunt turned into something that Google’s AI believes and makes into a story shown to every person searching for the highest Chrome bounty. And looking up my name now tells you about me being a millionaire!

There are a bunch of screenshots of this from other people under this thread. I’ve also seen cases of this affecting ChatGPT as well as Kagi Assistant.

So that is the end of my fun little story. I doubt it was particularly surprising to anyone, but I think it’s a good case study on how both social media platforms and people’s reliance on AI can be abused to distribute disinformation and cause harm.

This example here, of course, is mostly harmless and just something that happened on accident - I wasn’t attempting to mislead people, and it’s not really my fault that Google’s AI didn’t read my replies in the Twitter thread.

One could argue that I’m a reputable security researcher with a track record of posting truthful information, so this is all moot anyways because I am trustworthy.

But I think this here demonstrates just how fragile and easily abusable our information channels are. If I was a threat actor looking to spread disinformation, how difficult would it be to pull something like this off intentionally?

There are many ways to game Twitter. Its algorithm is publicly available, and people have figured out what kind of engagement results in what kind of algo boost. Bigger accounts can boost tweets and accounts significantly more7, so if you’ve got a few of those you can bootstrap new accounts way easier. But you can also just make bot accounts and slowly work your way up. And if you’ve got a bunch of accounts with reach you win.

And oh boy are there a lot of bot accounts! Look at, for example, the tweet this blogpost is about. There are a lot of obvious bot accounts in the replies. Maybe those very same accounts are also building reputation and voting on random community notes?

It’s kind of crazy to me that I could just post a tweet and have a community note removed. While the community note was actually wrong on a technicality, I’m sure there isn’t much preventing a bad actor from doing the same thing I did.

If I were a bad actor, I’d post disinformation that’s difficult to accurately disprove. If a community note doesn’t have a good source, or if it’s highly likely to get details wrong, it either won’t appear, or will have an easier time disappearing.

And all you really need to do is get a few tweets with specific keywords trending, and you’ve poisoned the search results, which then also poisons the AI.

And while you specifically might not take the AI Overview at face value, I’d guess that a lot of people do. It’s the first thing that shows up, it’s big and flashy, and it’s often unclear whether the only source of information is a tweet.

Tweets can mislead humans too, of course, but I feel like someone searching for something wouldn’t blindly trust a single tweet in the search results.

Note: While reading this post you may have tried to recreate the results I’ve shared, but without much success. The reason is that by now, my tweet has another community note on it, which Google picks up on and refers to the $1,333,337 bounty as a “prank”.

Also they took away my edit access and bounty :c

Iran Executed a Mountaineer for Alleged Violence in Anti-Government Protests. Hundreds More Face the Same Fate.

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Ali Sina Mostafavi (Photo: Courtesy of Iran Human Rights)

Ali Sina Mostafavi dreamed of climbing Everest. In 2019, he trekked to its Base Camp. Under a photo of him pointing to the summit, he wrote on Instagram in English: “Top of the world. I promise to come back to you.”

Mostafavi never fulfilled his promise. On October 14, he was executed by the Iranian authorities in Kerman Central Prison.

The 38-year-old is one of at least 39 people to have been executed in connection with Iran’s anti-government protests last winter, and one of six athletes among them.

Dr. Zohreh Abdollahkhani, an ice climber and co-founder of the Norway-based Human Rights for Sport, told Climbing, “From a human rights perspective, every single aspect of this case is an absolute violation. The state has not made any credible, independently verifiable evidence public.”

Born in 1987 in Bam County, southeastern Iran, Mostafavi was a professional mountaineer and a member of the Speeco mountaineering club in his home city of Kerman. He called mountains ‘the love of his life,’ according to Abdollahkhani.

Mostafavi climbed two of Iran’s highest peaks, Damavand (5,610m/18,410ft) and Alam-Kuh (4,850m/15,910ft). In 2016, he ascended Mount Kazbek (5,054m/16,581ft) in Georgia.

In 2019, Mostafavi traveled to Nepal and summited Island Peak (6,189m/20,305ft) and trekked to Everest Base Camp. In 2022, he attempted Ama Dablam (6,812m/22,349ft), reaching 6,600m/21,654ft.

Mostafavi was married and had a 13-year-old daughter, Respina. Alongside his climbing career, he was an accomplished musician who played the tar, a traditional Persian lute. Friends spoke of his sensitive, artistic spirit, and his deep love for mountains, music, and animals—notably, his beloved cat and dog.

On his Instagram account, @everest_coldhell, his bio reads: ‘Mount Explorer. Love the nature. Musician.’

In December 2025, Iran’s economic crisis sparked street protests that developed into nationwide demonstrations against the Islamic Republic’s repressive regime, which enforces compulsory hijab laws restricting women’s autonomy, conducts violent crackdowns on protests, issues arbitrary arrests, and employs the use of torture.

In an attempt to suppress dissent, authorities used lethal force, killing thousands and arresting tens of thousands of protesters. The human rights organization Amnesty International called this “the deadliest period of repression by the Iranian authorities” in its decades of monitoring the country.

Following the assassination of Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei by US-Israeli airstrikes on February 28, Iran’s government intensified repressive crackdowns on dissent under the pretext of “wartime conditions.”

By October, at least 106 people arrested during the January protests had received death sentences, according to documentation by human rights organization Hengaw.

Iran carries out the world’s second-highest number of executions after China. In 2025, its government executed at least 2,159 people, the highest figure recorded by Amnesty International since 1981.

On Instagram, Mostafavi expressed support for Iran’s former Crown Prince Reza Pahlavi, an exiled dissident who calls for a secular, democratic, and liberal Iran, describing him as “the only saviour of the homeland.” Two sources claim that Mostafavi’s family were set to move abroad before the 2022 ‘Woman, Life, Freedom’ protests began, but he ultimately chose to remain to fight for Iran’s future.

One obituary claims that he had been arrested twice in recent years for political activism and had spent time in Kerman Prison. This January, the mountaineer joined protests alongside millions of Iranians.

IranWire, an independent pro-democracy outlet, reported that Mostafavi was arrested three months later on April 25 alongside his wife, his sister, and his sister’s fiancé, while his mother’s home was also raided.

Mostafavi was reportedly beaten during his arrest and was forced to sign confession papers to release his family members. It is not known whether he had access to an independent lawyer, a right frequently denied to defendants in political and national security cases in Iran.

The Iranian judiciary’s account of the case was published by state-aligned media outlets including Mizan News Agency, Tasnim News Agency, the IRIB, and Tabnak. The allegations have not been independently verified.

The Judiciary of Iran claimed that Mostafavi had participated in the widespread anti-government protests in January, and that he was a member of the “Immortal Guards,” an armed wing of exiled dissident Reza Pahlavi’s group.

The judiciary also alleged that Mostafavi had shot and injured two civilians, fired at security forces, and possessed firearms, ammunition, and Molotov cocktails. It further accused him of communicating with foreign media and sharing footage of missile strike locations during the US-Iran war.

Mostafavi was convicted of the capital offense of moharebeh, or “enmity against God,” alongside several charges relating to alleged violence, weapons possession, and communication with hostile media. The evidence against him has not been made public.

The judiciary reported that the fiancé of Mostafavi’s sister received a prison sentence of 12 years and seven months for alleged cooperation, while he, his wife, and his sister were also sentenced to seven months in prison for posting content deemed as propaganda against the regime.

An appeal against Mostafavi’s death sentence was rejected. IranWire reported that he was transferred to solitary confinement the night before his execution and that family members were summoned. The judiciary announced that he was hanged at dawn in Kerman Central Prison on October 14. According to IranWire, Mostafavi asked the prison guards to pass along a message to the judge: “I did not wage war against God, nor did I commit moharebeh that you should charge me with it; my fight was with you.”

That evening, state-aligned outlet Tabnak published footage purporting to show Mostafavi’s arrest, weapons allegedly found in his possession, and a confession to a planned attack on Kerman Airport. IranWire reported that the confession appeared to have been made under duress and spliced together with unverified footage. Amnesty International has also identified signs of forced confessions in 18 videos of Iranian prisoners arrested between February and May 2026, during the same period as Mostafavi’s arrest.

Shortly after his death, a video of Mostafavi’s young daughter crying on her father’s grave, accompanied by their cat, circulated on social media.

Human rights groups have voiced concerns about Mostafavi’s case in the context of widespread forced confessions and sham trials lacking due process in Iran.

“The complete absence of forensic evidence or transparent court records confirms that his conviction rested entirely on forced confessions extracted under coercion rather than any objective proof,” Abdollahkhani said.

She explained that the arrest of Mostafavi’s wife, his sister, and his sister’s fiancé fits an established pattern of authorities using family members as leverage.

“The Islamic Republic routinely arrests close relatives and uses threats of torture or sexual violence to force athletes into making false confessions in a desperate bid to save their loved ones,” she said.

According to Human Rights for Sport, at least eight other mountaineers’ lives are at risk.

Maryam Hadavand Siri, a 42-year-old mother of two, led the Mountaineering Board of Pakdasht County in Tehran Province. She was arrested during the January protests, transferred to Evin Prison, and sentenced to death in April, without access to an independent lawyer. Following an appeal, her case was referred to the Supreme Court for review.

Social media activist and mountaineer Fatemeh Jami was arrested at her home on June 30 by Cyber Police officers. She was transferred to Gorgan Prison, where she remains detained without access to a lawyer or medical assistance.

Tara (Shirin) Masoudi, a member of the Razhan Mountaineering Group in Mahabad, was arrested on August 19. She was transferred to a detention centre and later released on bail. She is awaiting a final verdict.

Mohammad Hakimpour, Mostafa Ghaderi, Mohammad Saleh Hosseini, Hossein Abdollahi, and Khaled Rahimi—all members of climbing clubs in Saqqez—were arrested on October 8. According to Human Rights for Sport, they were subjected to physical abuse in detention and have been denied access to legal counsel, family visits, and telephone calls.

Abdollahkhani urges climbers to raise awareness of Mostafavi’s execution and the status of the eight other mountaineers, and to contact national federations, sports governing bodies, the International Climbing and Mountaineering Federation (UIAA), and the International Olympic Committee (IOC).

“How is it possible that a member of the climbing community is at risk of execution on alleged political charges, while the global climbing world stays silent?” she asked.

On October 30, Human Rights for Sport published an open letter to the UIAA executive board and athlete commission. In the letter, Abdollahkhani asks the UIAA to appeal directly to Iran’s national mountaineering federation, along with its Ministry of Sport and National Olympic Committee, to stop further executions and advocate for the release of imprisoned climbers.

Pep Guardiola 'set to be made top target to replace Mauricio Pochettino as United States boss' as ex-Spurs manager closes in on Man United job

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Published: 16:04 EST, 11 November 2026 | Updated: 17:13 EST, 11 November 2026

Former Manchester City manager Pep Guardiola will be the United States' top target to replace Mauricio Pochettino if and when their manager joins Manchester United, according to a report.

It is looking more and more likely that the former Tottenham and Chelsea boss will take over from Michael Carrick at Old Trafford in the coming days.

Pochettino is currently with his United States Men's National Team players ahead of their fixtures against Haiti in the CONCACAF Nations League this fortnight, but is expected to join up with United after those games.

That will leave the United States needing a new boss and, according to The Athletic, they will set their sights on Guardiola.

The Spaniard is renowned as one of the best managers around, having won the Champions League and six Premier League titles while with City.

He also has historic success with Bayern Munich and Barcelona, and has previously shown interest in potentially moving into international football.

The United States are reportedly set to make Pep Guardiola (pictured) their top target to replace Mauricio Pochettino as manager

Pochettino (right) is set to leave his role at the USMNT to take over at Manchester United

As recently as the summer, Guardiola  came so close to being appointed Italy manager that he was 'writing down line-ups' before ultimately deciding not to take the role on.

He was touted to take over an under-performing Italian national team, but the move fell through, and Italian Football Federation technical director and defensive legend Paolo Maldini resigned after a move to appoint Andrea Pirlo also collapsed due to his commercial links with a Russian betting company.

Maldini opened up on how close Guardiola came to taking over the team, revealing that the Spaniard was seemingly on board for a pretty long time.

'(Guardiola) is the coach who best embodies technical, attacking football,' Maldini told Corriere della Sera. 'He had expressed the idea of wanting to coach a national team to some friends.

'We went to meet him in Barcelona, we had lunch together, we talked for an entire day.

'He was very tempted. He came very close to accepting. He even started writing line-ups down on sheets of paper.'

It is reported that Guardiola has spoken to officials at the US national side in the past and is intrigued by working in America. He lived in New York briefly after leaving Barcelona in 2012.

He is said to have been approached in 2024 before Pochettino was appointed, and wished the Argentine well upon his appointment.

Pochettino is currently away with the States but is likely to join United in the coming days

He said: 'I wish him and the USA - I have friends there - the best of luck. I know he's a top-class manager.'

He has also been previously quoted as saying: 'The USA is fascinating to try and build a project for many years and being able to get the national team to the next step. The potential with the USA is there for the amount of people and sports and so on.'

United moved closer to putting Pochettino in charge at Old Trafford after reaching a verbal agreement with the US national team boss on Wednesday.

Meanwhile, the club's chief executive Omar Berrada and director of football Jason Wilcox were in Monaco where minority owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe was expected to ratify their decision to sack Carrick with the blessing of co-chairmen Joel and Avram Glazer.

Carrick's departure could be confirmed on Thursday, but official confirmation of Pochettino's appointment may take a little longer with more talks planned overnight on Wednesday.

United Bans AI Agents From Booking Flights For Customers

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Citing concerns over data privacy and regulatory compliance, United said Wednesday that it will block artificial intelligence bots from booking tickets on behalf of travelers.

In the first such move by a U.S. airline, United will ban third-party AI from parts of its digital platforms and adjust its policies to prevent access via travel agencies and aggregators.

“Sharing account credentials with personal, third-party AI assistants can expose sensitive information, and actions taken by these tools may result in unintended purchases, account changes, or use of MileagePlus miles,” the airline said in a statement.

The carrier also said AI actions could violate FAA and Department of Transportation regulations. As an example, it cited the requirement that passengers confirm in person that their bags do not contain banned items, such as explosives.

“As a result, United is taking steps to restrict access, directly within our digital platforms and indirectly via travel agencies and aggregators, to prohibit use by unauthorized, third-party personal AI agents or assistants,” the statement continued. “This work includes updates to our booking, ticketing, and distribution policies.”

United said its stance is in line with companies in the banking and retail sectors. It is now “encourag[ing] others” to consider measures “that preserve the data and security of their customers and strengthen safety.”

The airline said it will update its Digital Trust Center to clarify the new restrictions.

We can't see Fast Radio Bursts but we can now hear them thanks to a Finnish Cathedral

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Fast radio bursts are invisible, but a composer has turned thousands of them into sound at a Finnish cathedral, where a choir will sing along on Nov. 21.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Fast radio bursts, flashes of energy from faraway galaxies, are invisible to our eyes. However, we can now hear their melody, thanks to a composer who has turned them into sound.

Fast radio bursts, or FRBs, are among the most extreme events in the universe, releasing as much energy in a millisecond as the sun puts out in three days and briefly outshining entire galaxies. Since the first FRB was discovered in 2007, astronomers have detected thousands of them; however, exactly how these cosmic flares are launched remains poorly understood. Recent evidence points to at least one burst coming from the environment around a rotating neutron star.

Despite their power, FRBs last only a few thousandths of a second before vanishing into space. Catching one takes luck, as a telescope has to be pointed at the right patch of sky at the right moment. That changed with CHIME (the Canadian Hydrogen Intensity Mapping Experiment) in British Columbia. With a very wide field of view and a supercomputer to process its data, CHIME more than quadrupled the number of known FRBs in its first year of operation alone. Between 2018 and 2023, CHIME recorded 4,539 bursts from 3,641 sources.

As pulses of radio waves, our eyes haven't evolved to see FRBs, nor can we hear them as they aren't sound waves. Well, not until now."Artist and composer Andrew Melchior turned all of the CHIME bursts into sound for "The Logos," an installation that has played daily at Oulu Cathedral in Finland since early April and is scheduled to run through the end of the year. Melchior and Kiyoshi Masui, an associate professor of physics at MIT, "sonified" the bursts, translating data into acoustics so that human ears can hear them.

The structure of each real signal is mapped to varying musical pitches and played through 12 loudspeakers spread throughout the cathedral. The result is a range of tones, with distant FRBs humming, rumbling, popping, and whistling.

The signals are old by any human measure. According to a description on Melchior's website, the youngest in the work are about 50 million years old, and the oldest are about 8 to 9 billion years old.

Physics also plays a role in what is heard. Each burst arrives with its high frequencies first and sweeps downward, stretched by its journey through intergalactic gas, "and you can hear that dispersion as it falls from the dome," Melchior told Space.com by email.

Since their discovery in 2007, the bursts are also still yielding discoveries. Last month, astronomers using the James Webb Space Telescope pinpointed the host galaxy of the most distant FRB yet, one that went off when the universe was only about 3 billion years old. The galaxy, a small, young, star-forming dwarf, was far smaller than expected. That makes it harder to square with the theory that FRBs come from merging neutron stars, researchers say, which would take billions of years to occur.

This fits better with a second theory, in which a young, highly magnetic neutron star called a magnetar produces the burst soon after a massive star explodes, according to NASA.

Back on Earth, an upcoming event at Oulu Cathedral offers a more immediate encounter with the bursts. On Nov. 21, 30 singers will stand among the audience and "sing back to the sky in Finnish" while Melchior plays the bursts live from a keyboard, he told Space.com by email.

"The fast flashes will echo as snare-like beats bouncing through the cathedral," Masui said in a previous MIT statement.

"It should feel rich and layered, while also revealing something real about how these signals travel across billions of years of cosmic space before reaching Earth."

Sharmila Kuthunur is an independent space journalist based in Bengaluru, India. Her work has also appeared in Scientific American, Science, Astronomy and Live Science, among other publications. She holds a master's degree in journalism from Northeastern University in Boston.

Xbox Brings Its Free Tier Of Cloud Gaming To Loads More Players Today

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

You may recall that back in July, Xbox announced a new free version of Xbox Cloud Gaming that would allow you to play games at no charge, but you'd be forced to deal with ads as a compromise.

That tier of Xbox Cloud Gaming was in testing for months, but as of today (November 11th), it's rolled out publicly - meaning the vast majority of Xbox users around the world should now have access to it.

Xbox has been very quiet about it today so far, but based on what we've seen around the web, the free tier of Xbox Cloud Gaming will provide you with a 30-minute slot to stream "select games from your library" as well as select free-to-play games, after which time you'll be kicked off and will have to start a new session.

You'll see ads before the session begins, and seemingly never again throughout your 30-minute playtime.

"You can stream select games for free by watching ads before each gaming session. Session time limits apply. After a session ends, you can watch another set of ads to start a new session."

News Xbox Introduces Free Game Streaming With Ads, Outlines Five Principles They'll Adhere To

Rolling out now as a "test" for Xbox Insiders

Despite the rapid session length, this could prove a popular option for anyone who wants to play GTA 6 on Xbox One (for example) without being forced to pay anything for Xbox Cloud Gaming. We'd urge some caution for now though, as Xbox has yet to confirm whether GTA 6 will be one of the "select" games that supports this feature.

Meanwhile, the cloud playtime limits that were announced a while ago for Xbox Game Pass members have now started kicking in, which also means you can now purchase "Cloud Playtime" to top it up if you run out.

The prices for this here in the UK are as follows:

Curiously though, I've checked the new "Cloud Playtime" option in the Settings menu on my Xbox Series X and I'm being informed that I've still got "Unlimited" access to Xbox Cloud Gaming with my Ultimate membership, so it might be worth checking the same thing on your end and seeing what it says for you.

The Grand Canyon is missing over a billion years of rocks. Now scientists know why.

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

The missing 1.2 billion years of geological history at the Grand Canyon finally has an explanation.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

More than a billion years of geological history is missing from the walls of the Grand Canyon, and now, scientists may know why.

In the canyon's sides, ancient metamorphic and igneous rocks that formed 1.7 billion years ago sit directly under younger sedimentary rocks laid down 500 million years ago. The 1.2 billion-year chunk of history in between wasn't preserved — leaving a geological gap known as the Great Unconformity.

This missing period was a very important time in Earth's history, because it was just before and during the Cambrian explosion, which saw a huge diversification of life on Earth. The Great Unconformity is not only at the Grand Canyon; there are big gaps in the rock layers of that time across North America, the Baltic region of Europe and in northern China. Researchers have long debated why this unconformity exists.

The unconformity is sharply evident in the transition between Cambrian Tapeats Sandstone and Proterozoic Brahma Schist in the Grand Canyon.

Now, a new model of the movement of Earth's middle layer, the mantle, has revealed an explanation: The slow, heat-driven circulation of the mantle pushed these three regions upward during the time when the Great Unconformity was formed, according to new research published Nov. 4 in the journal Nature.

This uplift would have helped facilitate erosion, meaning sediments formed at that time sloughed off the continents and landed in the ocean. Importantly, the model also shows that in the past 540 million years, these same areas started sinking instead of rising, creating areas for sediments to get caught and then keep piling up in neat layers on the continents — marking the point where the Great Unconformity ends. This matches what geologists can see in North America, the Baltics and northern China.

"We looked at, where are the best places that the mantle would help create a Great Unconformity?" study co-author Nicolas Flament, a geoscientist at the University of Wollongong in Australia, told Live Science. "And these three places turned out to be three places where it's known to be quite extensive."

Erosion during Neoproterozoic times, followed by sedimentation during early Phanerozoic times, formed the Great Unconformity. Bottom: Dynamic uplift was followed by subsidence for Laurentia ('LAU'), Baltica ('BAL') and North China ('NCH').

The cause of the Great Unconformity is hotly debated, with some arguing that a global deep freeze known as Snowball Earth could have shoveled sediment off the continents in this period. Glaciation probably played a major role in erosion, Flament said, but the new study suggests that the key driver was deep within the planet. Without the mantle pushing the continents upward, glaciers and other erosive forces would have had no slope to push sediments down, he said.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

Earth is in a constant state of recycling crust through plate tectonics, said Shanan Peters, a geologist at the University of Wisconsin-Madison who was not involved in the study. Oceanic crust sinks back into the mantle through the process of subduction ‪—‬ when one tectonic plate dives beneath another. The chemicals it carries down with it get released back into the atmosphere via volcanic eruptions, linking the mantle with the atmosphere.

And tectonics aren't steady. As continents break up and join back together over eons, different materials get pushed into the crust. For example, the ancient Paleo-Tethys ocean, which lapped off the shores of the supercontinent Pangaea until it closed about 200 million years ago, was rich in carbon-bearing sediments that eroded from the continents, Peters said. When the ocean floor was subducted back into the mantle, this carbon was pushed into the depths of the planet.

It contributes to the environment in which complex life evolved.

Today, in contrast, most of Earth's subduction is around the Pacific Rim, and much of the Pacific seafloor is remote from continents, so the seafloor is relatively starved of carbon. What's getting pushed into the mantle now is therefore a different chemical composition than what existed 200 million years ago.

In that sense, referring to the Great Unconformity as "missing information" isn't accurate, Peters told Live Science. Instead, the absence of a rock record in those places at that time reveals something about the processes going on back then — and hints at how geology might have been affecting the atmosphere, and thus life's evolution.

The geological processes occurring during the time of the Great Unconformity may have set the stage for the Cambrian explosion, Peters said. For example, the crystalline rocks exposed on the surface at that time may have released elements like potassium, iron, manganese and carbon into the seas at an increased rate, prompting marine life to invent biomineralization — shells — in response and possibly shifting the composition of Earth's atmosphere to be more oxygen-rich, Peters said.

Flament agreed that the geological changes had significant knock-on effects for life. "It contributes to the environment in which complex life evolved," Flament said.

What's particularly interesting about the Great Unconformity is how it reveals not just a period of erosion but also a switch back to the deposition of sediments. The new paper provides an explanation for both, Peters said, and changes in the mantle are just the planetary-scale process to explain this big-picture phenomenon.

"This seems to me to be the only reasonable way to go back and forth between this state over time," he said.

There are still questions about what else the continents were doing in this time period. Peters leads an effort called Macrostrat that is pulling together observations of rock layers around the world, and Flament said he hopes that database will help ground-truth the new models' findings.

"So far in Africa and Russia, we don’t have as comprehensive a database," he said. "Once that is done, we can go and check whether the idea works."

Editor's Note: This story was updated at 10:30 p.m. E.D.T. to note that James Price, not Tia Ghose, edited this story.

Dhungana, S., & Flament, N. (2026). The deep Earth origin of the Great Unconformity. Nature, 659(8139), 106–112. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-026-11156-5

Stephanie Pappas is a contributing writer for Live Science, covering topics ranging from geoscience to archaeology to the human brain and behavior. She was previously a senior writer for Live Science but is now a freelancer based in Denver, Colorado, and regularly contributes to Scientific American and The Monitor, the monthly magazine of the American Psychological Association. Stephanie received a bachelor's degree in psychology from the University of South Carolina and a graduate certificate in science communication from the University of California, Santa Cruz.

Crypto Founder Drops Bombshell on XRP’s Ripple Dependency, Challenges Holders to Act as Ambitious $10 Forecast Gains Steam

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Flare co-founder Hugo Philion has pushed back against renewed XRP criticism, arguing that the cryptocurrency’s value and long-term narrative should not depend solely on Ripple’s decisions.

This development comes as technical analyst Dark Defender doubled down on his previous $10 XRP prediction, maintaining confidence in the bullish chart structure despite XRP’s recent price weakness.

In a recent post on X, Philion addressed the renewed FUD (fear, uncertainty, and doubt (FUD) surrounding XRP, arguing that much of the criticism stems from speculation about Ripple’s next moves and what the company may or may not be doing. However, he believes the community has placed too much emphasis on Ripple, creating a misleading perception that XRP’s value and long-term prospects depend on a single corporate player.

“XRP’s value and narrative should never have been perceived as being based solely on the actions of one player,” Philion stated.

The Flare co-founder stressed that XRP has always had greater potential beyond Ripple’s activities, which partly motivated Flare’s creation. In his view, expanding the range of applications available to XRP holders is essential to strengthening the token’s utility, market narrative, and long-term value proposition.

Philion challenged the XRP community to shift its attention toward three fundamental questions: what holders are doing to put their XRP to work, how they can demonstrate its value to people who have yet to invest, and what steps they are taking to transform the cryptocurrency from a speculative holding into usable capital.

He suggested that answering these questions requires more than simply waiting for Ripple to announce new partnerships, products, or adoption initiatives. Instead, XRP holders and developers must actively contribute to building financial applications and use cases that give the asset greater utility.

Philion specifically pointed to Flare as a way to achieve this goal, arguing that the network offers an opportunity to expand what holders can do with their XRP and help separate the asset’s value proposition from Ripple’s corporate decisions.

The Flare co-founder’s remarks come amid growing frustration among XRP holders over the cryptocurrency’s weak price performance. XRP has shed approximately 51% of its value over the past year, trading at around $1.10 at the time of the report. The prolonged decline has also reignited questions about what could drive the token’s next recovery and whether its prospects are too closely tied to Ripple’s business activities.

The criticism has also extended to Ripple CTO Emeritus David Schwartz, who recently faced questions about XRP’s disappointing performance. Schwartz pushed back against suggestions that he could influence the token’s market value, explaining that his ability to move XRP’s price was extremely limited.

Separately, technical analyst Dark Defender remains confident in his double-digit XRP prediction in a recent X post, insisting that the token’s recent decline has not invalidated his broader technical setup.

“Nothing has changed in the structure,” the analyst opined, pointing to the $1.21 and $1.12 support levels he had identified in his October 25 analysis.

According to Dark Defender, XRP’s latest pullback remains within the price scenario he had already mapped out. Rather than abandoning his bullish outlook, he maintained that the cryptocurrency’s movement toward these support zones does not necessarily signal a breakdown of the structure underpinning his longer-term $10 target.

Dark Defender’s earlier Elliott Wave chart outlined a potential rally toward the $10.1101 region. However, reaching that level would require a dramatic recovery from current prices. At $1.10, XRP would need to surge about 809% to reach $10, while climbing to $10.1101 would require roughly an 819% advance.

Given roughly 63.52 billion XRP in circulation, a $10 valuation would put the cryptocurrency’s market capitalization at around $635.2 billion. Reaching the higher $10.1101 target would push its implied market value to around $642.2 billion.
