Emideltide Keeps Getting a ‘No’ for Compounding. What’s the Problem?

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Why the government’s renewed interest in peptides excludes this one.

THE TRUMP ADMINISTRATION has not been shy about its pro-peptide stance. US Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has pushed to make six popular yet unapproved peptides available for compounding to people with a prescription. A recent FDA advisory committee meeting voted in favor of adding six out of seven peptides to a list of substances that U.S. compounding pharmacies are allowed to manufacture and supply.

Six out of seven. So what happened to that last peptide? Emideltide, also dubbed DSIP, is a peptide people have turned to for better sleep. And it was the only peptide the FDA advisory board voted against moving out of the Category 2 list—a substance considered to carry significant safety risks, with compounding pharmacies prohibited from making it. Here’s why.

EMIDELTIDE IS A nine-amino-acid peptide originally isolated from the blood of sleeping rabbits during electrically induced sleep in the late 1970s. Since its discovery, a synthetic peptide version has been administered via injection or a nasal spray.

Emideltide reportedly helps you fall asleep faster and extend the time spent in deep sleep. It’s also been used to avoid waking up multiple times throughout the night and anecdotal reports online have mentioned more vivid, Inception–like lucid dreaming. With better sleep, people online using emideltide have mentioned feeling less stress, more refreshed upon waking, and faster recovery after a grueling workout.

MOST RESEARCH LOOKING into the peptide’s role in sleep, however, took place in the 1980s and 1990s—with mixed findings.

For example, a 1984 case study reported on a 35-year-old guy with 10 years of narcolepsy who had reduced sleep attacks, less daytime napping, and more alertness with weekly emideltide injections administered in the morning.

One clinical trial had 16 people take an emideltide injection or a placebo while measuring how long it took for them to fall asleep once they’re tucked into bed. People who had an emideltide injection said they felt more tired and actually went to sleep quicker than the placebo group. That should be great except when they repeated the experiment a second night, the placebo group knocked out quicker than the peptide group.

Another study in 1992 found the peptide helped chronic insomniacs feel less tired. However, there was such little difference in the time it took to fall asleep and stay asleep in the emideltide group versus the placebo group. The authors of the 1992 study said any small improvements with the peptide were probably “due to an incidental change in the placebo group” and “not likely to be of major therapeutic benefit.”

BEYOND SLEEP, EMIDELTIDE has been explored in animal studies for potentially protecting against cancer and improving motor ability after stroke. Another realm of interest is how emideltide might help with curbing withdrawal after addiction.

A 1984 study found emideltide injections helped reduce the severity of opioid withdrawal symptoms in 97 percent of people with addiction along with alcohol withdrawal symptoms in 87 percent of people with alcohol addiction. Although more peptide injections were needed in people with opiate addiction.

In 1998, researchers gave a single injection of emideltide to seven people with opioid dependence. While they reported improvements in opioid withdrawal, the team also noted “it remains an open question whether DSIP could be an effective alternative detoxification approach.” Some of the issues were the small sample size, lack of a placebo group for proper comparison, and that each person knew they were getting an emideltide injection. The lack of randomization makes it hard to say for sure that a treatment actually worked or if people subjectively felt better because they knew they were getting treatment.

WE KNOW EMIDELTIDE doesn’t act like a sedative that slows down the brain and body’s function. But how the peptide exactly works is still up for debate.

The peptide is often considered a sleep-regulator rather than a suppressant. We know emideltide doesn’t act like a sedative or sleeping pill that slows down the brain and body’s function. But how the peptide exactly works is still up for debate.

One possible mechanism is that the peptide increases delta-wave activity. These are your slowest brain waves at 0.5 to 4 Hz that are indicative of deep sleep. Deep sleep is linked to better memory, energy, and all that good stuff you get from a good night’s sleep. There may also be some neuroendocrine effects going on that could explain why people feel calm enough to pass out. A small study back in the ’80s injected a single dose of emideltide in 11 guys and found lower levels of ACTH, a hormone that stimulates your adrenal glands to release the stress hormone cortisol (though this mechanism has been disputed).

The FDA advisory meeting relied on studies that focused on emideltide’s cross-talk with the opioid system in the brain. While the peptide doesn’t directly interact with opioid receptors, evidence points to emideltide somehow being able to trigger the release of endorphins. This may also provide painkiller properties (which is why it’s been proposed as a way to suppress alcohol and opioid withdrawal syndrome).

EMIDELTIDE WILL PROBABLY stay banned—and found only through the sketchy gray market—because there’s not enough information to know if emideltide might do more harm than good.

Since emideltide interacts with the opioid system, there is a possibility that it could lead to addiction. Right now, there are no animal or human studies that prove emideltide wouldn’t worsen addictive behaviors.

There’s also not enough data on whether repeated emideltide use would cause other safety issues. While the small sleep trials did not report any serious side effects, withdrawal studies noted headaches, nausea, and vertigo. After the second injection, some had low blood pressure although the researchers also said it’s unclear whether those symptoms were because of the peptide or from withdrawal altogether.

Jocelyn Solis-Moreira, MS is the associate health & fitness for Men's Health and has previously written for CNN, Scientific American, Popular Science, and National Geographic before joining the brand. When she's not working, she's doing circus arts or working towards the perfect pull-up.

Over One-Third Of Audi Nuvolari Production Will Be Sold In The U.S.

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

The 2027 Audi Nuvolari isn't a successor to the R8 because whereas that supercar was mass-produced in the tens of thousands over 18 years and two generations, just 499 Nuvolaris will ever be made. Audi has now announced that 182 of those are earmarked for the U.S., which means 36.5% of all Nuvolaris will land stateside, or one in every 2.74 cars.

Each will go for an official starting price of $700,000 before destination or taxes, and U.S. deliveries are scheduled to start in the first half of 2028. That's almost twice as much money as the Lamborghini Temerario on which it's based, but the Lambo isn't limited production or quite as special. Despite the numbered production run and how this sort of car tends to sell out before the public even knows about it, Audi of America says there are still build slots available, as "orders continue to be accepted at authorized Audi dealerships throughout the United States for a limited time."

Sharing its powertrain with the Temerario, the Audi Nuvolari uses a twin-turbo 4.0-liter V8 and three axial-flux hybrid electric motors to make 987 horsepower combined. 0 to 60 mph happens in just 2.5 seconds, and its top speed stands at 217 mph. It's said to use Formula 1-derived tech — Audi is in F1 now, in case you forgot — in the hybrid energy management system, by-wire brakes, active aero, and launch control programming.

It's also a new luxury performance car that manages to look good and original without being "polarizing." It's brutalist and a departure from existing Audi styling, but most colleagues I've talked to about it view its design favorably. For what it's worth, I think it's hella cool, particularly from the rear three-quarter view. Audi says there are 50 gloss paint colors and over 10 matte finishes, but you can of course make it look however you want with Audi Exclusive. You can get Audi's first exposed carbon-fiber body on the Nuvolari, and on the inside there's dozens more options.

In any case, if you're in the States and have 700 grand to splash on a German supercar (or, let's be real, almost a million after accounting for options, fees and taxes), there's apparently still time to go to your local Audi dealership and put your name down for a Nuvolari. You'd be joining a small club that only 181 other Americans will be a part of.

Bitcoin Remains Shaky at $75,000 After Failing to Hit $80,000—Here’s What Happened

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

According to Glassnode’s latest Weekly Market Pulse report, Bitcoin was trading around $75,000 on Monday after a turbulent week that saw the leading cryptocurrency briefly push above $80,000 before rapidly giving back those gains. The price closed more than 4% lower than the previous Sunday and marked its weakest finish since August.

The midweek rally above $80,000 reversed the very next day, and selling resumed over the weekend, with ETF investors playing a clear role in the pressure. Glassnode data shows the funds posted outflows on four of five trading days, delivering the heaviest weekly total since early July. ETF MVRV has now slipped below its lower band, indicating that the profit cushion for these holders is thinning.

Derivatives markets reflected caution rather than full capitulation, with perpetual futures traders sold aggressively into Thursday’s drop and again on Sunday as long liquidations rose.

Open interest, however, remains in the upper part of its range, and long positions continue to pay elevated funding rates. Options traders are still paying for protection, with the 25-delta skew above its high band, even as the volatility spread retreated into its normal range once realized moves caught up.

Spot buyers failed to step in meaningfully, and cumulative volume delta turned negative despite rising volume. Notably, on-chain activity told a similar story of repositioning, with transfer volumes and active addresses both climbing above their upper bands while fees increased, Glassnode noted.

Supply has continued to shift into newer hands, as the short-term-to-long-term holder supply ratio and the share of hot capital remain elevated. Short-term holders now sit near break-even, and realized losses clearly outweigh profits.

The combination of persistent ETF selling and fragile short-term holder positions is weighing on price. At the same time, some leverage has been flushed from the system, and recent momentum no longer strongly supports further downside. Traders are now actively watching to see whether the market can stabilize or if another leg lower is still ahead.

Justices see immigration contract dispute as raising the possibility of major limits on administrative power

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

Yesterday’s argument in Department of Labor v. Sun Valley Orchards showed a court open to the possibility of a major limitation on the ability of agencies to impose fines or penalties under internal adjudicative processes, at least without ready review in court.

The constitutional problem is how to distinguish between the judicial power that is allocated to the judicial branch (in Article III of the Constitution) and the executive power that is allocated to the executive branch (in Article I of the Constitution). The Supreme Court always has recognized that it is permissible for officials in the executive branch – who don’t have the protections (including life tenure) that Article III gives to federal judges – to resolve questions in a proceeding that looks an awful lot like the kind of trial you might see in a federal court. And it also on several occasions has invalidated schemes that seemed to the court to go too far in that direction. To pick the most famous example, the justices invalidated the initial version of the Bankruptcy Code of 1978 because it gave undue adjudicative responsibility to bankruptcy “judges” that are not protected by Article III. What the court has never done well is explain exactly how far such powers can go. Nevertheless, recent decisions like Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo and Trump v. Slaughter show a great willingness to rein in the powers traditionally granted to the administrative state.

This case presents a dispute as to whether employers under the H-2A visa program (for temporary foreign agricultural workers) gave those employees certain things they were promised before being admitted to the United States. Conventionally, when the Department of Labor decided that an employer had failed to treat workers as it promised pre-admission, it imposed fines or penalties in an administrative proceeding, often handing the funds over to the employees as compensation in the nature of back pay. The question for the justices is whether Article III of the Constitution can tolerate that dispute being resolved internally by DOL employees rather than by judges in a federal court.

The government sees the dispute as squarely involving two categories of so-called “public rights” for which Article I treatment is permitted: the immigration power and public benefits. The immigration power is involved because the workers were permitted to enter the country on the condition that the employer treat them as agreed; the public benefit power is involved because the right to enter is a benefit almost entirely within the discretion of the executive.

Several of the justices found that argument rather weak. Chief Justice John Roberts, for example, commented early on that the leading case about the immigration power (Oceanic Steam Navigation v Mellor) “had to do very much with entry, a condition of entry, and loathsome diseases. This strikes me as significantly different. … You’re trying to leverage an entry several weeks, months … in the past to the particular condition of work that doesn’t seem to have much to do with the actual entry.” For the chief justice, the government was trying to “leverage what is a significant barrier to entry, where you have a lot of deference to the executive branch,” and “trying to … extend that broad authority beyond the justification for its application.” He repeatedly emphasized that the specifics of the case “look more like a condition of employment rather than … entry.”

In a similar vein, Justice Amy Coney Barrett stated to Principal Deputy Solicitor General Sarah Harris (arguing for the government): “I think we agree that the aliens could be removed pursuant to our immigration tradition without an Article III court. But the fines, I’m wondering if they’re different. …. So I’m … wondering here why that wouldn’t be the case for the alien. If the alien violated some sort of condition on the H-2A visa, why wouldn’t the alien be entitled to an Article III court before the alien had to pay that kind of a fine?” At which point Justice Neil Gorsuch chimed in that he “thought [an earlier case had said that] confiscating property goes to an Article III court when you’re trying to confiscate property from aliens. … I thought we decided a long time ago that you’ve got to go to an Article III court. Are … you resisting that?”

Justice Brett Kavanaugh pointed to yet another precedent on civil penalties: “In Tull [v. United States], we said … and this is 40 years ago – a civil penalty was a type of remedy at common law that could only be enforced in courts of law. …. That’s a fairly definitive statement in our precedent. What … are we supposed to do with that?”

That’s not to say that all of the justices sided against the government. Justice Elena Kagan, for example, found the case well within the boundaries of existing doctrine. To Harris, she commented early on: “[I]sn’t there a condition of entry here? … [T]hey wouldn’t be admitted unless the employer had promised certain things.” To Robert Johnson arguing on behalf of the employer – a different Robert Johnson, not the blues singer – she was much more pointed:

Offering a parallel perspective, Ketanji Brown Jackson emphasized that the court in numerous contexts has approved administrative adjudication of civil penalties. She repeatedly challenged Johnson to reconcile his argument with those decisions.

But ominous for the government was some repeated questioning of Harris about “the best way for you to lose” (as Justice Sonia Sotomayor put it). From early on in the argument, even the justices who seem most likely to accept the constitutionality of the arrangement were trying to help Harris identify the least damaging way for the court to rule against her. At the beginning of her argument and throughout it, Harris emphasized to the justices that a broad ruling against her on the constitutional question would unsettle programs in a wide variety of areas, ranging from Medicare, to government contracts, to student loans, but that did not seem to make any headway for her.

It is not at all clear from the argument how exactly the justices are going to come down. Clearly a significant group are considering the idea that an award of civil penalties is too far removed from the entry of the employee to be protected by the “public rights” doctrine that would protect administrative determination of the right to enter. My guess is that even those justices don’t yet know how far they would be willing to go – or how far they can go and secure five votes of agreement. I expect this case to come down late and be sharply divided.

Ronald Mann is a professor of law at Columbia, where he teaches courses in commercial finance, payment systems and deals.

A Tesla Aftermarket Shop Wants to Build the Cybertruck Under License

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Unplugged Performance took to Tesla boss Elon Musk’s preferred social medium of choice, X, to make the offer.

The Tesla Cybertruck, as you may well have heard by now, is on death row. How the news hits you will likely depend on what your opinions on the truck that company CEO “Technoking” Elon Musk once described as “what Bladerunner would have driven.” Those who dislike the steel-bodied machine range from pleased to gloating; fans, meanwhile, are rending garments and clutching their vowel-less vanity plates. Alas, poor Cybertruck; I knew him, Elon!

But one group of Tesla enthusiasts are aiming to take matters into their own hands. Unplugged Performance, an aftermarket shop specializing in Teslas, has taken to social media to entreat the automaker not to kill off the slab-sided pickup, but rather, to license its production out to them instead.

The company took to X—which is owned by Musk, who is also, shall we say, a vocal user—to make the offer. “An open offer to Tesla: We fully support putting Cybercab first, and we’d be glad to take the Cybertruck line off your hands. We’d fund it with a consortium of investors and run it at no cost to Tesla’s floor space, headcount, or focus,” Unplugged Performance wrote.

That offer, unsurprisingly, evoked a reaction amongst Internet-active fans of the Cybertruck, as well as quite a few questions for specifics. To their credit, Unplugged Performance responded with a comprehensive amount of detail regarding the plan so far. “A fair question we’re hearing: can UP really run the Cybertruck vehicle line? Not alone, and not overnight,” the brand tweeted (X-ed?), before elaborating that it intends to round up investors and manufacturing partners in the months leading up to Tesla’s planned changeover of its Texas plant that will use the capacity currently occupied by the truck to build autonomous Cybercabs.

“We believe this is highly fundable because one production platform serves three markets: existing demand for the pickup, pent-up demand for a seven-seat SUV, and Robotaxi network vehicles in useful all-terrain form factors (seven-seat people movers, cargo haulers, and pickups),” Unplugged Performance continued.

“Should add,” the shop added, “the number of Tesla alumni who have reached out offering to help has been overwhelming. For anyone questioning talent or experience, we already have a strong and resourceful team, and many people who helped build these vehicles want to participate and see the platform live on.”

As of this story’s Wednesday afternoon publication, Tesla and Musk don’t appear to have offered a public response to Unplugged’s offer. That said, Musk did post on Wednesday that pickup production is being extended through summer of next year to “work through more of the Cybertruck order backlog.” And given his follow-up, who knows? Maybe he’d be open to letting someone else take over after that.

Bitcoin to Witness First Back-to-Back Annual Losses as 2027 Could Spell More Trouble: Peter Schiff

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Prominent Bitcoin critic Peter Schiff has pointed out that the premier cryptocurrency is on track to post back-to-back calendar-year losses for the first time in its history. If the situation persists and bulls are unable to stage a quick recovery, the digital currency could see a major price decline the following year as well.

Schiff noted that Bitcoin had never posted consecutive calendar-year declines in its roughly 18-year history. The digital asset posted a 6.34% price decrease in 2025, and it is already down 16.5% based on the latest spot price. He warned investors to be wary of this trend-defying move, which could result in major losses in 2027 as well.

While Schiff is known for criticizing digital currency only when it is on a downturn, the statistics he presented are accurate. Bitcoin closed 2025 lower by about 6.3% to 6.4%, ending near $87,500 after starting the year around $93,400–$93,500. It recorded a new all-time high (ATH) of $127.5k in October 2025, but failed to hold those gains by the end of December.

As of 2026, the cryptocurrency started the year around the $86k price level and is currently changing hands around $73k, representing a loss of more than 16%. It is highly likely that the digital currency won’t experience a price rebound in the last 6 weeks of the year, and it will make it two out of two for bearish years.

Historically, Bitcoin’s loss-reporting calendar years have been solo occurrences. In most instances when the premier digital currency witnesses a major contraction, like that in the years 2014 (roughly –56% to –58%), 2018 (around –73% to –74%), and 2022 (about –64%), it has followed it up with major gains in 2015, 2019, and 2023, respectively.

As a result, most years have been positive. If Bitcoin has had a hiccup, it has immediately followed it with a strong performance, which we are not seeing in 2026 or in the bearish end of 2025. However, 2025 was barely a downward trend, with a small 6.5% net price decrease. It can be considered a net price-neutral performance, which would make 2026 the year of a major price pullback and 2027 the year of a significant turnaround for the bulls.

Schiff has long argued that Bitcoin and other digital currencies have no intrinsic value and are not a store of value like Gold, which he promotes endlessly.

Lately, Schiff has faced criticism for focusing only on Bitcoin when it is under pressure, not when it is posting a strong recovery, as it did, rising from around $57k earlier this year to $86k just a few weeks ago.

Brain Moves Its Touch Center to Keep Feeling Sensation

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Biomedical engineers at Penn State have solved a major hurdle in neuroprosthetics and sensory rehabilitation: sensory habituation, where the brain gradually stops perceiving continuous artificial electrical stimulation. In a paper published in IEEE Transactions on Medical Imaging, researchers led by Dr. Xiaogang Hu monitored cortical activity using functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) during transcutaneous nerve stimulation (TNS).

By delivering 5-second electrical pulse bursts paired with 40-second recovery intervals, the team maintained a stable, unattenuated sensation of touch over 45 minutes across 60 trials. Neuroimaging revealed that while perception remained completely constant, the brain actively distributed tactile processing beyond the primary somatosensory cortex to wider neural networks.

Loss of tactile sensation in the hands, whether resulting from stroke, peripheral nerve injury, spinal trauma, or limb amputation, fundamentally impairs everyday activities. Basic tasks such as holding a fragile ceramic mug, grasping utensils, writing, or typing become daily battles when real-time sensory feedback is absent.

While bioengineers have developed transcutaneous nerve stimulation (TNS) and invasive neural implants capable of delivering artificial electrical pulses to mimic the sensation of touch, these technologies face a universal physiological barrier: sensory habituation.

Over extended periods, the central nervous system adapts to repetitive, artificial electrical stimulation. As neural circuits adjust to constant inputs, perceived intensity weakens and eventually fades away entirely. For an amputee using a motorized prosthetic hand, this decay is catastrophic: without steady tactile feedback, an individual cannot regulate grip force, leading them to either crush delicate objects or drop them unexpectedly.

Now, a team of researchers led by Dr. Xiaogang Hu, the Dorothy Foehr Huck and J. Lloyd Huck Chair in Neurorehabilitation and professor of mechanical engineering at Penn State, has uncovered both the neural dynamics of habituation and a stimulation protocol to bypass it.

Published in IEEE Transactions on Medical Imaging, the study demonstrates that finely calibrated stimulation-and-rest intervals can preserve stable, long-term tactile sensations while triggering adaptive redistribution across the brain’s cerebral networks.

“Repetitive stimulation — regardless of whether you engage stimulation through the nerves, skin or directly targeting a part of the brain — will be perceived as declining or weakening over time,” explained Dr. Hu, who holds joint appointments in Penn State’s Departments of Kinesiology and Physical Medicine and Rehabilitation. “With this work, we are trying to avoid or minimize that habituation, enabling a more stable sense of touch over a longer timeframe.”

To determine how electrical parameters influence neural adaptation, the researchers systematically tested varying electrical pulse durations and resting intervals across healthy volunteers.

Electrodes were placed over peripheral nerves in the forearm to deliver mild electrical pulses designed to feel like a gentle tingling sensation. Using trial-by-trial psychophysical scoring, the team determined that a specific combination, 5 seconds of electrical stimulation followed by a 40-second recovery interval, prevented sensory decay.

When evaluated across an extended 45-minute continuous trial consisting of 60 consecutive stimulation cycles, participants reported zero habituation, retaining a crisp, consistent tactile sensation from the first pulse to the last.

“Picture someone is holding a cup of water with their prosthetic — the sensation of touch is critical for them to maintain an appropriate grip force,” Hu noted. “We don’t want them to crush the cup with too much force or for them to lose their sensation of touch over time and drop the cup; we need to ensure that the feeling is stable.”

To explore how the brain maintains stable perception during prolonged stimulation, the investigators deployed functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS).

Unlike functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI), which requires patients to lie immobilized inside a massive, noisy scanner bore, fNIRS employs a flexible cap equipped with lightweight optical optodes. These emitters project harmless beams of near-infrared light through the scalp and skull into the superficial cerebral cortex. By measuring how light reflects back to paired photodetectors, the system quantifies local shifts in oxygenated and deoxygenated hemoglobin, serving as a high-fidelity proxy for localized neuronal metabolism and synaptic activity.

Targeting the primary somatosensory cortex (the cerebral hub responsible for mapping bodily touch), fNIRS captured real-time cortical dynamics while participants sat upright and received TNS.

The optical scans uncovered an unexpected neurofunctional divergence: while participants reported that the physical sensation felt identical across all 45 minutes, their underlying brain activation patterns were constantly shifting.

“Participants report that the sensation feels the same, but the active regions in their brain actually change over time, with changing intensity of activation and spatial distribution,” said first author Dr. Susan Coltman, assistant professor at the University of Guelph, who conducted the study as a postdoctoral researcher in Hu’s lab. “The signals start out in the somatosensory cortex, but over time, they move out beyond that origin area to different regions of the brain, reflecting progressive recruitment of distributed brain regions to process information during prolonged sensory stimulation.”

Rather than a static, local response, sensory preservation appears to depend on the brain dynamically recruiting distributed associative and secondary sensory networks to sustain conscious perception.

These findings carry major implications for clinical neurorehabilitation. Individuals recovering from hemispheric stroke or living with upper-limb amputations already experience extensive cortical reorganization. Understanding how the healthy brain balances stimulation timing and network recruitment provides a quantitative template for programming next-generation bionic prostheses and wearable rehabilitation sleeves.

The research team plans to extend these optimized stimulation paradigms into patient populations with focal stroke lesions and limb loss, aiming to calibrate personalized electrical intervals that accommodate damaged, rewired neural pathways.

By striking the biological balance between stimulation and recovery, engineers are moving closer to artificial limbs that don’t just move naturally, but feel permanently real.

What is sensory habituation in nerve stimulation?

Sensory habituation occurs when the nervous system becomes accustomed to continuous, repetitive artificial electrical stimulation, causing the perceived sensation of touch to gradually weaken and fade away over time.

How did the Penn State team prevent habituation during nerve stimulation?

The researchers identified an optimized timing ratio: applying 5 seconds of mild electrical stimulation followed by a 40-second rest period. This cycle allowed peripheral nerves and central circuits to recover, sustaining a completely stable sense of touch over 45 minutes and 60 cycles.

How did brain activity change during the experiment?

Although participants felt the exact same level of sensation throughout the 45-minute window, fNIRS imaging showed that active brain regions shifted over time. Brain activity originated in the primary somatosensory cortex and then progressively migrated outward, recruiting wider distributed brain networks to sustain sensory processing.

Funding & Support: Supported by the National Institute on Disability, Independent Living, and Rehabilitation Research (NIDILRR) and the U.S. National Science Foundation’s Division of Information and Intelligent Systems (grants IIS-2330862 and IIS-2319139).

fNIRS Characterization of Temporal Potentiation During Optimized Nerve Stimulation

Restoring stable somatosensory feedback through transcutaneous nerve stimulation (TNS) represents a significant challenge in neural engineering; however, the cortical dynamics underlying sustained stimulation are not yet well characterized.

This study utilizes a multi-method functional near-infrared spectroscopy framework to quantify the temporal evolution of cortical engagement during temporally structured TNS. It was hypothesized that stimulation parameters fundamentally constrain the spatiotemporal profile of the hemodynamic response function.

A General Linear Model with temporal and dispersion derivatives was used to analyze hemodynamic responses in 10 participants across three phases: validation, duration optimization (2, 5, or 10 seconds), and sustained engagement (45 minutes). During the optimization phase, 5-second stimulation produced the highest canonical amplitude with optimal dispersion characteristics, while 10-second trains resulted in significant temporal delays.

During the sustained phase, parametric modeling indicated no linear decay in cortical response. Instead, the cortex demonstrated temporal potentiation, characterized by a transition from sparse early-phase activation to widespread late-phase recruitment across the sensorimotor network. The left posterior parietal cortex (BA5) served as a continuous integration hub, whereas primary somatosensory regions exhibited dynamic late-onset engagement.

These findings characterize a nonlinear temporal potentiation pattern, demonstrating that the selected stimulation/rest timing regime can sustain cortical engagement and facilitate delayed network expansion. The observed intersubject heterogeneity highlights the necessity for adaptive, closed-loop TNS controllers guided by real-time hemodynamic biomarkers.

Every Outfit TV Is the Digital-Age <i><em>Fashion Police</em></i> We Never Knew We Needed

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

The founders of @everyoutfitonSATC spill the tea on their latest venture into video podcasting

“There are some things that need to be seen to be believed,” says Lauren Garroni, the brunette half of @EveryOutfitonSATC—a Sex and the City fashion archive Instagram account turned Fashion Police-style podcast that she runs with her blonde counterpart, Chelsea Fairless. She’s talking about a stretched canvas image of Dave Navarro walking in the Anna Sui Spring-Summer 1997 runway show, which is currently being sold online by a well-known publishing company. But she might as well have been speaking about the pair’s latest venture: Every Outfit TV.

“Every Outfit is a weekly rundown of the comings and goings in pop culture and fashion,” explains Garroni. She and Fairless launched their podcast in 2021, around the time that the SATC spinoff And Just Like That was announced. Devotees will recall that it was actually phase two, phase one being an Instagram account born in 2016 from Fairless’s frustration with the lack of variety when it came to photos of SATC outfits on the internet. Ten years later, the podcast is viral, the Instagram has 709k followers, and the overall project has morphed into Every Outfit TV, a video component available exclusively on Patreon.

The video format offers Fairless and Garroni the chance to do things that wouldn’t work with solely audio. (Note: not every new podcast episode will have video.) “We grew up being obsessed with the VH1 Vogue Fashion Awards and stuff like House of Style,” says Fairless. The brilliantly lo-fi, green-screen-style opening sequence posted to their Instagram account last week distills the energy they’re going for with its nods to The Nanny, Clueless, and Access Hollywood.

Podcast listeners, TV viewers, and Instagram followers can expect new franchises like “It’s Always Fashion Week Somewhere” and “The Runway Rewind,” which will showcase the pair’s thoughts on runway shows while tapping into the tabloid entertainment vibe that they both admire. There will also be Oprah Winfrey-style book club episodes and “Top 10” lists fiercely curated by Garroni and Fairless themselves.

Ask the duo why now was the ideal time to pivot, and Fairless answers bluntly. “Lauren forced me to,” she says. Garroni sees it differently. “Chelsea finally succumbed to being on camera,” she refutes. Really, the two are responding to a general podcast-world shift to video, a medium that enables them to “fulfill an itch,” says Fairless. She didn’t have cable in her house as a child, prompting her to develop an obsession with fashion magazines and websites like First View (RIP). Garroni’s upbringing makes up for it. “My parents just gave me unrestricted access to television, cable, and the internet from a young age,” she says. Now, both of them are self-diagnosed as chronically online and cite hours on TikTok and Instagram as the reason why they collectively possess the knowledge of someone who has a PhD in fashion history.

While this wealth of niche information could potentially alienate an audience, Garroni and Fairless’s playful banter (think Siskel and Ebert, or Waldorf and Statler) makes up for it. They don’t shy away from cross-generational references, either. “We have laundered our older gay men references that we learned from watching E! News and E! True Hollywood Story,” says Garroni.

In the end, the duo intends to be authentically themselves. “I think Lauren and I are examples of millennials who are very culturally Gen X,” says Fairless. “We were always more interested in Gen X things, Gen X people. I didn’t give a shit about the Backstreet Boys when I was a teenager. We were into Hole and Pavement.”

It would be remiss not to talk about the hit television series that started it all. At this point, they have documented every outfit on Sex and the City more than once in the past ten years, but developments in the show’s universe kept them going. “Something would always happen to prevent us from abandoning this,” says Fairless. Garroni agrees, recalling when Cynthia Nixon ran for governor of New York in 2018. (They released a satirical book We Should All Be Mirandas a year later. Garroni claims to be a Miranda with a Carrie rising, while Fairless is a Miranda with a Samantha rising.) “It always sustained us,” says Garroni. “Every moment we thought, ‘Okay, how long can this joke go on for?’”

If Fairless has anything to do with it, the answer to that question is forever. “Sex and the City is never done. If there is one thing I know to be true in this life, it will never go away,” she says. “It will always change forms and there will always be something newsworthy to talk about.”

Asked what else is coming down the pipeline, Garroni is quick to mention field pieces that will take them out of their impeccably designed Los Angeles set, complete with vintage gray furniture designed in the ’90s by architect David Chipperfield and a pink carpet. (It was modeled after a mashup of Halston’s New York City apartment—an Every Outfit hyperfixation—and the Chicago department store Carson Pirie Scott.) Fairless, however, has other goals in mind. She’d like to add more single-breasted blazers to her wardrobe rotation, she says. Also: “I want hair and makeup.”

JFK Jr.’s ’94 Saab Is Up for Auction. You Should Buy It

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

The late John F. Kennedy Jr., in addition to being the subject of countless menswear moodboards, a hit Ryan Murphy series, and, hey, a new coffee table book written by yours truly, was one of the most stylish guys in 1990s New York. Between his American-dynasty pedigree, chiseled good looks, and impossibly thick head of hair, not to mention his eclectic wardrobe of preppy classics, funky gorpcore, and Armani suits, it’s not hard to see why. It should come as no surprise, then, that he also had excellent taste in cars. Throughout the ’90s, JFK Jr.’s whip of choice was a 1994 Saab 900 SE Coupe, a quirky Swedish sports car that embodied everything that was great about ’90s style, down to its hunter green paint. Fortunately, for fans of oddball vintage autos and JFK Jr. alike, it’s currently up for sale via RR Auction, and the bids are rolling in.

Sporty, sophisticated, and eccentric, the Saab 900 was to JFK Jr. what the pink Cadillac Fleetwood was to Elvis, or the Porsche 935 was to Paul Newman. An consummate outlier in the American car market since the ’50s, Saab’s popularity peaked in the mid-1980s when its weird, angular designs and quirks(e.g., the ignition was next to the handbrake) found a following among design-obsessed American yuppies. Combining the Scandinavian sobriety of a Volvo with the postmodern flair of a Memphis sofa, Saab loomed large in ’90s culture: Jerry Seinfeld’s TV alter-ego drove one, as did Niles Crane, Dana Scully, and Clark Griswald’s humorless neighbors in National Lampoon’s Christmas Vacation.

During the tail end of the decade, when JFK Jr. and his late wife, publicist Carolyn Bessette Kennedy, were fixtures of Lower Manhattan life, the couple was frequently photographed driving in the Saab near their Tribeca loft. With its sporty-yet-economical turbocharged four-cylinder engine, a sleek leather interior, and a custom set of aftermarket aqua-blue padded ski racks mounted to the roof, it embodied JFK Jr.’s characteristic blend of aristocratic, athletic, and bohemian to a tee.

JFK Jr.'s green 1994 Saab 900 SE, with a current bid of nearly $30,000 and rising.

According to Bobby Livingston, executive vice president at RR Auction, “what makes this lot so special is it's not some Maserati or something flashy like that—it's a practical car that was a part of his everyday life. For Kennedy, “the choice of this car, as opposed to a Mercedes or something, speaks a lot to his style.” This particular Saab—which JFK Jr. purchased new from Zumbach Sports Cars in Manhattan for $29,841.21 in the summer of 1994, and owned until his death in 1999—comes to auction via Kevin J. Hynes, a screenwriter and longtime friend of Kennedy’s who worked with him at the New York DA’s office in the early ’90s. According to the auction listing, the car had been left at Hynes’s Westchester home in the days following Kennedy’s fatal plane crash. In 2000, Hynes then bought the car from the Kennedy estate in 2000 for $6,000, and sold it for $12,000 two years later. Its third owner is said to have started up the car regularly, but judging by its overall condition and the looks of its odometer, which shows just 30,650 miles, it seems it hasn’t hit the road in a long time.

The Saab, which was listed with a minimum sale price of $15,000, is part of a RR’s Fine Autographs and Artifacts auction that also includes a tweed hat worn by the elder JFK and Lee Harvey Oswald’s overcoat. As of Wednesday afternoon, more than 20 bids have driven the price close to $30,000. “We’ve sold a Hummer that belonged to Tupac, and a Cadillac Escalade that was used by Tony Soprano in The Sopranos, but I didn't know there would be such an affection for John F. Kennedy Jr.’s Saab,” says Livingston. “It’s already exceeded our estimate, and there’s a lot of interest, so it could go for a lot more than where it is right now.”

The Kennedys, seen here in 1998, were frequently seen driving in the Saab near their Tribeca loft.

Given its provenance and climbing auction price tag, whether JFK Jr.’s Saab ends up with a car collector, a Kennedy buff, or someone with a keen appreciation for peak-1990s style, one hopes that whoever buys it will be well-equipped to give it the treatment it deserves. That is to say, this is a car that deserves to be street-parked in Tribeca, driven to Vermont for ski trips, and otherwise treated like the ’90s style icon it is. A backward Kangol cap and an “I TOLD YA” tee are optional, but highly recommended.

T-Mobile illegally imposed 50GB cap on low-income home Internet plan, NY says

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Law requires 25Mbps home Internet. T-Mobile lowers speed to 600Kbps after 50GB cap.

T-Mobile violated a New York state law by imposing a 50GB-per-month data cap and reducing speeds to less than a megabit per second on home Internet plans it is required to offer people with low incomes, New York government officials alleged.

T-Mobile agreed to a settlement requiring it to scrap the data and speed limits, to offer the required low-income plans to more parts of the state, and to make it easier for customers to find information about the plans. The settlement requires T-Mobile to eliminate the speed limits and expand availability of the plans within 120 days.

The alleged legal violation and the settlement are related to T-Mobile’s wireless home Internet service, not the company’s mobile plans. Governor Kathy Hochul and New York Attorney General Letitia James announced the settlement on Monday, saying that a state investigation found T-Mobile violated the New York Affordable Broadband Act (ABA).

The state law doesn’t specifically mention data caps, but it requires home Internet providers with at least 20,000 customers to offer plans at specific prices and minimum speeds to people who meet income-eligibility requirements. T-Mobile’s policy of reducing speeds after customers exceed a monthly data cap violates the law, New York officials said.

“An investigation by the Office of the Attorney General (OAG) found T-Mobile’s offerings did not meet the ABA’s requirements, which mandate that home Internet providers offer high-speed broadband to income-eligible households for $15 per month at speeds of at least 25 Mbps, or $20 per month for speeds of at least 200 Mbps,” the press release said.

New York says in the settlement with T-Mobile that “after the data cap is reached, the customer’s maximum download speed is reduced from 25 Mbps to 600 Kbps.” New York’s press release said “a typical customer could hit the data cap after about four days of usage.”

Data caps and speed limits aren’t the only restrictions. T-Mobile also “only offers ABA Internet plans to customers in homes that are eligible for 5G home Internet,” preventing residents in 4G-only areas from accessing the plans, the settlement says. The company also prevented search engines from indexing the webpage that provides detailed information about eligibility for and enrollment in ABA plans, it says.

“T-Mobile did not fully comply with the New York Affordable Broadband Act,” New York said in the settlement.

T-Mobile’s ABA webpage confirms that customers are provided only 50GB per month and that speeds after using the allotment are merely “up to 600 Kbps.”

Although the settlement includes a list of the New York AG’s findings, T-Mobile did not admit to any violation. “Respondent neither admits nor denies the OAG’s Finding in paragraph 1-14 above,” the document says.

New York started enforcing the Affordable Broadband Act in January 2025 after winning a nearly four-year court battle against broadband industry lobby groups that tried to kill the law. The groups that sued New York in a failed effort to block the law included CTIA, the wireless industry association that represents T-Mobile and other carriers. The law finally took effect a month after the Supreme Court rejected the broadband industry’s challenge.

When contacted by Ars, T-Mobile did not explain why it imposed the restrictions on low-income plans.

“We have a long history of helping customers in New York stay connected through low-cost broadband, and we’re updating our plan to meet the state’s requirements while maintaining the same low price,” T-Mobile said in a statement. “We’ve reached an agreement with the New York attorney general that builds on that commitment, provides greater clarity around our offering and makes the plan available to more eligible customers.”

The settlement with New York applies to T-Mobile’s fixed wireless Internet plans, which can be used for home Internet service as an alternative to wired broadband. The fixed wireless Internet relies on T-Mobile’s cellular network but provides access at a single location, delivering wireless signals to receivers installed at customer homes.

It took almost 18 months for New York to investigate T-Mobile’s compliance and reach a settlement with the company. “In June 2025, the New York Department of Public Service contacted the OAG and relayed concerns about certain features of the plan that T-Mobile was offering to customers eligible for reduced-price Internet service under the ABA,” the settlement said.

It seems that ISPs have generally offered the required plans, though cable company Optimum failed to comply until Ars made some inquiries on behalf of a customer who could not obtain the plan he was entitled to shortly after the law took effect. ISPs knew about the law long in advance due to the multiyear court case that they initiated, but Optimum wasn’t ready to comply with the requirement and rejected the low-income man’s request twice.

New York officials said the AG’s office “found that T-Mobile was only offering ABA plans in areas where it had deployed 5G wireless service, even though it offered full-price Internet plans that could deliver 25 Mbps download speeds in areas where it had 4G service. In addition, T-Mobile specifically coded its website to hide its ABA plan information from search engines, making it difficult for New Yorkers searching for low-priced Internet to find their plans.”

The settlement requires T-Mobile to stop intentionally limiting download speeds, and says the “temporary reduction of download speeds due solely to network congestion shall not constitute a violation of this Assurance.” There’s also an exception in the state law, which says ISPs shall provide 25Mbps “subject to exceptions adopted by the Public Service Commission where such download speed is not reasonably practicable.”

Extending the low-cost service to 4G areas, T-Mobile agreed to provide the low-income plans “at every address at which Respondent offers residential fixed wireless Internet service.” Speeds lower than 25 Mbps would not violate the settlement if T-Mobile “would be unable to provide a minimum download speed of 25 megabits per second to a residential fixed wireless Internet service customer enrolled in a non-ABA Plan at the same address.”

The document says that “T-Mobile offers 5G home Internet to roughly 40 percent of homes in rural areas of upstate New York and 4G (LTE) home Internet to the remainder of homes in the regions where T-Mobile offers fixed wireless service.”

To make its ABA webpage more accessible, the settlement says T-Mobile “shall not include a noindex tag on the webpage, or otherwise prevent search engines from indexing the webpage or presenting a link to the webpage in response to search engine queries.” T-Mobile must also prominently link to the ABA webpage on the part of its website that lists or describes its standard home Internet deals.

New York could sue T-Mobile if it violates the settlement. The ABA’s enforcement mechanism requires the attorney general to sue a broadband provider on behalf of residents. Such lawsuits can recover restitution and civil penalties of up to $1,000 per violation.

ISPs that want to raise another challenge to New York’s law could get help from the Trump administration, which asserts that states must not enforce price regulations or net neutrality laws against any ISP that obtains grants from the $42 billion Broadband Equity, Access, and Deployment (BEAD) program. The grants have not been distributed yet, and California argues that the Trump administration does not have authority to preempt such laws.

In addition to this week’s settlement, New York struck a deal with T-Mobile last month over the carrier violating its promise to never raise prices on certain mobile plans that came with a lifetime price guarantee.

A state investigation “found that from 2017 to 2019, T-Mobile advertised ‘Price Guarantee Plans’ to its 4G customers, promising to ‘never change the price you pay,’” the New York attorney general’s office said on October 20. “However, in 2024, T-Mobile changed course and began raising the price of these plans by approximately $5 per phone line. New Yorkers enrolled in approximately 252,957 of these Price Guarantee Plans saw their phone bills increase.”

T-Mobile agreed to give affected customers several reimbursement options and a price lock until May 2029. The attorney general’s office estimated that the agreement would provide up to $38 million in value to customers affected by the price hikes. T-Mobile also agreed to pay the state a fine of $98,834.75.

T-Mobile provided a statement on the price-hike settlement.

“The $38 million cited in the press release is an estimate of potential benefits that a group of long-standing T-Mobile customers may choose to take advantage of, not the amount in refunds or payments,” T-Mobile said. “The agreement provides for approximately $98,000 paid to the state and a range of optional customer benefits. We’re glad to have this matter resolved and remain focused on delivering value to our customers.”

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Investors Are Throwing Their Weight Behind Ethereum—Details

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Investor confidence in Ethereum appears to be holding firm according to a recent analysis from Cryptoquant, even as the cryptocurrency’s price has come under pressure. A clear divergence has opened up between Ethereum’s market performance and its staking activity, with the staking ratio continuing to climb despite the recent decline in ETH’s value, a trend that could help support the network’s supply dynamics over time.

The Ethereum 2.0 staking rate has risen by roughly 4.32% since May, meaning a growing portion of the total ETH supply is now locked in staking. This shift reduces the amount of ETH freely available for trading and introduces a potentially positive supply-side effect. While ETH’s price on Binance has dropped from about $2,800 in October to $2,132, the staking ratio has continued to rise, pointing to sustained investor commitment amid the weakness.

Around 36.32% of Ethereum’s total supply is currently staked, which limits the liquid portion that can be readily sold. Greater participation in staking may ease selling pressure and help stabilize prices if demand remains solid.

However, staked ETH is not permanently locked, since validators can begin withdrawals at any time. Meanwhile, total staking inflows have fallen by 40.11%, underscoring the need to separate longer-term growth in the staking ratio from shorter-term daily flow data.

The standout pattern is the negative correlation between ETH’s price and its staking ratio. As prices have moved lower, staking participation has continued to rise, suggesting investors have not meaningfully dialed back their commitment.

Conclusively, Cryptoquant’s report indicates that Ethereum still carries a potentially supportive supply-side dynamic for the medium to longer term. If the market can absorb current selling pressure and draw in enough demand during this correction, the conditions could be set for a firmer upward move ahead.​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​

Sandra Wollner’s ‘Everytime’ Is a Stunning Film About Grief Like You’ve Never Seen Before

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Few human emotions are as fathomable as grief. Someone (or something) was here, had meaning and value and presence, and is now gone. There is a hole, a gap, an empty space. The pain that accompanies that void may manifest in different people in different ways, but its very existence is understandable. What makes people happy? What makes them sad? What makes them angry? Grief, well, that’s an easier question with a tougher answer.

For her third feature film, German filmmaker Sandra Wollner tackles the most tangible of dramatic genres — the grief tale — and turns it on its head in a way that our own David Ehrlich called “infinitely beguiling.” Mostly, the film shows how infinitely elastic and malleable this genre is, and the emotions that power it. Related Stories How ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Dominated the Box Office: GKIDS Chief on IMAX’s Impact and the Growing Market for Foreign-Language Movies Democrats Won. When Do Hollywood Crews Get Back to Work?

The logline for the Un Certain Regard winner is simple enough: after the death of teenager Jessie (Carla Hüttermann), her mother Ella (Birgit Minichmayr), her little sister Melli (Lotte Shirin Keiling), and her boyfriend Lux (Tristán López) form an unlikely family unit as they attempt to move on. To say much else is spoiler-y in the truest of senses: It would detract from the joy and wonder of watching this story unfold.

That Wollner’s film is doing something different with seemingly well-trod material is obvious from the start, as “Everytime” layers on cut scenes from the video game “Vintage Story” — not “Minecraft,” though Wollner understands why the audience might think it’s the more well-known game — long before the real drama kicks in.

A familiarity with “Vintage Story” is not required to understand her film, Wollner promised during a recent interview with IndieWire. “Maybe if someone hears the dialogue of Melli and Lux, then maybe they would feel they have to know more about it, because they’re talking about how you can basically cut trees to harvest stuff and to get that, but I don’t feel that you have to know anything,” she said.

Wollner herself isn’t even a “hardcore gamer,” but she admires video games for their world-building and how they can encourage exploration. Her early forays into both “Vintage Story” and “Minecraft” tickled that. “It felt like the ‘Backrooms’ phenomenon, but before the ‘Backrooms’ phenomenon,” she said. “You’re walking through infinite worlds that feel so big, but minimalistic in a way. And the sounds; a very ambient sound feels eerie and soothing at the same time. I was just very compelled and inspired by that.”

The idea that Jessie would leave behind her own video game world that Melli could then play in was one of the film’s first seeds. “I thought, it’s a way of a memorial I haven’t seen so far,” she said. “[We have this] whole world of avatars and gaming communities where people meet online and then if someone really goes out, their avatar might live forever in there and create a life on its own in a way. I just found that very intriguing, and this is why I wanted in there.”

While the film is unique in how it handles digital mementos — avatars, literally — Wollner also pays close attention to real-world details that can make grief feel even bigger.

“One of the first scenes [I thought of] was Ella lying down on the couch and hearing how her daughter would come back [into their apartment],” the filmmaker said. “It’s something I think also everybody can relate to, even with a more natural loss and not such a tragic one. For example, when I was a kid, my aunt would always run down the stairs [in the apartment complex where we lived], and I would always hear her in the morning, running down in the morning. The noise was very specific to this person. I didn’t want it to be full on, but to let us think, it couldn’t be Jessie.”

Wollner was also intrigued early on by the “constellation of people” impacted by the loss of one. “The boy, the little girl, and the mother, it’s people I’ve met in my life in several decades, I would say, variations of them, but somehow similar constellations,” she said. “They seemed very clear to me. Three people who, especially the mother and the boy, really tried to do the right thing in a way. But there is a big conflict in there. They are having a weird dependent relationship. She needs him to grieve. He needs her to forgive. Of course, I could have made this conflict much bigger, but I really was interested in ordinary people who just try to do the right thing and just try to go along with that.”

Having her own child added a new dimension to the film, even for Wollner. The filmmaker was editing the film when her daughter was born (her longtime partner is writer and cinematographer Timm Kröger, who also worked on the digital imaging in “Everytime”), an experience that deepened her understanding of her own work.

In the film’s second half, the trio travel to Tenerife, Spain on holiday, a place that baby Jessie (who we’ve seen in home videos) once visited. There, they discover another baby, who looks just like Jessie, playing happily in the same quiet space that baby Jessie once occupied.

“We watched the scene again of baby Jessie sitting there, and we looked at each other,” she said. “I always understood what [that scene] means for me, but there, we really felt it for the first time, being a parent. And I felt, oh, I really nailed it! I mean, that sounds so stupid, but when I had the baby, I think, as many people feel, you still sometimes have the feeling, oh, where does it come from? It’s suddenly here. Suddenly there’s a person there. It’s like with that moment when she walks around the corner, and baby’s sitting there.”

Wollner was also compelled by the way we memorize different places and the unpredictability of memory. The odd little space, located in the far reaches of the resort’s clubhouse, comes to haunt Ella, Melli, and Lux in different ways, forever linked to Jessie and her absence.

“When my dad died, I was with him, and I suddenly had this image of him standing somewhere in this bend on the river and fishing, and it felt so familiar,” the filmmaker said. “This memory intruded on my present. We cannot control when we get a memory. They’re just coming. Sometimes we can make them happen to come. But we never know if maybe the weird place where we saw our baby sitting or the weird moment is the one that haunts us the most. It’s this weird banal little room with this weird banal kitschy toy that is playing this music. And this is the thing that then comes back.”

Those “banal little” details pack immense emotional power. So do turns from Minichmayr and López, who never turn to over-the-top histrionics to convey their grief. Wollner’s choice to set the majority of the film a year after Jessie’s death helped, giving the characters some distance from the immediate stab of loss.

“It was a conscious choice to pick some time after the death [to mostly focus the film on],” she said. “As a writer, we need to know what happened in the time between, and so I wrote that as well, but it honestly felt not depictable. I feel I can only fail to show that. I wasn’t interested in these big emotions. That just felt too overwhelming. And sometimes we are made to believe that grief comes in these neat boxes to check: Now you’re sad, now you’re angry, now you accept. Maybe you can be angry again, then you accept. It’s just this messy thing that you can feel for a very long time.”

Keiling also offers a nuanced performance, built more on a kid’s curiosity than on saddling the young actress with big, showy scenes. There’s no screaming and crying here, no sobbing or yelling. But that doesn’t mean that Keiling wasn’t accessing some major emotions.

“I love to work with kids,” Wollner said. “And even having a kid, I think I still am a kid in a way. For this one, I met Lotte, I had not met another kid, I only met her, I did not do any other castings. I just fell in love and thought, oh, she’s exactly what I thought of Melli could be and even better, so I trusted the script to her.”

Wollner worked with fellow filmmaker Juli Neuhaus, who served as a “kids’ coach” for the younger members of the cast. Wollner and Neuhaus were eager to meet Keiling where she was. The young performer loves to draw, so she would draw the scenes before performing them, to better understand what they were about, which allowed her to access a full spectrum of feeling.

“With Melli, there was no scene where she really had to act her grief,” she said. “I always felt that the film itself, the editing and the framing, the absence, we see her doing her homework, and then we see the same room behind her, but now we know that the sister is not there anymore, I always felt that this will tell us the grief, so she doesn’t need to act it. If she holds her phone and is texting her sister, it will already make us feel the grief. She doesn’t need to feel the heavy weight of that. So, actually for her, it was, I would say, like a very light shoot. Playful. It was really playful.”

Still, for all its sense of fantasy and play, “Everytime” is about a terrible heartbreak. Yet, as ever, Wollner chose to depict Jessie’s sudden, shocking death in a unique way, tracking her fall from a tall building while out with Lux from a great distance away. We can see it. We can do nothing about it. That’s grief, too.

“This was one that really was clear to me in the writing process already, that we’re going to have this lens and that we have this weird perspective of the universe itself looking at it, being so far away and still not able to do something about it, but still witnessing so intimate,” she said.

The actors were on one high-rise building, while Wollner and her crew were on another, about half a mile away. “And we had this insane lens. I had a 3,000-millimeter consumer camera, which is an insane lens,” she said. “It’s really like you don’t want to point it on people. It feels really not OK. But I was sure that I wanted to work with exactly that length or something similar to that, that feels kind of forbidden to be so close and too far away at the same time.”

While the scene is shot as if Jessie and Lux are very close to the edge of the building, the actors were not. (Hüttermann later told Wollner that she still had the feeling she might fall, even with myriad safety measures in place. That’s just how immersive the entire thing felt.) Hüttermann’s actual fall was shot in a studio, and their visual effects supervisor Kariem Saleh combined that footage with the outdoor shots.

Because they needed a certain kind of light, Wollner estimated they had only about five takes to get it right, though she knew they would likely only get one shot with the “perfect” light. Her script also called for the camera to follow a bird after the fall, but she said with a laugh, “Unfortunately, no bird came in exactly that moment.” (They lensed other birds, though, and those also ended up in the final composite shot.)

“We worked so many hours just on the sounds, just to get the right level of there is a little sound, and there is a little tink when she falls down, a little bit,” she said. “Just to realize, given how far away we had to be, how does the sound have to be? We worked the whole time on that, and I never really felt it until I sat at Cannes in the audience. And I heard the gasps for the first time and really it went through my bones. I mean, it’s weird to say that, but I was happy, but I felt the first time that it actually worked.”

Wollner remains very emotionally connected to the film, constantly talking about the way certain scenes make her feel, either while making them or editing them or even seeing them with an audience. Of course, such a deep-feeling filmmaker crafted such a beguiling film, one that invites all sorts of responses and theories. Since Cannes, audiences have been talking about its genuinely thrilling third act and the implications of its final scenes. And while we won’t spoil them here, we had to ask: What did Wollner want her viewers to feel in those moments?

“The feeling that I wanted to have is that [this film that is] grounded or hopefully grounded in the beginning, then suddenly you walk around the corner and you feel still some ground, but it’s such an odd place now and you don’t know,” she said. “I always hoped it doesn’t feel just dreamy, but there is some groundedness to it and that the people still react in a way that made sense for them, the characters, but that you don’t know what room you entered now, some kind of back room. That was clear from the beginning.”

And, as fantastical as it might sound, she’s clear: it’s all real. “I mean, it’s such a cliché to say that, but everything we see at the end is true at the same time,” she said.

A 1-2 Special release, the film is in limited theaters now, with a wide release to come in February 2027.

Why Nervous System Hyperarousal Keeps Jolting You Awake

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

A psychological and sleep research study published in PLOS Mental Health reveals that people who frequently startle awake while drifting off to sleep, a phenomenon known as “sleep starts” or hypnic jerks, report significantly poorer sleep quality, greater insomnia severity, and heightened anxiety.

Conducted by Tanja Grünberger and Manuel Schabus at Paris Lodron University Salzburg, the investigation examined 194 adults across Austria and Germany. The researchers discovered that frequent sleep starts do not merely represent isolated motor twitches, but manifest alongside autonomic surges, sensory flashes, and cognitive-emotional hyperarousal driven by unhelpful beliefs and acute pressure to fall asleep.

Just as consciousness begins to dissolve and the body relaxes into slumber, an abrupt, violent jolt shatters the transition. Often accompanied by the vivid sensation of tumbling into an abyss, an electric zap down the spine, or a blinding flash of light, the heart races as the sleeper gasps awake in sudden panic.

Known medically as “sleep starts” or hypnic jerks, these sudden myoclonic twitches occur at the boundary between wakefulness and non-rapid eye movement (NREM) sleep. While sporadic sleep starts are a benign, universal human experience, for a substantial subset of the population they recur persistently, transforming the threshold of sleep into a source of dread.

In a study published in the open-access journal PLOS Mental Health, researchers Tanja Grünberger and Manuel Schabus of the Centre for Cognitive Neuroscience at Paris Lodron University Salzburg, Austria, investigated the psychological and physiological profile of individuals afflicted by chronic hypnic jerks.

Their findings indicate that recurrent sleep starts are not isolated neuromuscular glitches; rather, they serve as a behavioral manifestation of sleep-related hyperarousal and cognitive-emotional anxiety.

Because standardized psychometric tools for quantifying hypnic jerks have historically been lacking, the research team synthesized existing clinical neurology and sleep medicine literature to construct a targeted assessment battery.

They surveyed 194 adults across Austria and Germany, dividing them into two cohorts: 96 individuals who experienced abrupt awakenings accompanied by at least one secondary symptom weekly, and 98 matched control participants without frequent sleep starts. Participants completed validated measures assessing insomnia severity, subjective sleep quality, daytime sleepiness, dysfunctional beliefs about sleep, anxiety, and depression.

The resulting symptom profiles revealed that sleep starts involve a multi-sensory and autonomic cascade:

“Sleep starts are much more than a simple muscle twitch when falling asleep,” explains first author Tanja Grünberger. “Participants described not only jerks, but also sensations of falling, a racing heart, flashes of light or loud bangs, and strong feelings of alarm or fear – and those who reported sleep starts at least once a week also reported poorer sleep quality, more unhelpful beliefs about sleep and more anxiety symptoms.”

When comparing the two cohorts, the differences were statistically pronounced. Individuals experiencing weekly sleep starts had significantly poorer overall sleep quality, greater insomnia severity, higher levels of general anxiety, elevated depressive symptoms, and more dysfunctional beliefs regarding sleep.

Statistical modeling revealed that how frequently people experienced sleep starts was closely tied to sleep quality, rigid sleep beliefs, and anxiety levels. Together with demographic variables (age and gender), these psychological factors accounted for 26.9% of the variance in sleep start frequency between individuals.

Importantly, participants reported that sleep starts surged during periods of psychological distress and emotional strain. In contrast, physical sleep deprivation was less influential on the frequency of episodes.

“What we found most interesting was how closely sleep starts are linked to how people think and feel about sleep,” Grünberger notes. “The more strongly participants held unhelpful beliefs about sleep and the more anxious they were, the more often they reported sleep starts – and in the moment itself, many described a strong pressure to finally fall asleep. Our data cannot show what causes what, but this pattern resembles what we know from insomnia.”

Traditional medical advice has often dismissed hypnic jerks as inconsequential physiological quirks requiring no clinical management. However, the Salzburg findings show that for individuals caught in chronic hyperarousal, sleep starts create a self-reinforcing loop: pre-sleep anxiety increases nervous system excitability, triggering a violent startle response, which then reinforces catastrophic beliefs about one’s inability to sleep.

“These findings support the view that sleep starts should be considered within broader frameworks of sleep-related hyperarousal and cognitive-emotional activation,” the authors write.

Looking forward, senior author Manuel Schabus highlights the therapeutic avenues this connection opens: “For most people, sleep starts are a harmless feature of falling asleep. For a subgroup, however, they go along with distress and a longer time to fall asleep. Whether approaches that reduce arousal and sleep-related worry also help with distressing sleep starts has not yet been tested – that is an important next step.”

Targeted cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia (CBT-I), autonomic down-regulation protocols, and pre-sleep relaxation techniques could offer relief for patients whose transition into slumber is repeatedly derailed by their own nervous system.

Sleep starts are sudden, involuntary muscle twitches or jolts that occur during the transition from wakefulness to sleep. They can be strong enough to wake a person fully and are often accompanied by sensations of falling, electric shocks, flashes of light, a racing heart, and a sudden surge of fear or alarm.

How do frequent sleep starts relate to mental health and sleep disorders?

People who experience sleep starts at least weekly report significantly worse sleep quality, greater insomnia severity, higher anxiety, and more dysfunctional beliefs about sleep compared to those who do not. The frequency of these jolts tracks closely with daytime emotional strain and psychological stress.

What causes sleep starts to happen more often?

While occasional jerks are normal, frequent episodes appear driven by central nervous system hyperarousal. Heightened stress, performance anxiety around falling asleep, and catastrophic beliefs about insomnia keep the autonomic nervous system on high alert, triggering a startle response instead of a smooth physiological descent into sleep.

Phenomenology and associations with sleep, psychological, and demographic characteristics

Sleep starts (SS) are transient phenomena during sleep onset. Although traditionally characterized by sudden muscle jerks, they may also involve sensory, cognitive, emotional, and autonomic experiences. Despite their high prevalence, empirical data on phenomenology and associated characteristics remain limited. This online survey examined SS phenomenology and exploratory associations with demographic, sleep-related, and psychological variables in 194 adults.

Participants with self-reported sleep-related movement disorders or relevant medical conditions were excluded. Individuals reporting abrupt awakenings ≥1/week and ≥1 additional symptom formed the SS group (SS frequency ≥1, n = 96); all others the comparison group (SS frequency <1, n = 98). Because SS were operationalized using self-reported symptoms, they could not be distinguished from related sleep-onset phenomena (e.g., exploding head syndrome, hypnagogic hallucinations).

Standardized questionnaires (PSQI, ISI, ESS, MZS, HADS) assessed sleep quality, insomnia severity, daytime sleepiness, dysfunctional sleep-related beliefs, and anxiety/depression symptoms. Participants described diverse experiences including motor jerks, falling sensations, dream-like experiences, somatosensory symptoms, autonomic arousal, and emotional reactions, accompanied by alarm, anxiety, and confusion.

Compared with controls, the SS group reported poorer sleep quality, greater insomnia severity, stronger dysfunctional sleep-related beliefs, and higher anxiety/depressive symptoms (all p < .05). Exploratory regression analyses indicated that poorer sleep quality, stronger dysfunctional sleep-related beliefs, and higher anxiety symptoms were associated with higher SS frequency, whereas depressive symptoms were not. The final model explained 26.9% of variance (R2 = .269, adjusted R2 = .245).

More frequent SS were retrospectively reported during emotional stress, whereas sleep deprivation was perceived as less influential. Although many described SS as manageable, a substantial subgroup reported distress and sleep disruption.

This suggests that SS represent heterogeneous sleep-onset experiences associated with subjective sleep disturbance and cognitive-emotional arousal. Although causality cannot be inferred from cross-sectional self-report data, the results highlight potentially relevant links between SS and sleep-related cognitive-emotional processes.
