An Elder Scrolls Classic Is Finally Getting the Remaster It Deserves After 22 Years

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Covering the video games industry since 2017, with experience in news, articles, and reviews. Currently a writer at DualShockers and gaming magazines.

As a nostalgic gamer, I have a soft spot for forgotten games that were left behind by time, especially when they're part of a franchise as beloved as The Elder Scrolls.

While most players have spent the past two decades exploring Morrowind, Oblivion, and Skyrim, one rather unusual entry in the series has been sitting on a much less familiar platform: the Nokia N-Gage. Thankfully, that's about to change!

The Elder Scrolls Travels: Shadowkey, a first-person RPG originally released in 2004, is getting a second chance thanks to an independent project called Shadowkey Reforged.

Developed by Dan Whelan, the project brings the forgotten spin-off to Windows, Mac, and Android with a new engine, widescreen support, controller compatibility, and plenty of visual and gameplay improvements. Even better, it's launching today, November 11.

Morrowind is rightfully regarded as one of the greatest open-world RPGs of all time, but it's aged badly and would benefit from a full remake.

For anyone unfamiliar with it, Shadowkey was the third entry in The Elder Scrolls Travels, a series of portable spin-offs released between Morrowind and Oblivion. Unlike the mainline games, however, it was exclusive to Nokia's N-Gage, a rather ambitious handheld-phone hybrid that didn't exactly have the longest life on the market.

That exclusivity made Shadowkey one of the more inaccessible chapters in the franchise's history. And while you could technically track down the original hardware and a copy of the game, that's hardly the most convenient way to revisit a 22-year-old RPG.

That's where Shadowkey Reforged comes in! Rather than simply emulating the N-Gage, Whelan built a new engine capable of running the original game's data and scripts on modern devices. Players can choose between a Classic mode that preserves the original presentation and a Modern mode featuring upgraded artwork, improved lighting, and several gameplay additions. So yes, it is literally the best of both worlds.

For anyone unfamiliar with it, Shadowkey was the third entry in The Elder Scrolls Travels, a series of portable spin-offs released between Morrowind and Oblivion.

The latter isn't just a fresh coat of paint, by the way. Modern mode introduces features inspired by newer Elder Scrolls games, including stamina-based sprinting, stealth, and ragdoll physics. You can also toggle individual features to customize the experience instead of committing to one preset. And if you happen to have The Elder Scrolls III: Morrowind installed, there's another neat little bonus: Shadowkey Reforged can use its soundtrack and sound effects, preserving a connection between two games released during the same era, in case you want an extra touch!

Skyblivion team says a former volunteer is threatening legal action, even after the devs removed their code and rebuilt the affected systems.

Interestingly, Whelan has also been transparent about using AI during development. According to the developer, AI tools helped with coding, building the engine, and debugging, while the updated artwork combines AI-generated images with Photoshop and manual sprite editing.

Shadowkey Reforged is available as a free, unofficial project, with versions for Windows, Mac, and Android. However, there's one important detail players should know before getting too excited: the download doesn't include the original game's files. This means you'll need to provide your own copy of Shadowkey to play it – you can check more info about that on the port’s website.

Still, it's great to see such an obscure piece of The Elder Scrolls history getting another shot at life. Bethesda hasn't announced an official remaster, but Whelan's project might be the closest many players will get to experiencing this forgotten adventure without digging up a Nokia N-Gage.

How One Creative Couple Found Harmony in Their 1920s Los Angeles Craftsman

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Nothing about where interior designer Harrison Soll is now was part of the plan. Five years ago, he was focused on his work, and he didn’t want any distractions. But when he met Rodney Burns, a stylist and owner of the legendary Los Angeles vintage gallery, Church Boutique, it set everything on a new path. They met in the fall; by New Year’s they were a couple. “I didn’t think it was going to be a relationship,” Soll says. He credits their shared interests for bringing them together: “We always found a throughline with design.”

They now live in a 1925 craftsman house in Laurel Canyon. Soll took the lead decorating the nearly 1,400-square-foot home in a combination of his and Burns’s styles with many pieces coming from Burns’s eclectic collection of vintage art, furniture, and sculpture. Burns has worked with Lenny Kravitz as a stylist and creative collaborator for the past 15 years, and the singer says he admires what Burns and Soll have created together. “The mix of Rodney’s early- to midcentury style mixed with ethnic expression next to Harrison’s bold minimalist modern approach seamlessly blend together,” Kravitz tells AD.

The backyard of Soll’s and Burns’s Laurel Canyon home is a bricolage of funky, inherited stonework and vintage pottery.

The nail art above the fireplace has a cute backstory story: Burns and Soll had seen it at a vintage shop in Santa Monica and Burns regretted not buying it. When he went back to purchase the piece, it was marked as sold. But only because Soll had already covertly bought it as a Valentine’s Day gift for Burns.

The giant Sergio Bustamante giraffe bust in the foyer is a delicate topic. “The giraffe was one of our biggest fights in decorating this house,” Burns explains. “I was determined to have that giraffe live some place in this house because he’s just fantastic, and the scale of him, and the rarity of him.” Except, counters Soll, that the placement of the giraffe is treacherous because the front door swings right in its path. “You just have to be careful,” Soll says.

The foyer—which features a collection of masks and vintage paintings—was one of the most difficult areas to style, explains Burns. The couple was trying to be selective of what pieces to incorporate from Burns’s massive collection and at one point Burns suggested putting all of his masks in storage to save for another house down the line, or passing them onto Burns’s vintage boutique, Church. Soll dissented, saying, “No, let’s put them everywhere.”

The living room features two sofas—a Joseph D’Urso for Knoll piece reupholstered in leopard fabric and a leather De Sede Terrazza. The carpet is the Antilocarpa style from Stark. A trio of fiberglass shell art was sourced through Kelly Wearstler. The art behind the sofa is a manipulated canvas garment bag by the artist Roosevelt “Rip” Woods, the first black artist to have a solo show at the Phoenix Art Museum.

As renters, there were naturally limitations on what could be achieved. Soll got permission to retile a bathroom and install a new vanity. The doe-patterned wall-to-wall carpeting in the living room was an “ask for forgiveness” move, but his landladies ended up loving it. Burns, on the other hand, needed more convincing. “The carpet took many dinners of conversations between the two of us,” Soll says.

Soll describes himself as more of a minimalist while Burns avows, “I’m a maximalist to the umpteenth power.” The couple attempted to strike a balance while still filling the space with character. Some rooms nod to Soll’s leanings toward minimalism like an office with a midcentury desk and a 1970s tapestry and the dining room where a Sergio Bustamante table and a Chinese Art Deco rug are the main attractions.

When decorating their Laurel Canyon home, Soll and Burns had to figure out how to combine their opposing styles, with Soll being more of a minimalist and Burns being a maximalist. But, says Burns, “[Soll] has made me be able to take a pause and then also make me a little less attached to some of the things that I’ve collected  and given me an opportunity to maybe show a little bit more restraint.” In the corner, a brass Maison Jansen palm tree mingles with a Sergio Bustamante monkey.

Burns, who has been styling Lenny Kravitz for over a decade, says his approach to home design and fashion are one in the same: “Building an outfit, building a room: same thing in my opinion.”

Kravitz offers these kind words on Burns: “What I love about Rodney is that he is limitless in his concepts and always thinks outside of the box. His visual vocabulary enables him to mix a vast stew of eras and genres. He pays tribute to our ancestors and uplifts new innovators. Always soulful, expressive, and elegant.”

In the dining room, Soll placed a Sergio Bustamante table on a diagonal to make enough room for the couple’s large dog. “She’s a girthy girl,” says Soll, referring to the table, not the dog. The chairs are by Paul Evans and the Chinese Art Deco rug was sourced from Ariana Rugs. The Tom Greene chandelier was sourced from Burns’s boutique, Church.

Burns’s maximalism is on display in the living room, where a couch upholstered in leopard Scalamandré mixes with zebra-hide chairs and a sinuous De Sede sofa. (That animal print couch, Soll wants you to know, also features a block of leather upholstery: “I just think it would have been too maximalist to do a full Scalamandré sofa.”)

The living room is filled with brass sculptures (a Maison Jansen palm tree and a Sergio Bustamante monkey), bronze figures sourced from an estate sale in Arizona, and several pieces of sculptural artwork.

The bedroom mixes patterns and textures with a stainless steel bed from TTMM, Paul Evans “Cityscape” nightstands, striped bedding, and antique bronze table lamps with African mud cloth shades.

The 1970s textile art in the office is a vintage find of Burns’s that Soll loves. “I used to go to Rodney’s apartment when we first started dating, and I would be like, ‘Oh my God! Like, if we break up, I’m never gonna be able to find this tapestry,’” he says.

Soll got permission from his landladies to retile the bathroom and add a new vanity. The metal Curtis Jeré artwork comes from Burns’s collection.

The bohemian architecture of the home lends quirkiness to the design. The main bedroom isn’t a perfect square, making it seem like a stainless steel bed from TTMM is placed on a diagonal but is actually following the geometry of a very funky, angular bedroom.

The couple has taken their time tinkering and adjusting the decor, taking a considered approach to collecting and combining their belongings. Soll describes the overall vibe as having an Afrocentric theme that’s also a little bit Ralph Lauren. “Our visions have mingled,” Burns says.

Still, there is one last item the couple needs to complete the design: a blanket basket. But, says Soll, “We still haven’t found one we agree upon.”

The backyard is also home to a shed (not pictured), which functions as Burns’s closet.

Anduril says its first satellite can see adversaries maneuver in orbital night

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

“What’s unique is it’s got a huge dynamic range. We can see things in full dark.”

Anduril Industries has announced the successful demonstration of a new satellite-based sensor designed to track other spacecraft that may be hiding their maneuvers in Earth’s shadow.

Funded with Anduril’s internal capital, the company’s oven-sized satellite launched on a SpaceX rideshare mission in July with a package of tech demo payloads: an edge-compute processor, a power-management system adaptable to different mission types, and a long-wave infrared space sensor capable of detecting other objects in orbit.

Anduril reported on the success of the tech demo mission Tuesday. The company’s satellite, named Anduril-216, flies about 590 km (366 mi) above Earth, spending about one-third of each orbit in darkness. Anduril-216’s night passes allow its infrared imager to shine.

“This is a unique sensor that allows us to take imagery in long-wave [infrared],” said Gokul Subramanian, Anduril’s senior vice president of engineering. “What’s unique about that is it’s got a huge dynamic range. It has very low solar exclusion, so we’re blocked by the Sun very rarely, and we can see things in full dark.”

The US military and a growing number of commercial data providers use a network of radars and optical sensors on the ground and in space to monitor satellite traffic. Anduril acquired one of the commercial satellite-tracking companies, ExoAnalytic Solutions, earlier this year. But there’s a gap in optical tracking as satellites fly in Earth’s shadow, so space traffic monitors could temporarily lose track of a spacecraft if it performs a maneuver in orbital night.

The Space Force is charged with tracking satellites for two primary reasons. First, military commanders want to know if an adversary moves its satellite in a threatening manner. Second, the Space Force issues traffic alerts to global satellite operators if there’s a risk of a collision.

“Right now, we don’t have ground sensors in our adversaries’ sovereign lands, and then in the eclipse period where we are relying heavily on visible sensors, the satellites that are not illuminated by the Sun, we can’t see those,” said Adam Thurn, vice president and general manager of orbital systems at Anduril. “With the long-wave infrared space sensor, we’re looking to close that gap.”

Anduril’s long-wave infrared sensor works by detecting heat signatures from spacecraft, even in periods of eclipse. It provides a “unique capability for us to witness when an adversary or other resident space objects are maneuvering during those eclipse periods, where in the past we’ve been fairly blind,” Thurn said.

In creating the infrared space sensor, engineers applied imaging technology Anduril uses in its autonomous weapons for use in space. Anduril released a single image Tuesday from the sensor on Anduril-216 showing an infrared view of the Moon above Earth’s horizon. The Moon image offers just a taste of what the instrument can do, Thurn said.

“The long-wave infrared capability we have on orbit can do both unresolved and resolved imagery,” Thurn said, suggesting Anduril’s sensor could be used to inspect other satellites in orbit if it flies close enough. For now, Thurn said Anduril is focusing on optimizing its tracking sensor for low-Earth orbit, but it could be used in other orbits, and eventually in cislunar space near the Moon.

Apex Space, a satellite-manufacturing startup, built the spacecraft platform for Anduril-216. Subramanian said Anduril has no plans to start its own satellite production line. Instead, Anduril foresees buying third-party satellite platforms and integrating them with sensor and compute modules to perform a range of different missions in space.

“You’re seeing that today with Anduril-216, where we started with a simple mission, which was how do we track satellites, especially in these eclipse periods where the US currently does not have the ability to maintain custody,” Subramanian said. “And we designed a mission around that.”

The compute module on Anduril-216 is designed to fuse data from different kinds of sensors, such as infrared or visible imagers, and generate tracking solutions on its own, without intervention or analysis from the ground.

Subramanian said Anduril’s current focus is on acquiring space domain awareness data for sale to the US military, not selling entire satellites or sensors to the government. “At present, Anduril is not offering this system as a merchant supplier,” he said. “That could change in the future.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

The Traditional Kitchen Knife Bobby Flay Recommends Is On Amazon

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

Whether you're an amateur home cook or a seasoned chef, a high-quality chef's knife is one of the three knives you need in your kitchen arsenal. However, with so many options out there, choosing the right one can quickly become overwhelming. You'll want something versatile enough to finely chop vegetables, break down a chicken, and handle just about everything in between. Durability matters, too. A good knife should last for years, if not a lifetime, with the right care. And, of course, it needs to hold its sharp edge. Luckily, Food Network star Bobby Flay has a recommendation that takes some of the guesswork out of the process (per The Kitchn): the Shun Classic 8-inch Western Cook's Knife, available on Amazon.

Before getting into the knife's many selling points, there's one thing we have to address. At $163.95 (which is the sale price — it's normally around $205), it's certainly not a budget-friendly buy. But there are plenty of reasons why we think it could be worth the investment. At its core, knife steel is a blend of iron and carbon, with manufacturers adding other elements to improve its performance. It's these added elements that set Shun knives apart from the rest.

The Shun Classic 8-inch Western Cook's knife uses a proprietary blend called VG-MAX, which is specifically formulated to resist corrosion and wear while maintaining a sharp edge. It also has a high carbon content, helping the blade achieve the hardness needed for excellent edge retention. The combination of elements in VG-MAX gives the Shun an advantage over many more affordable knives made with lower-grade steel, which have the tendency to lose their sharpness far more quickly with regular use. In other words, you're paying for a knife that's designed to stay sharp, rather than one that needs constant maintenance to keep it performing.

If the name didn't give it away already, Shun is a Japanese knife brand, and all its knives are handcrafted in Seki City, a region renowned for centuries of sword and knife making. Any kitchen nerd knows that Japanese knives are up there with German ones when it comes to quality, so just by looking at the place of origin, you can expect a considerable degree of craftsmanship to have gone into this blade.

Interestingly, despite its Japanese origins, the Shun Classic 8-inch Western Cook's Knife doesn't adopt the traditional santoku shape, often considered Japan's answer to the Western chef's knife. Both blades are versatile, all-purpose kitchen knives, but they differ in their blade profiles and the way they're typically used. Neither is necessarily better than the other; that distinction largely comes down to personal preference and preferred cutting style.

While the blade might not have a traditional Japanese shape, the handle certainly draws on Japanese craftsmanship. It's made from Pakkawood, an engineered composite created by stacking thin hardwood veneers and infusing them with phenolic resin, which is then bonded together using high heat. The result of this admittedly complicated process is a durable, moisture-resistant material that's less prone to warping and cracking than ordinary wood. This makes it easy to keep clean, although that doesn't mean you should put the knife in the dishwasher or leave it to soak in the sink. Even quality materials can't protect even the most durable blades from the harsh detergents, water, and high temperatures, which will inevitably cause rusting and damage over time. Hand-washing and drying your knife promptly remains the better option.

Multiple Sclerosis Atrophy Spreads From High-Stress Neural Hubs

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

A massive neuroimaging investigation involving 2,187 patients with multiple sclerosis reveals that grey matter atrophy does not accumulate at random; rather, it radiates outward from specific cortical and subcortical “disease epicenters.” Published by Mar Barrantes-Cepas and colleagues, the study provides strong clinical evidence for the “nodal stress hypothesis.”

The authors demonstrate that high-degree topological network hubs, such as the visual, sensorimotor, and temporal cortices, alongside the thalamus, hippocampus, and amygdala, are selectively vulnerable to neurodegenerative collapse because their intense metabolic demands exhaust local cells. The findings also document transneuronal degeneration propagating along structural connections, confirming that progressive neurodegeneration in multiple sclerosis occurs independently of acute inflammatory relapses.

Multiple sclerosis (MS) has long been defined as a chronic autoimmune disorder characterized by inflammatory demyelinating lesions scattered across the white matter of the central nervous system. Yet in recent decades, advanced neuroimaging has revealed an equally devastating, covert pathological hallmark: widespread, accelerated grey matter atrophy.

Unlike transient focal inflammation, grey matter volume loss begins during the earliest clinical stages, correlates directly with irreversible physical disability and cognitive decline, and progresses relentlessly even when disease-modifying therapies effectively suppress clinical relapses.

For years, the spatial patterning of this tissue loss remained elusive, presenting in non-random yet seemingly disparate cortical and subcortical distributions.

Now, in an extensive multicenter neuroimaging study evaluating MRI scans from 2,187 individuals diagnosed with multiple sclerosis, a research team led by Mar Barrantes-Cepas and colleagues reveals the organizational logic behind this neurodegenerative spread.

Their work substantiates the nodal stress hypothesis, demonstrating that grey matter loss originates within high-traffic, hyper-connected “disease epicenters” and propagates throughout the connectome via transneuronal degeneration.

To map the spatiotemporal evolution of brain atrophy, the researchers utilized structural MRI datasets from an international cohort of 2,187 patients across various stages and clinical subtypes of multiple sclerosis, comparing localized tissue loss against normative connectome architecture.

The analysis revealed that the earliest and most severe tissue loss localized within the brain’s primary topological hubs. These “epicenters” included:

These findings provide large-scale empirical backing for the nodal stress hypothesis. In complex network topology, hubs represent the most critical communication centers, routing traffic across disparate functional modules.

However, this central positioning carries a severe bioenergetic cost. High-degree hubs maintain higher baseline firing rates, express higher densities of energy-intensive ion channels, and require disproportionate mitochondrial ATP production to preserve synaptic transmission.

In multiple sclerosis, where chronic subclinical microglial inflammation, widespread demyelination, and oxidative stress compromise mitochondrial respiratory chain efficiency, these high-demand hubs become metabolic bottlenecks. The relentless functional demand exhausts cellular energy supplies, triggering localized axonal degeneration and neuronal apoptosis.

The study demonstrated that once a disease epicenter begins to decay, the pathology does not remain confined. Instead, the scans revealed clear markers of transneuronal degeneration, a cascading process where damage in a primary hub travels anterogradely or retrogradely across anatomical white matter tracts to damage downstream connected neurons.

As high-traffic epicenters fail, their connected secondary and tertiary structural partners lose vital neurotrophic support and experience excitotoxic or disuse stress, causing neurodegeneration to systematically ripple through the structural connectome.

Crucially, the investigators tracked how these network-based degradation pathways differed across relapsing-remitting and progressive forms of the disease, finding that network-level strain drives disability accumulation across all cohorts.

“It is now clear that disease progression can occur independently of relapses and acute inflammation,” write Barrantes-Cepas and colleagues. “Network-based measures, which are easily obtainable from conventional MRI and normative datasets, could be used to explain future atrophy progression in individual patients.”

The recognition that multiple sclerosis progression is driven by network-mediated metabolic exhaustion challenges the classical clinical focus on managing only acute, gadolinium-enhancing white matter lesions.

Because conventional MRI scans routinely collected in routine clinical practice can be evaluated using normative connectome templates, network-based nodal stress modeling offers a low-cost, non-invasive prognostic tool. Clinicians could potentially map an individual’s specific epicenter vulnerability early in the disease course, forecasting spatial trajectories of grey matter loss years before clinical deficits emerge.

Furthermore, these findings highlight fresh avenues for neuroprotective drug discovery: therapies designed to bolster mitochondrial bioenergetics, protect against metabolic exhaustion, or buffer high-traffic hubs from metabolic overload could provide the missing therapeutic weapon needed to halt progressive disability in multiple sclerosis.

How does grey matter atrophy spread through the brain in multiple sclerosis?

Atrophy does not spread at random. It originates in specific “disease epicenters”, the brain’s most heavily connected topological hubs, such as the thalamus, hippocampus, visual, and sensorimotor cortices. From these epicenters, damage propagates to distant brain regions along structural nerve pathways through transneuronal degeneration.

The nodal stress hypothesis suggests that brain hubs with the highest number of connections are under constant, intense metabolic strain because they must process and route massive amounts of neural communication. In multiple sclerosis, where energy production is already impaired, these high-traffic hubs suffer metabolic exhaustion, making them the first to degenerate.

Can multiple sclerosis worsen without relapses or acute inflammation?

Yes. This study confirms that progressive neurodegeneration and brain volume loss can proceed independently of acute clinical relapses and active autoimmune lesions, driven instead by chronic network-based metabolic failure and structural disconnectivity.

The role of network connectivity and transcriptomic vulnerability in shaping gray matter atrophy in multiple sclerosis

Clinical progression is strongly linked to gray matter atrophy in multiple sclerosis (MS), detectable early on MRI and progressing nonrandomly across the brain. However, the mechanisms driving its spatiotemporal progression and individual variability remain unclear.

Using MRIs from 2187 participants, alongside normative data, we systematically investigated network-based mechanisms underlying MS-related atrophy. Regional atrophy was associated with functional cortical hubness, supporting the nodal stress hypothesis, and was constrained by anatomical and functional connections, consistent with transneuronal degeneration. Lesional disconnection and transcriptomic vulnerability played marginal roles.

Patient- and subgroup-level analyses revealed that network-based mechanisms are specifically linked to MS-related neurodegeneration and may operate differently in distinct subtypes or disease phases. Atrophy patterns were anchored to the connectivity profiles of disease epicenters involving the visual, sensorimotor, and temporal cortices, as well as the hippocampi, amygdalae, and thalami.

Network-based measures enhanced the explanation of future atrophy progression in people with MS, providing a mechanistic framework to understand neurodegeneration in MS.

Can You Be Too Honest at Work? Here Are 3 Questions to Ask Before Giving Feedback.

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

It is possible to be too honest in the workplace.

Saying exactly what you think isn’t always the best career move.

Social psychologist Vanessa Bohns recently said in an interview with NPR that knowing when to speak up and when to stay quiet can be more challenging than figuring out how to phrase your thoughts.

“There are times when honesty is the best policy,” Bohns said. “On the other hand, honesty is not the best policy if whatever we say is going to be honest without any instrumental value.”

In her book Should I Say Something? The Science of What to Share—and What to Keep to Yourself, published in October, Bohns draws on research to explore which thoughts and feelings are worth voicing, and which are better left unsaid. Her guidance spans everything from offering an honest opinion to celebrating a win, apologizing or opening up.

People can find it hard to deliver criticism, even comments meant to help. “It pains us to embarrass or hurt other people,” Bohns said. Researchers call it the MUM effect, or minimizing unpleasant messages. It’s a marker of a general human tendency to hold back bad news more than good news.

Bohns advised workers to ask themselves three questions before sharing their honest opinion or feedback. First, is this information helpful? Second, would I want to hear this? And finally, do they want my feedback?

Speaking up tells the other person that you noticed something and felt it mattered enough to mention, according to Bohns.

The question, then, isn’t simply whether your opinion is honest, but if it serves the person hearing it. Will it help them improve or offer insight into themselves?

If your opinion is unkind or about something they can’t change, it might be the better route to keep it to yourself.

If a new colleague repeatedly mispronounces the CEO’s name, gently correcting them now could spare them embarrassment later.

Pointing it out could feel uncomfortable in the moment, but it could end up helping your colleague make a good impression on senior management.

Before holding back with honesty or feedback, consider whose feelings you are protecting. As Bohns writes in her book: “Are you being kind, or are you avoiding your own discomfort?”

Bohns advises against giving advice to someone who hasn’t asked for it. You may end up explaining something they already understand better than you do, she said.

Even with good intentions, deciding what someone needs to hear or how much honesty they can handle can come across as condescending, Bohns writes in her book.

Instead of making that decision for them, ask if they’d welcome your feedback. “Give them the autonomy, then respect their decision, whatever it might be,” she said.

Saying exactly what you think isn’t always the best career move.

Social psychologist Vanessa Bohns recently said in an interview with NPR that knowing when to speak up and when to stay quiet can be more challenging than figuring out how to phrase your thoughts.

“There are times when honesty is the best policy,” Bohns said. “On the other hand, honesty is not the best policy if whatever we say is going to be honest without any instrumental value.”

This 28-Year-Old Started a Caffeinated Side Hustle. It Made $125K in Year 1 — So She Quit Corporate to Work on It Full-Time.

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Rosie Goldwasser wanted to create a product delivering calm, focused energy without the crash.

This Side Hustle Spotlight Q&A features Rosie Goldwasser, 28, of Santa Monica, California. Goldwasser was working full-time as a customer experience strategist at PwC (PricewaterhouseCoopers) when she started her side hustle: More Than Matcha, a premium matcha and lifestyle brand.

The business hit $125,000 in revenue without spending a single dollar on advertising in its first year and doubled revenue in its second. Goldwasser left her corporate role in March 2026 to focus on More Than Match full-time. The brand is on track for between $400,000 and $500,000 in revenue this year. Responses have been edited for length and clarity.

When did you start your side hustle, and where did you find the inspiration for it? I started building More Than Matcha in 2023 and officially launched in March 2024. The idea actually came from a personal frustration. For years, I was either too tired to function or too wired, jittery and anxious from coffee to sleep. There was no in-between until I discovered matcha. It was love at first sip. It gave me a calm, focused energy that actually lasted.

But finding high-quality matcha was challenging. Cafés charged $10 for drinks that consistently left me disappointed. At-home options lacked quality, consistency and a brand I could genuinely connect with.

Having spent four years in marketing and six years in customer experience consulting, I’ve always been fascinated by what makes consumers fall in love with brands and how to create experiences that exceed their expectations. As a daily matcha consumer myself, I saw a gap I couldn’t ignore.

What were some of the first steps you took to get your side hustle off the ground? How much money did it take to launch? I was never going to launch a matcha brand unless I found a matcha powder so good that I felt I’d be doing the world a disservice by not bringing it to market.

So I spent months researching, sourcing, sampling and learning everything I could about what makes exceptional matcha, from growing regions and cultivars to harvesting methods and sustainable farming practices. I finally discovered the four-cultivar blend we use. To this day, it’s the best matcha I’ve ever tasted. I’ve since had the opportunity to visit our supplier in Japan, meet some of the farmers behind our matcha and see firsthand the care that goes into producing it.

After finding the right product, I invested $25,000 of my own savings into building the matcha brand, without any outside funding. Over the next several months, I brought every detail to life: branding, packaging, website development, product photography — and all the little things that go into creating a brand from scratch.

Are there any free or paid resources that have been especially helpful for you in starting and running this business? For the first year of More Than Matcha, I packed and shipped every single order myself, spending nights and weekends fulfilling orders. I’d recruit friends and family to help pack during busy periods, turning my 700 square-foot apartment into a makeshift fulfillment center.

At first, doing everything myself felt like the smartest way to save money. But eventually, I realized I was spending so much time keeping the business running that I had almost no time left to actually grow it.

Making the decision to outsource fulfillment was a turning point. Yes, it was an added expense, but it gave me back something far more valuable: my time. Suddenly, I could focus on product development, marketing, partnerships and all the things that would actually move the business forward.

If you could go back in your business journey and change one process or approach, what would it be, and how do you wish you’d done it differently? I would have been much more disciplined about focusing on our hero product before expanding into other categories. When we first launched More Than Matcha, we introduced our matcha powder alongside four different hats. I loved the idea of building a lifestyle brand and imagined people wearing our merchandise everywhere.

But the problem was that nobody knew who we were yet. We spent so much time and money sampling, designing and producing merchandise before we had built a community of people who actually wanted to represent our brand.

If I could do it again, I would put that money and energy into making our core product even more successful, building customer loyalty and understanding what our audience wanted next.

When it comes to this specific business, what is something you’ve found particularly challenging and/or surprising that people who get into this type of work should be prepared for, but likely aren’t?Especially in the CPG space, you’re constantly making decisions that could change the trajectory of your business. From the products you develop and the manufacturers you trust, to your branding, pricing, hiring, retail partners and which sales channels to pursue. And when you’re a bootstrapped, solo founder, there’s no team of executives to turn to for the final call.

I’ve learned to start the decision-making process long before I actually need to make the decision. I’ll start interviewing candidates months before I’m ready to hire and begin conversations with manufacturers long before I need to make the switch. I’ll ask other founders about challenges they’ve faced before I’ve even encountered those challenges myself.

In a business where one decision can impact your product, customers and cash flow for months to come, that preparation can make all the difference.

Can you recall a specific instance when something went very wrong — how did you fix it? Just a few weeks ago, we were gearing up to launch our new matcha travel collection. After months of development, I had approved the final sample of our single-serve matcha sticks and moved forward with producing 25,000 units.

But the manufacturer kept delaying production. It wasn’t until the night before launch that the first four boxes finally arrived. I had a gut feeling something was wrong, so I drove to our fulfillment center to inspect them myself. Unfortunately, I was right.

The product was nothing like what I had approved. Matcha powder was leaking out of both ends of the sticks, covering the other products in the boxes. They were completely unsellable.

We had spent weeks teasing the collection, built an entire marketing campaign, redesigned our website and generated so much excitement. And just like that, on launch day, I had to cancel everything.

Rather than trying to hide what happened, I picked up my phone, filmed a video in one take and told our community exactly what went wrong. The response was beyond anything I could have imagined. Instead of being disappointed, our customers rallied around us. The comments and DMs poured in, thanking me for being transparent and refusing to compromise on quality.

We’re now working with a new manufacturer to bring these sticks to market the right way, and somehow our community is even more excited for their eventual launch.

How long did it take you to see consistent monthly revenue? How much did the side hustle earn? It took about four months to start seeing consistent monthly revenue. In our first year, we generated $125,000 in revenue without spending a single dollar on advertising. In our second year, we more than doubled that revenue, continuing to grow organically and reinvest what we earned back into the business.

What does growth and revenue look like now? 2026 has been a major reinvestment year for More Than Matcha. Rather than focusing solely on short-term revenue growth, we made the intentional decision to invest in the long-term future of the brand.

We completely rebranded, upgraded everything from our packaging to our website and spent much of the year developing our new matcha travel collection, our biggest product launch yet, now scheduled for early 2027.

Even with so much of our time and resources focused on building for the future, we’re still on track to generate between $400,000 and $500,000 in revenue this year, all while remaining completely bootstrapped.

What is your best piece of specific, actionable business advice? Done is better than perfect. When I first started More Than Matcha, I didn’t have a three-year, five-year or 10-year business plan. My goal was simply to get my idea out into the world to see if others believed in it too.

Looking back, I actually think that was one of the best things I could have done. Had I tried to map out everything I wanted this business to become before I even launched, I might have gotten so overwhelmed that I never would have started in the first place. Instead, I focused on getting a product I was proud of into customers’ hands, letting them validate the idea, and learning, iterating and adjusting along the way. About a year into the business, after seeing how customers responded and what opportunities were opening up, my long-term vision became so much clearer.

So figure out the smallest version of your idea that you’re genuinely proud to put into the world and launch it. Don’t wait until you have the perfect website, the perfect packaging or a five-year roadmap. Let real customers help you figure out what comes next.

This Side Hustle Spotlight Q&A features Rosie Goldwasser, 28, of Santa Monica, California. Goldwasser was working full-time as a customer experience strategist at PwC (PricewaterhouseCoopers) when she started her side hustle: More Than Matcha, a premium matcha and lifestyle brand.

The business hit $125,000 in revenue without spending a single dollar on advertising in its first year and doubled revenue in its second. Jacob Elordi will star in an adaptation of The Executioner’s Song

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Fresh off his Oscar-nominated turn as the Creature in Frankenstein, Jacob Elordi is set to star in and produce an adaptation of Norman Mailer’s Pulitzer prize-winning 1979 novel The Executioner’s Song.

The Executioner’s Song is a complex, fictionalizd biography-of-sorts of American murderer Gary Gilmore, who, after being convicted of murdering two men in Utah, insisted on his death penalty sentence being carried out, despite the national moratorium on capital punishment implemented by the Supreme Court case Furman v. Georgia, which decided that different states’ inconsistent approaches toward criminal execution violated the Eight and Fourteenth amendments. Gilmore, who fought anti-capital punishment attorneys insistent on preserving his life, was eventually executed by firing squad in 1977.

The novel is broken up into two sections: “Western Voices,” and “Eastern Voices.” The first part is about the forces that made Gilmore into the man he became (including a youth spent in child detention facilities in the barren American west), while the second is about the media frenzy and national debate around the case, and spotlights, in particular, Gilmore’s relationship with the celebrity photographer Lawrence “Larry” Schiller, who conducted many interviews with Gilmore, his family, and others related to the case and brought the project to Mailer, in the first place.

Mailer continued Schiller’s research to craft a nearly 1000-page, plainly-written epic about the nihilism of the American West, the American proclivity towards violence, media madness, the exploitation of the working class, and the cult of white male loneliness. In her review in The New York Times Book Review, Joan Didion raved about Mailer’s accomplishment, calling it “an absolutely astonishing book,” and noting how it shocked her by being about something much more than what it initially seemed: “The Executioner’s Song did not suggest, in its inception, the book it became,” she wrote. “It began as a project put together by Lawrence Schiller, the photographer and producer who several years before had contracted with Mailer to write “Marilyn,” and it was widely referred to as ‘the Gary Gilmore book.’

It seemed one of those lives in which the narrative would yield no further meaning… What Mailer could make of this apparently intractable material was unclear. It might well have been only another test hole in a field he had drilled before, a few further reflections on murder as an existential act, an appropriation for himself of the book he invented for An American Dream, Stephen Rojack’s The Psychology of the Hangman. Instead Mailer wrote a novel, a thousand-page novel in a meticulously limited vocabulary and a voice as flat as the horizon, a novel which takes for its incident and characters real events in the lives of real people.

What she found instead, she writes, was a novel telling a story of a uniquely American desperation. She marvels at how Mailer uses his sentences to convey this above all else: the countless failed attempts for relatedness between people and things, with the sparseness of his prose summoning a “search for a field of negative energy linking these events and these people and the empty melancholy of the place itself.”

She also notes, with surprise, that the strongest of the novel’s voices are the women: “Men tend to shoot, get shot, push off, move on. Women pass down stories.”

Schiller and Mailer had collaborated as photographer-and-writer on numerous projects, beginning with 1972’s Marilyn: a Biography, and The Executioner’s Song is unquestionably their most monumental. Schiller, who directed the 1982 film adaptation of the novel starring Tommy Lee Jones, will executive-produce the new adaptation.

Elordi, who rose to stardom in HBO’s Euphoria, will play Gilmore (a fortuitous thing, given the resemblance between the two men). Elordi has been gravitating to literary projects—most obviously Frankenstein (for which he was nominated for every major acting award). Recently, he has also starred in Emerald Fennell’s 2026 adaptation of Emily Bronte’s Wuthering Heights, and the miniseries adaptation of Richard Flanagan’s Booker-prize winning novel The Narrow Road to the Deep North which was released on Prime Video, last year. Recently, he also played Elvis Presley in Sofia Coppola’s Priscilla and co-starred in Ridley Scott’s postapocalyptic thriller The Dog Stars.

This marks Elordi’s first turn as a producer (not executive producer); Scott Stuber of United Artists and Nick Nesbitt will produce, in addition to Elordi’s manager Matt Vioral and Sherry Marsh.

Schiller, whose photographic legacy is entwined with the landmark moments of the 20th century, was the last person to photograph Marilyn Monroe before she died. He also photographed Lee Harvey Oswald the night he was killed, and Jack Ruby moments after he killed Oswald. He photographed Paul Newman and Robert Redford on the set of Butch Cassidy and the Sundance Kid (and also directed the photographic section of the film), and captured the Watts riots and the Electric Kool-Aid Acid Tests. He captured landmark touchdowns by O. J. Simpson and Muhammad Ali defeating Floyd Patterson to win the World Heavyweight Boxing Title. As a filmmaker, he made numerous projects for television, and won an Emmy in 1986 for his adaptation of Peter the Great. He collaborated with O.J. Simpson on a book called I Want to Tell You, while Simpson was in prison, and after he was acquitted, collaborated with former Time magazine journalist Jim Willwerth to write the New York Times bestseller American Tragedy: The Uncensored Story of the Simpson Defense. His photos and stories appeared in countless publications, including Life, Paris Match, The New Yorker, Time, Newsweek, and the Saturday Evening Post.

Olivia Rutigliano is an Editor at Lit Hub and CrimeReads. Her other work appears in Vanity Fair, Vulture, Lapham's Quarterly, AirMail, the Los Angeles Review of Books, Public Books, The Baffler, Bright Wall/Dark Room, Politics/Letters, The Toast, Truly Adventurous, and elsewhere. She has a PhD from the departments of English/comparative literature and theatre at Columbia University, where she was the Marion E. Ponsford fellow. She hosts the podcasst "Culture Schlock" at Lit Hub Radio. She is on instagram at @oldebean, twitter at @oldrutigliano, and bluesky at @oliviarutigliano.bsky.social.

I sit down with Hailin Jin, Adobe's research lead for imaging and design, to discuss how the project could revolutionise vector design.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Sneaks is often the highlight of Adobe MAX, giving the brand an opportunity to share the exciting innovations it's working on. Among the wild Adobe Sneaks announced today, one tool in particular could be game-changing for a whole host of creatives, from illustrators to concept artists, marking an exciting upcoming era for the brand.

Project Link Up is set to revolutionise vector design, turning the simple dragging function into context-aware fusion that automatically matches a scene's perspective. (TLDR: constantly redrawing objects at new viewing angles could soon be a thing of the past).

To understand more about Project Link Up, I caught up with Hailin Jin, senior principal scientist and research lead for imaging and design at Adobe, to get his behind-the-scenes insight into the work.

Mohit and the team started with an observation about an everyday gesture: dragging an object. Design tools usually interpret that as an instruction about placement, but there’s much more intent in what a designer moves, where they move it and how they move it.Placing an illustrated car into a parking space, for example, can involve separately adjusting its perspective, scale and style. The team asked whether the gesture itself could communicate more of that creative direction and help adapt the object to its surroundings, while keeping the result editable.

Yes. In our current prototype, a creativity slider lets the designer guide how subtle or expressive the adaptation should be, from preserving more of the original form to exploring a more dramatic transformation.The goal is to combine the ease of a gesture with explicit creative control. The resulting vector artwork remains editable, so designers can continue refining it.

Two contrasting styles don’t necessarily need to become one. The key is understanding the creator’s intent: should the object match the scene, retain its own personality, or create a deliberate contrast?Project Link Up explores combining the drag gesture and scene context with creative controls and prompt guidance. A designer could specify “match the scene’s illustration style” or “keep this character’s style, but adapt its perspective and lighting.”The aim is to help designers steer the relationship between elements, while keeping the resulting vector artwork editable.

The philosophy is to make creative exploration more immediate. When combining two elements requires substantial adjustment before you can judge whether an idea works, that effort can interrupt the process of discovery.Link Up explores how a familiar gesture could let designers try different relationships between objects, see what happens and develop the ideas they find interesting. Keeping the result editable as vector artwork matters: a promising combination becomes something the designer can continue shaping.

A strong example is manually reworking an asset to fit a new scene, particularly redrawing it from a different perspective. The team is exploring how that adaptation could happen as the designer places the object. That could reduce the preparation needed to evaluate a composition and give designers more time to refine the creative decisions that make it work.

A major direction in our research is helping tools understand creative content and the relationships within it. That understanding can make interactions more meaningful: moving an object into a scene could communicate an intention about perspective or composition, rather than simply a new position.For experienced designers, the opportunity is to express their judgment more directly and retain precise control over the result. Link Up explores one part of that future. We want to learn what creators expect a gesture to mean and how tools can respond in ways that feel useful and predictable.

For more MAX news, check out Adobe's new Photoshop 2027 features.

Daytime Sleep Waves in Parkinson’s Linked to Hallucinations

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

A clinical neurophysiology study reveals that sleep-like slow brain waves frequently intrude into conscious wakefulness in patients with Parkinson’s disease, correlating directly with minor hallucinations and impaired cognitive functioning. Conducted by Fosco Bernasconi and colleagues, the high-density electroencephalography (EEG) investigation evaluated 84 individuals with Parkinson’s disease alongside 30 healthy older adults.

The researchers discovered that patients not only exhibited higher frequencies of wakeful slow waves per minute but also that heightened wave amplitude tracked closely with psychotic symptoms and worse neuropsychological scores, suggesting wake slow-wave dynamics could serve as non-invasive prodromal biomarkers.

Parkinson’s disease is traditionally diagnosed and monitored through cardinal motor signs: resting tremor, muscle rigidity, bradykinesia, and postural instability. However, patients and caregivers frequently report that non-motor complications, particularly cognitive decline, sleep architecture disruptions, excessive daytime sleepiness (EDS), and perceptual distortions, impose the greatest burden on daily quality of life.

In healthy brains, low-frequency slow waves (< 4 Hz), typically encompassing delta rhythms and slow oscillations) serve as the electrophysiological hallmark of non-rapid eye movement (NREM) slow-wave sleep. While healthy individuals may occasionally show transient local slow waves during brief episodes of mind-wandering or acute fatigue, wakefulness is predominantly governed by desynchronized, higher-frequency oscillations.

While previous sleep laboratory research established that nocturnal slow waves are degraded and fragmented in neurodegenerative disorders, whether these sleep-like slow oscillations abnormally penetrate the conscious, awake brain in Parkinson’s disease has remained an unresolved question.

To address this, a research team led by Dr. Fosco Bernasconi utilized electroencephalography (EEG) to examine awake cortical dynamics in 84 individuals diagnosed with Parkinson’s disease and 30 healthy elderly control participants.

Their findings demonstrate that sleep-like slow waves systematically breach wakefulness in Parkinson’s patients, directly correlating with cognitive deficits and the presence of minor hallucinations.

The investigators recorded resting-state EEG data while participants remained awake in a controlled testing environment, simultaneously evaluating subjective and objective daytime sleepiness.

The electrophysiological analysis revealed that patients with Parkinson’s disease experienced both heightened daytime sleepiness and a significantly higher incidence of sleep-like slow waves per minute across the scalp compared to the healthy control group.

This phenomenon aligns with the emerging neuroscience concept of “local sleep”, a state wherein isolated cortical regions enter a transient, sleep-like bistable state of neuronal silence while the rest of the cerebrum remains awake. In Parkinson’s disease, the degeneration of ascending subcortical wake-promoting monoaminergic and cholinergic pathways (originating in the locus coeruleus, raphe nuclei, and basal forebrain) may undermine the thalamocortical mechanisms responsible for sustaining wakeful oscillatory tone.

Beyond the baseline frequency of slow waves, the structural geometry of the waveforms yielded striking clinical insights:

These findings suggest that when cortical networks fall into slow-wave synchronization during the day, the brain’s ability to correctly parse sensory feedback and maintain veridical perceptual boundaries is compromised. The intrusion of sleep-like oscillations into waking perceptual networks may provide the electrophysiological foundation from which illusions and hallucinations emerge.

“Future studies should investigate whether [sleep-like slow wave] alterations are already present at a prodromal stage of [Parkinson’s disease],” write Bernasconi and colleagues, noting that quantitative slow-wave profiling could aid in identifying populations at elevated risk.

Because non-motor indicators such as REM sleep behavior disorder (RBD), mild cognitive changes, and visual phenomena can predate motor onset by up to a decade, identifying objective, scalable biomarkers is a clinical priority.

High-density EEG is non-invasive, accessible, and significantly less resource-intensive than molecular positron emission tomography (PET) or cerebrospinal fluid sampling. If atypical awake slow-wave oscillations can be verified in prodromal cohorts, clinicians could deploy rapid daytime EEG protocols to stratify individuals at high risk for Lewy body pathology, opening a window for early neuroprotective interventions before extensive dopaminergic denervation occurs.

What are slow brain waves, and when do they normally occur?

Slow brain waves are low-frequency electrical oscillations (such as delta and sub-delta waves) that typically appear during deep, restorative non-REM sleep. In healthy individuals, they indicate reduced cortical excitability, although brief, localized bursts can occasionally emerge during daytime fatigue or mind-wandering.

How do wakeful slow waves differ in Parkinson’s disease patients?

Patients with Parkinson’s disease experience a significantly higher rate of sleep-like slow waves per minute while awake compared to healthy older adults. Furthermore, those who experience minor hallucinations exhibit slow waves with markedly larger amplitudes.

Why does this study matter for the early diagnosis of Parkinson’s?

Because daytime slow-wave abnormalities directly track with hallucinations and cognitive decline, researchers believe these wave patterns could emerge during the prodromal (pre-motor) phase of the disease. Measuring awake slow waves via non-invasive EEG could provide an objective biomarker to detect Parkinson’s risk years before irreversible neurodegeneration sets in.

Sleep-like slow waves during wakefulness are associated with hallucinations and poorer cognition in Parkinson’s disease

Slow waves during sleep are fundamental for neural homeostasis, metabolic regulation, and waste clearance and are known to be altered in neurodegenerative diseases. Sleep-like slow waves (SLSWs) have been observed in awake, healthy individuals, where they are linked to fluctuations in vigilance and mind-wandering.

Whether SLSWs are altered in Parkinson’s disease (PD), and whether such alterations contribute to psychiatric or cognitive complications, remains unknown.

In a cohort of 84 nondemented patients with PD and 30 healthy older adults, we found that SLSWs occurred more frequently in patients with PD. Hallucinatory traits in patients with PD were associated with higher SLSW amplitude, especially over frontocentral regions. Moreover, more prominent SLSWs were associated with poorer cognitive functions.

We conclude that, whereas slow waves during sleep have positive physiological functions, exacerbated diurnal SLSWs may reflect responses related to PD neuropathology, at the cost of hallucinations and reduced cognitive function.

How to Make Money on Social Media Without a Huge Following

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Creator-business expert Shana Davis-Ross explains what actually turns a social audience into revenue.

For years, follower count was the scoreboard. More followers meant more influence, more brand deals and, in theory, more sales. That logic no longer holds.

If you’re building a business and treating social media as a numbers game, you may be solving the wrong problem. That’s the core argument from Shana Davis-Ross, founder and CEO of Ponte Firm, an Austin-based talent management agency that has worked with more than 100 creators building real businesses on social platforms.

In a free Entrepreneur virtual event, Davis-Ross broke down what actually drives revenue online and what’s just noise. Here are five key takeaways from the session, from the metrics worth tracking to where to focus when your time and budget are limited.

▶ Watch the full 40-minute session on YouTube

Followers are easy to count, which is exactly why they became the default measure of success. But a follower who never engages, clicks or buys adds nothing to your bottom line.

Davis-Ross makes the case for quality over quantity and explains which numbers entrepreneurs should track instead. Think engagement, conversations and conversions rather than raw audience size. If you’ve ever felt discouraged by slow follower growth, this section reframes what progress really looks like.

Jump to “Quality over quantity” in the video (2:20) →

A viral post can feel like a win. Yet views and revenue often come from very different kinds of content. Davis-Ross digs into the real difference between the two, and why chasing algorithm-friendly trends can pull you away from the people most likely to become customers.

This may be the most encouraging idea in the session for early-stage founders. A tight community that knows, likes and trusts you can generate more revenue than an audience many times its size.

Davis-Ross walks through how creators build trusted communities and why that trust compounds over time. It’s a practical blueprint for anyone who has assumed they need to “get big” before they can make money.

Watch “Building trusted communities” (4:04) →

Most entrepreneurs know they need to sell on social, and most hate how it feels. Constant promotion wears out an audience fast.

The session covers how to talk about your products and services in a way that feels natural, so your audience welcomes your offers instead of scrolling past them. Davis-Ross also tackles the consistency question: how often to show up, and how to keep going when growth feels slow.

Not every business owner can post daily on five platforms. Davis-Ross addresses where to put your limited time and budget first, the common hurdles creators face, and the strategy and persistence it takes to push through them.

She also covers what it takes to scale a creator-led business once the foundation is in place. The session closes with an audience Q&A on platform choice and growth, where viewers asked the questions many entrepreneurs are wrestling with right now.

If your social strategy still revolves around follower milestones, this conversation is worth 40 minutes of your time. Davis-Ross draws on work with more than 100 creators to show what converts, what doesn’t, and where to focus first.

Stop measuring the wrong thing. Start building the kind of audience that buys.

Another bill to release Epstein files headed to House floor

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

As Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., enters his final weeks in Congress, he is one step closer to another vote on legislation related to the late sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

A discharge petition led by Massie aimed at extracting more files from the Justice Department has now received enough support to force a vote on the House floor.

Rep. John Garamendi, D-Calif., was the decisive 218th signature on the discharge petition, a procedural tool allowing rank-and-file House members to register their support for a piece of legislation and circumvent leadership to force a vote. Massie and other supporters of the effort say the bill is a bid for transparency.

Earlier this week, Massie said his pitch to his colleagues was simple.

“They could be a hero if they sign it,” he said, as he chased down the final two signatures as the House returned from recess on Monday. Rep. Adam Smith, D-Wash., signed just before Garamendi.

Massie’s time left in office is ticking down after a challenger backed by President Donald Trump ousted him in his GOP primary earlier this year.

The petition marks the second push led by Massie to require the release of the Epstein files. Last fall, Massie and Rep. Ro Khanna, D-Calif., led a discharge petition on a bill dubbed the Epstein Files Transparency Act, which directed the Justice Department to publicly release documents in its possession related to Epstein and his associate Ghislaine Maxwell.

The bill passed the House by a vote of 427-1 last November, and in the Senate a few days later. Trump signed the measure shortly afterward.

In a statement this summer, Massie said he believes the Justice Department is still withholding more than 3 million files that it is required to release under the November 2025 law and is “maintaining heavy redactions” on others.

The new bill would allow state attorneys general, local prosecutors and victims to sue the federal government for withholding such documents. It would also allow victims to privately view unredacted versions of any records that mention them.

Now that the new petition has reached 218 signatures, it must sit for seven legislative days before being called up by anyone who signed it. Last time around, Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., scheduled a House vote on the initial Epstein bill before that ripening period was over, saying it was a foregone conclusion that the legislation would reach the floor.

Other Republicans who have signed on to the petition this year include Reps. Nancy Mace, R-S.C.; Ralph Norman, R-S.C., and Brian Fitzpatrick, R-Pa., none of whom will be returning next Congress. Rep. Lauren Boebert, R-Colo., backed the initial effort last fall, but her name did not appear on the petition this time.

Allies of the issue in Congress have faced pushback from Trump. While the president suggested during his 2024 reelection campaign that he was open to declassifying some Epstein documents, Trump then shifted gears, calling them a “hoax.”

“Once you get to 218, you can never sign it,” Massie said. “There’s nobody left, no spots left. It goes straight to the floor, and then you can vote for it. But you were never part of the group that actually got it to the floor.”

Nike’s Goal With the Structure Plus? Make Stability Less Boring

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

The brand’s classic stability shoe gets the super trainer treatment with a tall stack height, lighter weight, and PEBA added to the midsole.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

How do you change runners’ conception about stability? Last December at The Running Event in San Antonio, Texas, Nike Expert PLM Ashley Campbell held up the answer: the Structure Plus.

“Stability can be boring,” she admitted. The Structure Plus makes it exciting.

The shoe follows the Vomero Plus, a super trainer version of the Vomero 18. The Structure Plus is the super-ified version of the Structure 26. Where the latter is more accessible for $140, the “plus” iteration is the original amplified in every aspect.

The Structure Plus became my chosen shoe for long runs during my 2026 Boston Marathon training cycle. Here’s how the shoe won me over on a 12-miler, and why you should consider trying a stability super trainer.

While developing the Structure Plus, Campbell said the team set out to make the super trainer “more supportive, smoother, softer, and more palatable.”

Tech-wise, you can see how Nike landed on the first three. The shoe’s platform is wider to provide support. A convex flat shape of sidewall provides stability, the density underneath the arch acting as a stabilizer to help with alignment. A dual layer of foams, like in the Vomero Plus, provides a soft, energizing ride.

In terms of making the shoe more palatable, Campbell wanted the Structure Plus to be all-inclusive. “You don’t have to be an overpronator [to run in the shoe.]”

At 42 mm in the heel and 32 mm in the forefoot, it’d make sense if you expected the shoe to be heavy and unstable. But the Structure Plus challenges these notions.

At 10.9 ounces, men’s 10, the shoe is lighter than the Structure 26, which weighs 11.3 ounces. The maximal cushioning is a layer of PEBA-based ZoomX, a soft foam that delivers high rebound (the foam is also in Nike’s premium marathon shoe, the Alphafly), and TPE-based ReactX, a firmer foam that helps stabilize your ride (the same foam found in the Vomero 18).

Despite the high stack height and max cushioning and stability components, the shoe didn’t weigh me down on my runs, nor did it cause my feet to roll. I didn’t feel like I was forced into alignment. The shoes guide you instead of directing your foot.

It was such a contrast after running in the Vomero Plus the weekend before. During those miles, it got to the point where my friends were getting concerned after I tripped over myself for the umpteenth time. My next long run was smooth, all thanks to the Structure Plus for keeping me stable.

I’m a neutral runner, but as the miles pile on, like they do on long runs, my form gets erratic due to fatigue. It’s those later miles where the Structure Plus saves me from hitting the pavement, or worse, benching myself with injury.

Concerning actual overpronators, our testers were satisfied with the support the shoe provided.

“What I liked most about the Nike Structure Plus was the strong sense of support it delivered on every run,” said tester and overpronator Julian Caiazzo. “The shoe felt stable and dependable without ever coming across as stiff or restrictive. It gave me the confidence to settle into my stride, and even with all that structure, the ride still felt smooth and enjoyable.”

Another overpronator, Katherine Thomas, admitted she usually avoids Nike shoes because of their lack of stability. She was pleasantly surprised with the support of the Structure Plus.

If there’s any reason why you should try the Structure Plus, it isn’t just that it’ll change your preconceived notion of a stability shoe; it’ll also change what you think of Nike trainers.

Like Thomas, I also avoided Nike shoes (unless I had to test them) because of their lack of stability and narrow fit. The Structure Plus doesn’t feel like a Nike shoe; there’s no squeezing on the sides of my midfoot, no pinching by my toes. And yet, I reap that quintessential Nike ride, that layer of ZoomX adding more pop to my strides. Those long runs are anything but boring slogs. I can emphatically say that Nike achieved its goal of making stability—and my runs—more exciting.

Curiosity Blog, Sols 5063-5069 Rocks and Chips

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Mars Science Laboratory Mission Team Members

Written by William Farrand, Senior Research Scientist, Space Science Institute

Earth planning date: Friday, Nov. 6, 2026

This week Curiosity has been completing its drill campaign at the “Nazca” drill hole. This is an area characterized by bedrock containing disk-shaped (or in a liberal interpretation, potato chip shaped … see linked image) crystal forms. A key objective has been to better characterize these chip-shaped features. Drill samples were delivered to the Sample Analysis for Mars (SAM) instrument. While the SAM analysis was on-going, Monday’s plan included a ChemCam LIBS observation of another crystal-form, “Ahi Veremos,” more Mastcam frames to fill out a mosaic of the Nazca near to mid-field, and environmental observations including a large dust devil survey, and horizon and atmospheric zenith movies.

Wednesday’s plan included two targeted science blocks with a LIBS depth profile of the “Colca” crystal-form and LIBS of the “Bermejo” target. A long distance Remote Micro-Imager (RMI) mosaic of rock clusters below the “Cordillera” ridge was also collected. Mastcam collected more frames of its Nazca mosaic, and more environmental observations were made.

Friday’s planning was for three sols with three targeted science blocks and a drive away from the Nazca drill site. A MAHLI mosaic of the side of the block that hosts the Nazca drill hole was planned. It was also planned to use MAHLI’s nighttime white-light and ultraviolet LED lamps for nighttime imaging of the crystal-forms. Mars’ moon, Phobos, was eclipsing the Sun and imaging of that event was prioritized. ChemCam LIBS observations were planned for each sol including a cluster of crystal forms, a resistant layer in the side of the Nazca block and a dark float rock. A long distance RMI mosaic of dark lenses beneath a planar surface on the “Mishe Mokwa” ridge was to be made. Another round of environmental observation was also planned. Finally, Friday’s drive was to take the rover back towards its southerly trending route where Curiosity would continue its trek of exploration and discovery.

With $720M round, Britain’s Fractile gets a leg up in the race for AI chips

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Fractile, a British startup developing AI chips, has raised a $720 million Series C backed by Britain’s Sovereign AI Fund with hopes of “ensuring the U.K. has a greater stake in the hardware that will underpin its future economy,” its government stated.

Alongside the likes of Axelera, Etched, and OLIX, this makes Fractile one of the top-funded startup players in the race for purpose-built AI chips. Such chips are one of several avenues being explored to reduce the cost of inference — aka using increasingly demanding AI models in production.

However, Fractile seeks to go beyond hardware with a full-stack approach that also includes a machine learning compiler to maximize the speed at which LLMs will get executed on its chips.

As a result, the company frames itself as a frontier AI company, and its latest round led by Thrive Capital, Founders Fund, Lightspeed Venture Partners, and Redpoint was priced accordingly. According to the British government, the valuation was “over £5 billion” — quite possibly $7 billion, a figure reported both by the Financial Times and a Reuters source.

Still, Fractile’s main differentiator will arguably be its hardware design. After a pivot, its approach now revolves on DRAM, the most common type of RAM, but stacked on top of each chip in such a way that it is hoping will bypass the usual trade-off between memory and capacity.

This trade-off has become more problematic with the rise of agentic AI, as companies seek to perform longer chains of tasks with large models without compromising on speed. As compute struggles to keep up, hopes are high for AI chips, despite their limited availability to date.

In a LinkedIn post, Fractile CEO Walter Goodwin wrote that “there is still a lot of work ahead” for the company, and that the new funding will enable the startup to “ship [its] first chips and systems next year.”

Singapore Airlines Unveils the ‘Largest Double Bed in the Sky’ As Part of a $1.6 Billion Redesign

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Getting a good night's sleep on a long-haul flight isn't easy, even when you're lucky enough to be flying first class. But Singapore Airlines—consistently voted as one of the best airlines in the world by CNT readers—is taking things to a whole new level, unveiling what it claims will be “the largest double bed in commercial aviation” as part of a major revamp of its long-haul cabins.

The airline announced the $1.6 billion redesign on Friday, November 6, with the first aircraft expected to enter service in the second half of 2027.

Singapore Airlines's new first class cabin

The biggest change is coming to first class, where passengers traveling together will be able to transform two adjoining seats into a double bed measuring 80 inches long and 60 inches wide—the same size as a standard queen bed in the US. The seats can also be converted into a two-person sofa, while curved sliding doors and retractable dividers will offer additional privacy.

And the luxury doesn't stop there. The new suites will feature Italian Poltrona Frau leather upholstery, personal wardrobes, dedicated dining spaces, and 32-inch 4K entertainment screens. Passengers can also expect proper bedding, including cotton sheets, duvets, a choice of pillows, and mattress toppers from German bedding specialist Paradies.

The new business class seats also come with adjoining seats, offering the option to sleep alongside a traveling companion.

Business class passengers won't be left out either. Redesigned seats will come with sliding privacy doors, improved lumbar support and fully flat beds, with selected adjoining seats offering the option to sleep alongside a traveling companion. Smart controls in both premium cabins will even remember passengers' preferred seating positions for future flights.

But what about those of us traveling farther back? Premium economy will benefit from additional personal space, adjustable leg rests, dedicated storage, and larger entertainment screens. Meanwhile, economy passengers can look forward to redesigned seats with more leg space, adjustable headrests, and new storage compartments.

The new economy class will feature more leg space, adjustable headrests, and new storage compartments.

The upgrades will be introduced gradually across Singapore Airlines's new Boeing 777-9 aircraft and 41 retrofitted Airbus A350-900 aircraft, including its ultra-long-range fleet. The latter will also receive first class cabins for the first time.

There's no confirmed date yet for when passengers will be able to try the new double beds, but one thing is certain: cuddling up at 35,000 feet has just become a little more appealing.
