10 Most Creative Traversal Abilities in Metroidvanias

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Ronald is a video game journalist with more than 10 years of experience in the industry, working on several of the most important Spanish websites.

Movement abilities in Metroidvanias are a fundamental part of their appeal, determining both the pace of exploration and our ability to interact with the surrounding world.

The genre is built on understanding spaces, their composition, and how to traverse them, making movement a crucial variable regardless of the game's perspective, mechanics, or philosophy.

As a result, the possibilities a game enables for movement along the Cartesian plane largely determine how satisfying, tense, or rewarding a campaign will be, depending on its intended purpose.

Therefore, in order to pay homage to those video games that make the best possible use of the philosophy of moving from one place to another, here are the ten most creative traversal abilities in Metroidvanias.

One of the main problems games in this genre face when creating spectacular movement abilities lies in their limitations, as they have to be balanced so that they aren't too powerful.

For this reason, Silk Soar from Hollow Knight: Silksong is a masterpiece of design. Among all the powers in Metroidvanias that let you fly upwards, it's part of the few that properly contextualize the limits of its capabilities.

Not only do you acquire it in the game's last third, unexpectedly opening paths out of nowhere, but its use is also tied to your needle, requiring the presence of a ceiling to be used, so you can't just break the map's traversal in its entirety.

In terms of creativity, it's on the spectrum's simpler side, yet it solves a major verticality imbalance by granting you a great deal of ease in exploration without making you overpowered, which is a tremendous feat.

Going in the opposite direction from the previous entry, Pipistrello and the Cursed Yoyo is a constant display of reinterpreting Metroidvanias' most common forms of movement, with Walk the Dog being the crown jewel.

The game plays with yo-yo tricks to establish its mechanics, adapting them with thematic coherence to make you feel that, more than just having powers within a video game, you're learning new maneuvers to show off.

In this particular instance, it's as simple as being able to move orthogonally across the map at a greater speed, but this is maximized thanks to the bounce pads distributed throughout all areas that change your direction and make the ability the key to numerous puzzles.

Pipistrello and the Cursed Yoyo is brimming with ideas magnificently adapted to its setting, making it by far one of the most inventive Metroidvanias to come out this decade.

Well Dweller breaks one of the genre's cardinal rules by not including a double jump at any point in its story, though it compensates with a Slingshot mechanic that is simply mesmerizing.

The title allows you to throw your weapon and embed it in any surface to use it as a springboard and jump higher than usual, encouraging inquisitive and creative exploration in a truly compelling way.

Most of the game's movement is based on the biomes you find yourself in, whether in the form of bubbles, air currents, or elevators, and it works precisely because this direct yet versatile ability adapts to any situation.

Of course, it leaves you without any means of defense, but, like Kratos's Leviathan Axe, Well Dweller's matchstick flies back to you when you need it, which also leads to spectacular and almost cinematic moments when transitioning from platforming to combat.

If you're looking for games that subvert your expectations, try these eight Metroidvanias that totally reinvent themselves halfway through.

In terms of inventiveness, Animal Well is a couple of notches above the rest of the genre, with all its progression tools being as unique as they're rewarding.

Since the title is more puzzle-based than platforming-focused, most of its abilities are proportional to said distribution, but the Disc is a versatile component that you can use both to solve environmental puzzles and to traverse in seemingly impossible ways.

Throwing the frisbee and timing its trajectory to hop on and soar through the air never gets old, both because of how mechanically satisfying it is and because of all the paths mastering this skill opens up.

Animal Well is a sandbox where most of its castles are built through iteration and experimentation, but fortunately, you have the Disc on your side to make the journey faster, smoother, and more enjoyable.

Despite my reservations about Constance due to its short length and limited side content, its movement mechanics are some of the smoothest I've ever seen in the genre, with Paint Dive being the standout.

It functions as both a dash and a dematerialization of the character, allowing her to blend into the environment and pass through obstacles and hazards as a graceful mass of purple paint that seamlessly integrates with the surroundings.

In its abstract form, it's simply a directional boost, yet its execution is elevated by the environments, which force you to use the ability in increasingly diverse ways, ensuring it's more than just a regular push.

Given that platforming is Constance's forte, Paint Dive plays a prominent role throughout the entire campaign, making it a proportionally more important element of the game than many of the other abilities on this list.

From classic Resident Evil to Dark Souls, these are eight Non-Metroidvanias that heavily influenced the genre.

Returning to what I mentioned about Silk Soar and the problems of having very powerful abilities, the Crystal Arrows in Aeterna Noctis take the conversation to another level by granting you the power to teleport to wherever you aim them.

Certainly, they're used in many of the most excruciatingly difficult platforming sections the genre has ever seen, but also in one of the deepest movement systems.

Being able to chain together consecutive teleportations while fighting and traversing Aeterna's world quickly becomes a delight, especially once you get used to them and feel you've mastered their immeasurable offensive and defensive potential.

While Aeterna Noctis' structure doesn't fully capitalize on the Crystal Arrows, they remain magnificent and satisfying, surprisingly without ever breaking the game's sequence.

Castlevania: Symphony of the Night dares to take its most unusual ability and only make it necessary for a limited number of sections, yet the Mist Form remains an unforgettable memory for anyone who plays the game.

Becoming an incorporeal entity to pass through bars is the kind of power fantasy that only a character like Alucard can materialize, with the added significance of having achieved something so astonishing thirty years ago.

Although there aren't many mandatory instances where you must use it, the ability is a major surprise when you unlock it, primarily when it becomes an incredibly powerful toxic weapon that allows you to both move freely and eliminate all enemies in your path.

Years have passed, and I don't think I've ever been so impacted by unlocking a new ability, which only speaks to Castlevania: Symphony of the Night's timeless greatness.

Of all the innovations that Ori and the Blind Forest brought to the genre during the formative years when its heirs were still establishing themselves, Bash is undoubtedly the most prominent.

The ability to bounce off both enemies and projectiles to move omnidirectionally and reach new heights and locations is absurdly incredible from the very first minute to the last.

The mechanic was significantly improved in the sequel, yet its stellar and unprecedented appearance in the franchise's first title is a core memory of the genre, particularly due to its use in combat to stylize every fight sequence.

I must admit Ori and the Blind Forest isn't among my favorite Metroidvanias, though that doesn't stop me from acknowledging that, in terms of uniqueness, there's no other ability that truly compares to Bash.

If you're already tired of 2D side-scrolling games, I recommend you read about these ten best first-person Metroidvanias, ranked.

By 1994 standards, the existence of a video game of Super Metroid's caliber seems absolutely insane, as years have passed and the game remains so fresh and enjoyable that it's astonishing how ahead of its time it was.

People usually praise the Shinespark or the Morph Ball, which are masterful abilities by any measure, but I have to admit that what truly amazed me was the Grapple Beam and how wonderfully it utilizes Zebes' layout.

You spend much of the journey wondering what those strange cross-shaped blocks are for, and when you discover that they allow you to aim a beam that lets you swing freely, spin, and propel yourself at top speed, you can't help but be blown away.

Whether you played it back then or today, Super Metroid surprises you with its mastery at every turn, and there are so many ways I could have included it on this list that all I can do is stand up and applaud.

I'm going to lean towards modernity to define the top spot on this list and praise the work of MIO: Memories in Orbit, a contemporary masterpiece of the genre whose scale is truly breathtaking.

There are many reasons to say this, but unlocking the Striders, tendrils into legs and arms that can grab any surface to walk on walls, was the moment in its campaign when I realized I wasn't just playing a good Metroidvania.

Even though their use is limited by a stamina bar, thinking about the titanic effort it must have taken to craft each room of its vast map based on the eventual access to this omnipotent movement tool makes me dizzy.

Not only does it have fluid and pristine animation with perfect sound design, but it also blends seamlessly with exploration and platforming mechanics to make everything related to movement a true gift for players.

I've had many surprising moments with movement mechanics in the genre, yet the Striders in MIO: Memories in Orbit are unique while also being functional, powerful, and visually appealing, which is an elevated balance that's extremely difficult to achieve.

From the best bosses to essential lore explanations, these are eight Metroidvanias that hide their best content behind the most unexpected paths.

As AI Gets Cheaper, the Real Business Opportunity Is Shifting. Here’s Where the Value Is Moving.

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

As models become cheaper and more interchangeable, more value may move to the companies that own the customer relationship, workflow and distribution.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

For the past few years, the AI industry has operated on a fairly simple assumption: Better models would create more valuable companies. The enormous investment going into chips, data centers and foundational models has largely been built around that idea.

China is starting to test the assumption.

In October, Reuters reported that China’s STAR50 technology index had fallen 40% in less than four months, wiping out around $480 billion in market value. AI-related stocks had grown to represent 42% of Chinese market capitalization and 57% of trading turnover across mainland China and Hong Kong.

Some of this is simply what happens when valuations run ahead of financial performance. But I think there is a more interesting change happening underneath the market correction. AI is continuing to become more capable while the cost of accessing that capability is falling and competition between model providers is increasing.

For businesses using AI, that is very good news. For every company investing billions to build the underlying technology, the economics may be more complicated.

The AI industry could create enormous economic value without the companies building foundational models capturing most of it.

The pace of model development is still extraordinary, but the gap between the strongest models is getting smaller.

The 2026 Stanford AI Index found that Anthropic, xAI, Google and OpenAI were all within 25 points of each other on the Arena leaderboard in March. Chinese models are also closing the gap, with Stanford describing the performance difference between leading U.S. and Chinese models as having “effectively closed.”

That matters because when several providers can complete the same task well, the buying decision starts to change. Businesses begin looking beyond which model is technically the best. Cost matters. Reliability matters. Speed matters. The ability to use a model inside an existing workflow matters.

I have seen this change in how we approach AI internally. When we started building more AI agents and automations, it was easy to focus heavily on the model. We would look at which model produced the best output and build around it.

As the technology has improved, the question has become much more practical: What is the cheapest reliable way to complete this particular task?

The best model for analyzing a complex problem may be unnecessary for extracting information from a document. A cheaper model may work perfectly well for a repetitive classification task. Some workflows can even use several models, depending on what needs to happen at each step.

Once models become components rather than the product itself, the economics of AI start to look different.

There is a tendency to assume that the companies responsible for the biggest technological advances will also capture most of the financial value those advances create. Technology markets do not always work that way.

Consider what happens when the cost of completing an AI task falls significantly. A customer service team can process more conversations. A developer can run more coding agents. A marketing team can analyse more campaigns and build more experiments. A small business can automate administrative work that previously required another employee or an external service.

The economic value of all those activities can increase even while the amount paid to the model provider for each task decreases.

This is why aggressive competition between model providers is so important. As capabilities converge and prices fall, some of the value created by AI moves downstream to the companies applying it.

For a business owner, cheaper models can mean better margins or the ability to automate a process that was previously too expensive. For a software company, it can make an AI feature viable for thousands of smaller customers rather than only large businesses.

The technology improves while the cost of using it falls. That can be great for the AI economy without necessarily being great for every company building AI infrastructure.

This also changes where founders should look for defensibility.

A model can have a significant technical lead and lose part of that lead within a few months. Building a long-term company around access to one particular model therefore looks increasingly difficult.

Customer relationships are harder to replicate. So are distribution, proprietary context and a deep understanding of how work is actually performed. This is especially relevant in business software.

Most customers are not trying to buy artificial intelligence. They are trying to get something done. They want to respond to a customer, prepare a proposal, reconcile an expense, schedule employees, create a campaign or understand why revenue changed. The model is only one part of delivering that outcome.

This is similar to a shift I have seen as we have become more AI-native as a company. The useful part is rarely the model in isolation. The difficult work is connecting AI to the right data, giving it enough context, building the workflow around it and deciding where a person still needs to intervene.

That business context becomes increasingly valuable as the underlying intelligence becomes easier to access. A company that already understands a customer’s workflow can potentially apply each improvement in AI much faster than a new entrant that first has to understand the customer.

I think that is where some of the more durable advantages in the application layer will come from.

There is another issue that founders building AI products need to understand early: Usage now has a cost.

Traditional SaaS created an attractive economic model because the marginal cost of another customer using a feature was usually very small. Whether somebody opened a dashboard five times or 50 times did not dramatically change the infrastructure economics.

Generating documents, analyzing conversations, reasoning across data or running autonomous workflows consumes compute each time the task is performed. This means a customer using an AI product heavily can also become significantly more expensive to serve.

It changes what good growth looks like. A team can celebrate that customers generated one million AI actions this month, but that number has little meaning on its own. If every action costs more than the value it creates, greater adoption simply increases the problem.

When I look at an AI workflow now, I am increasingly interested in cost per successful outcome.

How much does it cost to complete the task? How often does the AI succeed without intervention? How much human work remains? Does the task create revenue, save meaningful time or improve the customer experience?

Those are much more useful questions than how many prompts or tokens were consumed.

This also puts pressure on the subscription model that has dominated software for the past two decades. SaaS largely trained customers to pay for access. Choose a plan, pay a monthly fee and use the software.

AI introduces a meaningful variable cost into parts of the product. That does not mean every software company needs to abandon subscriptions. But it does mean businesses need a clearer understanding of the relationship between usage, customer value and cost. For some products, a traditional subscription may still work perfectly well. Others may move towards credits, usage allowances or a combination of subscription and consumption pricing.

What matters is that pricing and infrastructure economics cannot be separated.

If heavier usage makes a product less profitable, the pricing model eventually has to reflect that. If inference costs continue to fall, the opposite can happen: Previously expensive capabilities can be included more broadly, making the product more useful without damaging margins.

This is one reason falling model prices could have a much bigger effect on software than many people expect.

The correction in China does not make me less optimistic about AI. If anything, it reinforces why I think the opportunity for companies applying AI is becoming larger.

More competition between foundational models should produce better models, lower prices and more choices for businesses building on top of them. Stanford’s data already shows the performance gap narrowing, which means competition increasingly moves towards cost, reliability and usefulness in specific domains.

Founders should design around that direction of travel. Avoid making a single model the entire competitive advantage. Understand the workflows where the business has genuine expertise. Measure the cost of completing tasks rather than simply celebrating AI usage. Build systems that can take advantage of cheaper or better models when they become available.

Most importantly, focus on what happens after the model produces an answer.

The biggest business opportunities may not sit inside the model at all. They may sit in the customer relationship, the proprietary context, the workflow and the distribution required to turn intelligence into something useful.

That is why China’s correction is worth watching even for founders who have no interest in Chinese technology stocks. The important signal is not that AI is losing value. It is that creating value with AI and capturing that value are two different things.

As intelligence becomes cheaper and more widely available, that distinction will become increasingly important for anyone building a business around it.

For the past few years, the AI industry has operated on a fairly simple assumption: Better models would create more valuable companies. The enormous investment going into chips, data centers and foundational models has largely been built around that idea.

China is starting to test the assumption.

In October, Reuters reported that China’s STAR50 technology index had fallen 40% in less than four months, wiping out around $480 billion in market value. AI-related stocks had grown to represent 42% of Chinese market capitalization and 57% of trading turnover across mainland China and Hong Kong.

We'll be in your inbox every morning Monday-Saturday with all the day’s top business news, inspiring stories, best advice and exclusive reporting from Entrepreneur.

How ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Dominated the Box Office: GKIDS Chief on IMAX’s Impact and the Growing Market for Foreign-Language Movies

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

“Godzilla Minus Zero” almost pulled of a Godzilla-times-three on its opening weekend, earning $31.6 million in North America to nearly triple the domestic debut of its 2023 predecessor “Godzilla Minus One.” With that Godzilla math, it destroyed the record for the biggest North American debut for a live-action foreign language film.

It was a crowning moment for the speciality distributor GKIDS. Founded in 2008, the label primarily built itself as a distributor of foreign language animated features, most notably with its acquisition of the North American distribution rights to the Studio Ghibli library. As a day-one employee of GKIDS, and its president since 2017, Dave Jesteadt has helped expand the stateside audience for subtitled features. After a 2025 acquisition by Toho, the Japanese film industry giant behind “Godzilla,” the reach of GKIDS is wider than ever with “Minus Zero,” which drew a box office debut that some studio tentpoles this year would envy. Related Stories Democrats Won. When Do Hollywood Crews Get Back to Work? Attend, the First Marketplace Connecting Filmmakers Directly to Theater Owners, Finally Launches Today

“Anecdotally, so many people I know are like, ‘I took my dad this movie and he doesn’t go to any subtitle movies,'” Jesteadt told IndieWire. “Godzilla brings us together. If we’re spreading the gospel of foreign cinema, and Godzilla is a vehicle to get people to engage with great films and deep themes, then I’m all for it.”

After a flashy premiere at the New York Film Festival and a historic cume from IMAX and other premium large-format auditoriums, Jesteadt digs into how GKIDS helped “Minus Zero” come to America.

The following interview has been edited and condensed for clarity and length.

IndieWire: “Godzilla Minus One” was seen as a breakout success in 2023, with $57 million in North America. What lessons did GKIDS take away from Toho‘s release of “Minus One” in building toward this big opening for “Minus Zero”?

Dave Jesteadt: GKIDS joined the Toho Group in October 2024, but our story of working together on this began before that, back in 2023. GKIDS had “The Boy and the Heron” from Hayao Miyazaki and Studio Ghibli in theaters just a week after “Godzilla Minus One” opened, which was being handled by Toho’s L.A. office at the time. We got to know that team because we had these two films in theaters, sort of competing for similar audience profiles.

In terms of lessons that both sides took, it’s that you can really drive conversation with theaters as a source of celebration and community. When we joined the Toho Group, we took in the team that worked on “Minus One.” We re-released “Shin Godzilla” in 2025 as a test for bringing modern Japanese “Godzilla” films to theaters. Then the planning for “Minus Zero” really began in terms of how we could build and improve on the audience that “Minus One” already found.

Did “Minus Zero” get a bigger marketing spend stateside? How did you consider which people to reach to expand the audience for a Japanese “Godzilla” film?

I can’t give a specific number, but I’d say that we’re very efficient and very mindful of how we can reach the audience in the most direct way possible. For this film, we have the great benefit of our colleagues at Toho International owning and operating Godzilla.com, overseeing merchandise licensing. They have direct communication with the “Godzilla” fan as they exist in America.

But the other goal is to reach, as “Minus One” did, an audience that hadn’t engaged in Japanese “Godzilla” films. We also balanced reaching that core “Godzilla” fan and making sure that they turn out with this desire to continue the story from “Minus One,” reminding people that these films from director Takashi Yamazaki really speak to broader feelings today. They have prestigious bona fides through playing film festivals and great critical reviews. We’re leading with the quality of the film, making people feel that this is a very safe choice — not just if you like spectacle, but that this is going to be one of the best films of the year.

The New York Film Festival premiere played as a flex, especially after a stretch where Hollywood would premiere summer blockbusters at Cannes, draw mediocre reviews, and then have to weather weeks of bad buzz for their expensive tentpoles. With the NYFF premiere, “Minus Zero” was positioned as a prestige object. Is that the aura you were trying to cultivate around it?

I think so. Godzilla has been one of the most famous characters in the world. There’s a big audience that you can potentially address. But the “Minus” franchise takes it into more realistic, human-centered stories. That differentiates it. It is a risk to go to a film festival; I think that has been pretty well-argued. It is a risk anytime you want to share something with the world early before you open, especially when you have such high hopes about how it’s going to go. On the other hand, this was a really important way to demonstrate confidence.

“Minus Zero” opened in more than 3,200 theaters, roughly 1,000 more theaters than “Minus One.” How much did you expand its presence in premium large-format auditoriums?

“Minus Zero” was the first Japanese production filmed for IMAX. That was a central part of the very first announcement and it was a defining part of the marketing campaign. IMAX mentioned it was the highest-indexed opening gross on IMAX screens for an opening weekend in the U.S. We had Godzilla Day on November 3, with IMAX early access screenings globally. So, PLFs definitely played a big presence: Imax, 4DX, Dbox, and others. That’s very unusual for a subtitled foreign film.

It speaks to the expectations for high quality that audiences brought. The PLF story is a way to reinforce production quality. Everyone is saying, “You have to see it in the biggest screen possible with the best sound.” I’ve seen many, many beautiful foreign films. They often end up playing circuits to audiences that are very passionate, but don’t have access to those kind of screens. So it’s a privilege to be able to play a film on the kind of screen that’s typically reserved for the biggest mainstream titles.

How long are you staying in IMAX and PLFs?

It depends on the market. We’re definitely here for the rest of this week. But as “The Odyssey” and other titles have demonstrated, there’s such draw to these moments. It can even create this quest to see something in multiple formats. But as we head into the Thanksgiving frame, the Hollywood competition is obviously huge. We’re realistic about that, even while we’re trying to encourage people to see it as soon and as often as they can.

The reported statistic that more than 70 percent of grosses came from PLFs seems a bit of a double-edged sword to me. It speaks to a high regard for the film among consumers, but it also might indicate that it has a specialized audience that may only want to see the movie in a premium format. Is that a consideration GKIDS takes in, particularly when other titles are taking those auditoriums in the coming weeks?

Obviously, PLFs are time-sensitive. It’s not just about performance, but also about new titles coming in. We’re up against the biggest studios in the world. I’m obviously thrilled that we were able to get so many and I’m thrilled that people saw it that way. I also encourage everyone to just see it in regular theaters too. The way that it was mastered and the way that director Yamazaki made the film is so you won’t have a bad experience in any house watching it. But with any of these core fandoms, there’s a lot of interest in seeing something “the right way.” I’m happy for that, but then the hope is they tell their friends and we continue the growth from there.

You’ve been with GKIDS since its inception and have played a major part in growing the American audience for foreign language cinema. What do you think has changed in the marketplace that has allowed a Japanese “Godzilla” film to now find a much broader audience in the West than it could find before?

There’s no doubt that the quality of the films really helps. “Minus One” was a fantastic film. It won the Oscar for visual effects and became an audience darling. But really, the thing that’s changed the most is that younger generations are much more open to subtitled and foreign cinema than they would have been 10 or 20 years ago. We’ve entered an age of opportunity where what can constitute a hit film is really broadening. That gives me a lot of optimism compared to negative stories about the overall economics of the industry. GKIDS has been bringing subtitled animated movies and anime to audiences for almost 20 years and seeing that audience grow naturally. At first, dubs were essential; now, they’re not.

Though GKIDS isn’t producing these films, I’m curious if, as a distributor, you think that Japanese productions like “Minus Zero” are now being developed and produced with more of a global audience in mind.

The films are still intrinsically Japanese and are basically impossible to separate from the cultural tradition of “Godzilla” in Japan, as well as the overall Japanese film industry. On the other hand, there’s no doubt that the property has the ability to reach a global audience. The popularity with the “Minus” series is really a focus on quality, and how its storytelling is rooted in a time and a place that feels authentically Japanese. Although it deals with a post-war moment in Japanese history, it is incredibly contemporary in terms of the thoughts and preoccupations of a global audience.

So I don’t think there’s necessarily a change in focus. I would say that the audience and the director are in a much more direct conversation. On social media now, everything is auto-translated. You can see real-time active conversation between Japanese fans and viewers commenting about the film with American or Spanish or European audience. Everyone is able to share their thoughts or opinions about some of these themes in a way that brings local perspective to the table. By creating something that feel like it’s created for a specific audience, ironically, you end up making it feel more universal, just because it feels more lived in.

Do you think we are headed to a world where a Japanese “Godzilla” film can outgross a Western “Godzilla” film in North America?

I don’t really think about that. I’m very competitive against a lot of people, but the “Godzilla” franchise has benefited from having a lot of flavors to it. By building that big tent, it ends up being a character that has endured.

“Godzilla Minus Zero” is now in theaters.

Rand Fishkin’s Seattle game studio serves up first trailer for ‘The Snackbar at the End of the World’

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

by Kurt Schlosser on Nov 11, 2026 at 11:21 amNovember 11, 2026 at 11:21 am

If the eventual release of “The Snackbar at the End of the World” is a full-on meal for video game fans, news this week of the game’s first trailer should at least suffice as an appetizer.

Seattle-based Snackbar Studio, co-founded by SparkToro founder Rand Fishkin and his wife, writer Geraldine DeRuiter, unveiled its debut title and Steam page on Tuesday. The hand-drawn narrative adventure RPG puts players in the role of a chef trying to save a struggling family restaurant in a magical version of 1960s Italy.

The studio now plans to launch the game late next year, after originally targeting the second half of 2026. Snackbar raised $2.15 million from 38 angel investors two years ago, and the project began four years ago, according to Fishkin. The team includes five co-founders (Fishkin, DeRuiter, game designer Nicolas Kraj, lead programmer Miriam Cabrera and art director Francesco Mazza) plus about 10 contractors.

In the studio’s description on Steam, players forage for ingredients in the “forbidden wilds” outside town, battling explosive tomatoes, chaotic zucchini and vendetta-driven poultry. They then cook dishes back at the restaurant before a tyrannical mayor known as the Sindaco can shut it down. The game mixes combat and stealth with cooking mini-games based on real-world recipes.

The trailer is set to “’O surdato ’nnammurato,” a classic Neapolitan song performed by professional musicians recruited by the studio’s music director. Fishkin said he and DeRuiter both cried the first time they heard it.

Fishkin is best known in Seattle tech circles as co-founder of SEO software company Moz and audience research startup SparkToro, and he more recently launched Alertmouse, a tool for tracking online mentions. DeRuiter, a writer and author, is Snackbar’s head writer.

In a LinkedIn post this week, Fishkin recalled telling DeRuiter during the COVID lockdown that he wanted to fulfill a childhood dream of making a video game. Her reply: “Don’t wait. Do it now. I’ll do it with you.”

In the same post, he said the game’s themes include found family and fighting fascism “even when there’s no hope you’ll win.” He called it a love letter to DeRuiter’s small family village in the mountains outside Naples, to that era of Italy, to video games, and to the many people both of their families have lost in recent years.

Moments like hearing that song come often in the work, Fishkin said, such as when a new feature clicks or a new animation brings DeRuiter’s characters to life. He has always loved art without being good at any form of it. “I may not be able to create art, but I can enable it,” he said. “What a wonderful feeling.”

There have been lows, too. The team spent its initial 18 months on the project with a contract agency that didn’t work out, losing money with nothing to show for it. Fishkin is glad they kept going, “even when it felt financially irresponsible.”

The game is entirely human-made, and Snackbar’s no-AI stance is firm. “Geraldine hates AI with the fury of a thousand suns,” Fishkin said. He once suggested, just once, that she use Google’s AI Mode to find a synonym, and she was mad at him for the rest of a six-hour flight home from Philadelphia. Even if players, the rest of the team and the games media were fine with AI (and they’re not), DeRuiter alone would make it a no-go, he said.

Still ahead are 10 to 12 months of finishing the game’s content, playtesting, bug fixing, marketing and launch prep. Fishkin called it daunting, but said team members have done it many times before, “even though it’s our first rodeo.”

Fishkin is still juggling Snackbar with SparkToro and Alertmouse, and describes his role at the studio as mostly oversight and management. SparkToro is growing again, and Alertmouse recently hit a big milestone in annual renewals with high retention. He and his Alertmouse co-founders are putting more money into the company to fund growth.

Balancing the three roles is a challenge, he said, but only rarely completely overwhelming. “Like this week.”

In just a few weeks, AWS re:Invent brings advanced and expert-level sessions on the services you build on every day, from serverless and containers to databases and developer tools. Go hands-on with new launches the same week they’re announced, and hear directly from the engineers who built them. Experience the depth you won’t find anywhere else.

Seattle studio led by Big Fish founder raises more cash for mobile battler ‘Mutant Forge’

Scott Colom elected Mississippi’s first Black senator since Reconstruction

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Democrat Scott Colom has unseated Republican Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith to become Mississippi’s first Black senator since Reconstruction.

The result is a stunning flip for Democrats in a deep-red state where they last won a Senate election in 1982, and it brings the Senate Democratic Caucus to at least 55 seats in the next Congress.

Colom was leading Hyde-Smith 49.2 percent to 48.7 percent — or by just over 4,600 votes out of more than 960,000 cast — when The Associated Press called the race Wednesday, a little over a week after Election Day. Ty Pinkins, the 2024 Democratic Senate nominee who ran as an independent this year, had 2 percent.

In recent days, as absentee and provisional ballots were counted, Colom had all but declared victory, repeatedly saying that Hyde-Smith had no path to victory.

“I’ll work every day in the U.S. Senate to build a future that generations of Mississippians can be just as proud to call home,” he posted Monday on social media.

With his victory, Colom joins several other Democrats who flipped seats in deep-red states, including Texas’ James Talarico, Kansas’ Adam Hamilton and South Carolina’s Annie Andrews. Democrats have so far flipped eight Senate seats to win control of the chamber, overcoming a difficult Senate map and giving them their largest majority in the chamber since 2011.

That majority could expand further if Democrats flip the Senate seat in Alaska, where former Rep. Mary Peltola narrowly leads Republican incumbent Dan Sullivan.

Republicans have dominated Senate elections in Mississippi in recent decades. The last Democrat to be elected senator in the Magnolia State was John C. Stennis, who won his sixth full and final term in 1982. Colom will be the third Black man to serve as a Mississippi senator after Republicans Hiram Revels and Blanche Bruce during Reconstruction.

While Revels and Bruce, who left office in 1881, were elected by the state Legislature, Colom will be the first Black senator elected directly by voters.

Colom has served as a district attorney for four counties in northeast Mississippi since 2016. President Joe Biden nominated him for a federal district judgeship in 2022, but Hyde-Smith effectively blocked his nomination by not returning a so-called blue slip, a Senate Judiciary Committee tradition that lets senators derail district judge nominees in their home states.

Senate Minority Leader Charles E. Schumer said last week that he’d had Colom in his sights for several years as a potential Senate candidate, including for a race against the state’s senior senator, Republican Roger Wicker.

“I had Scott Colom and his father come to my house, and I served them a nice little brunch or something. I said, ‘You got to run,’” the New York Democrat recalled to MS NOW. “They were smarter than me. This was four years ago. They said, ‘We don’t want to run this time, but we know that Hyde-[Smith], the incumbent, is so weak, we could beat her.’”

Still, the Mississippi race didn’t capture as much national attention as other Senate contests until the closing weeks of the campaign. A trio of Black senators, Angela Alsobrooks of Maryland, Lisa Blunt Rochester of Delaware and Cory Booker of New Jersey, all campaigned with Colom throughout the summer and fall. Key national figures, including former President Bill Clinton, former Vice President Kamala Harris and former Transportation Secretary Pete Buttigieg, also stumped for him in October.

Colom’s campaign engaged voters with quirky ads and unconventional forms of outreach, including meeting with people lining up to attend a Trump rally. One ad that went viral played on Hyde Smith saying earlier this year that people had “so many proteins to choose from” when asked about rising beef prices.

“Pick another protein?” says a man in the ad. “I’d rather pick another senator instead.”

Major Democratic outside groups made a late play in the state, with WinSenate, which is affiliated with Senate Democrats’ top super PAC, investing $2.2 million. The Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee spent $500,000 on a coordinated ad buy late last month.

The pro-Republican Senate Leadership Fund dropped $1.7 million on the race, and Trump headlined a rally for Hyde-Smith on Oct. 26.

Avelo Brings First Commercial Flights to McKinney

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Avelo operated its inaugural flight from McKinney National Airport in McKinney, Texas, on Wednesday.

Avelo Flight 1848, operated with a Boeing 737-800, was the first commercial airliner service from the airport to Fort Myers, Florida.

“We target the secondary airports of very large metropolitan areas where we can offer a different experience to the customers,” Avelo founder and CEO Andrew Levy said in an interview with AirlineGeeks.

McKinney is the third airport in the Dallas/Fort Worth Metroplex to offer commercial service, with Dallas Love Field and Dallas/Forth Worth International Airport being the other two.

“For a lot of folks we’re going to be closer and a more convenient drive. And hopefully they’ll take advantage of that,” said McKinney airport director Ken Carley.

Carley said the airline has been wanting to be in the market since 2018. McKinney is the first new airport in Texas in 20 years to be certified for commercial air service.

“We had to build all this infrastructure because it did not exist. There was nothing on this side of the airport. So it’s not just this building, it’s the ramp and the taxi lane and all of that stuff, the taxiway that connects to the runway system. So it was a building, all the infrastructure, and meeting all those requirements,” Carley said.

McKinney is about 30 miles northeast of Dallas in Collin County. Levy said the area is growing like crazy.

“What we’re going to offer people who live close to this airport is a very different experience than getting on the highways and driving all the way to DFW or going down to Love Field,” he said.

The Texas-based airline will serve 11 destinations from McKinney: Las Vegas, Tampa, Orlando, Fort Myers, Sarasota/Bradenton, Nashville, West Palm Beach, New Orleans, Denver, Atlanta, and Fort Lauderdale.

“I think it’s phenomenal that Avelo has announced additional routes before they’ve even started service. That’s a testament, I think, to what they’re seeing in the response they’re getting from for bookings. So they think it’s a strong market,” Carley said.

Levy said the approach was to offer a lot of selections and get a better sense of the market.

“All the markets we’re serving have massive proven demand from this area. And so of course, the trick for us is just to get people to know we’re here and then give us a try,” Levy said.

McKinney is a crew base for Avelo, its fifth, with pilots, flight attendants, airport operations personnel, and aircraft technicians, about 150 people behind the operation, Levy said.

“The demand will dictate how we grow, if we grow, and how fast we grow. This is going to be based on, do customers like the service? Does it resonate with them? Are we serving a need that exists every other place we’ve been? We don’t have any trouble filling up airplanes. I think this market is just going to do great, but time will tell, and the consumer will let us know,” Levy said.

“Our focus is on building a business that’s sustainable for the long term,” he added. “And that means everything we do has to work. It’s got to work well.”

Avelo currently offers service from DFW to New Haven, Connecticut. Levy said that service will continue but could move to McKinney at some point.

She Trained Through Alaskan Winters to Run a Sub-3 Marathon. These Strategies Helped Her Fight the Cold and Stay Consistent.

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Snow, ice, and short days didn’t stop Michelle Baxter from regular runs outdoors.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

When winter arrives, it’s tempting to put your running goals on hold until the temperatures warm up, the days get longer, and the roads clear. Cold temperatures make it harder to leave a warm bed, while snow and ice can turn a familiar route into a much slower—and more slippery—run.

For months at a time, Michelle Baxter’s running routes in Anchorage, Alaska, were covered in packed snow and ice. After a snowstorm, plows could take days to reach every neighborhood, and by then, cars had compressed the fallen snow onto the roads. Even after the plows came through, the slick surface often remained.

Baxter—now an RRCA- and V.02-certified run coach—spent 15 years living in Alaska and training through winters like these, figuring out how to keep running through harsh, unmotivating conditions.

She trained for 12 marathons in that time, continuing to build fitness year after year. Eventually, she even achieved her most ambitious running goal: breaking three hours in the marathon. After eight years of attempting to break the barrier—and a heartbreakingly close 3:01:14 at Humpy’s Marathon in Anchorage in August 2021—Baxter finally ran a 2:58:24 at the California International Marathon in December 2021. She has since lowered her PR to 2:56:57 at the Indianapolis Monumental Marathon.

Your winter running routes may not be covered in snow and ice for six months straight, but chances are you’ll face your own challenges as the temperature drops in the coming weeks and months. The good news: Baxter’s experience can help you navigate tough terrain and make the most of your training.

Here’s how you can put her lessons to work this winter so when the ice thaws, you’re ready to run your fastest race yet.

What you wear can make or break a winter run, Baxter says. When you’re too cold, too warm, or worried about taking a spill around every tight corner, it’s hard to focus on any other aspect of the run. Baxter spent years figuring out what works, and her gear advice starts with two essentials: finding reliable footwear and appropriate layers for the conditions.

In Anchorage, finding reliable footing was Baxter’s biggest winter challenge. She initially resisted buying studded shoes, assuming they wouldn’t make much difference. But once she tried them, she was surprised by how much more confident she felt in every step.

For packed snow, Baxter often wore regular trainers fitted with screws in the outsoles, which she added herself.

On sheer ice, Baxter preferred shoes made with built-in carbide studs; she swears by the Icebug brand. “You can wear these shoes and make a 90-degree turn and feel so confident running on ice—like sheer ice,” Baxter says. “They were a game-changer for me.”

You may not encounter sheets of ice on your regular running routes, but consider the conditions you encounter most often and look for an option suited to them.

Now living in Minnesota, Baxter says she reaches for studded shoes if she expects at least half of a run to cover potentially snowy or icy ground. She notes that while the shoes may be slightly less comfortable than your normal trainers, protecting your body from harm is the number-one priority during the winter months of training.

“I just think it’s worth it to feel confident running on the slippery surfaces and not risk injuring yourself,” she says.

One of the most common winter gear mistakes Baxter sees runners make is overdressing. It’s easy to look out the window, see cold weather, and pull on every layer you own in an effort to stay warm on the run. But remember you’ll warm up once you start moving.

Baxter’s rule of thumb echoes the advice of other run coaches: Start your run feeling a little chilly. After about 10 minutes, you should warm up and feel comfortable for the rest of the run.

Don’t forget to consider the wind, too; Baxter generally recommends dressing for the wind chill temperature. She recalls learning this lesson during one particularly windy Alaska run when she dressed for the ambient temperature rather than the wind chill temperature and ended up feeling sick from potential hypothermia.

If you’re unsure where to start when it comes to gearing up for a winter run, use our temperature-based layering guide, then pay attention to how you feel after each run. Were you still cold after warming up? Did you get sweaty and uncomfortable? Use your responses to adjust your layers on your next run.

For Baxter, keeping her legs warm as temperatures approached subzero was a recurring problem. She first turned to wearing multiple pairs of tights, which helped with the cold, but the layers restricted her movement. Eventually, she found a thick, fleece-lined pair of running tights that solved the problem, recommending them to other runners for temperatures ranging from 15 all the way down to minus 10 degrees Fahrenheit.

She made a similar change on top: Instead of wearing a base layer, fleece layer, and wind layer, she found a high-quality warm jacket to wear over a base layer.

Your biggest frustration might be cold hands or wet feet. For these issues, Baxter suggests putting hand warmers in your gloves, running-specific wool socks, and a waterproof shoe.

For visibility during the winter months, Baxter wears a lighted vest. She began treating it as a non-negotiable in her running wardrobe after noticing, as a driver, how much easier it was to see runners wearing blinking lights rather than simple reflective clothing items.

“Investing in a few quality pieces [of winter gear], I think you’re just going to enjoy your run more,” Baxter says. “You’re going to be safer and more comfortable.”

Even with the right layers, leaving your warm house and getting out the door often proves difficult when it’s cold and dark.

Baxter understands the struggle better than most. Anchorage gets less than six hours of daylight during the darkest winter months, and the city’s winter high temps hover around 25 degrees Fahrenheit.

So, how did she keep getting out there? “Running just became a habit of mine,” she says. “It’s how I start my day. I get up, I go for a run.” Even during peak winter in Alaska, Baxter often ran seven days a week simply because it was a part of her daily routine.

Baxter says each individual needs to find what helps them keep going, whether that’s a future goal, a training partner to run alongside, or a plan that lets you check off workouts.

For Baxter, her motivation and discipline to make running a habit often came from looking ahead.

“I just know how important those base miles are during the winter,” she says. “So I know I need to keep putting in the work, keep doing some workouts, because it’s only going to pay off down the road.”

If you have a spring marathon, a summer 10K, or another goal in mind, remind yourself how your winter routine fits into that bigger picture. You can also adjust your expectations for the season: Baxter says it’s okay to scale back during winter if that’s what works for you. A simple maintenance routine can help you arrive at your next training cycle ready to build.

The point is, doing something is better than doing nothing.

A goal months away isn’t always enough to get you out the door on a cold morning. Baxter suggests finding something closer to look forward to, too.

Consider signing up for a winter race, even if it isn’t one of your main events for the year. A Turkey Trot, holiday run, or another low-pressure race with friends can provide a checkpoint and make winter training more fun.

Soon after moving to Anchorage, Baxter joined a training group that held workouts at The Dome, an indoor athletic facility featuring an indoor track. The group helped her settle into the running community while giving her a reason to show up for a workout each week.

Once she developed those friendships, other runners expected her to show up, and those weekly track workouts were no longer optional.

If you’re looking for a similar boost, check for a local run club or training group. A standing workout appointment can give your week structure, while supportive company can make a difficult session feel less daunting.

Baxter generally followed the workouts on her schedule, but she changed where she ran to make them possible. The day after a substantial snowfall, she would head to the treadmill rather than plodding through fresh snow, for example. She also took many of her harder workouts to the treadmill or indoor track, where she felt more comfortable running fast.

Her easy and long runs, meanwhile, almost always happened outside.

You can take the same approach by considering the purpose of the day’s run. If you have a faster workout planned and the outdoors only offers slippery conditions, an indoor option may help you complete it with more confidence. If you’re running easy outside, give yourself room to slow down when conditions make your usual pace unrealistic.

Most of all, remember that one winter is part of a much longer running journey. Baxter’s progress—and eventual sub-three breakthrough—came from conquering multiple training cycles and years of running. The work you do this winter can be another step in that process.

“You simply can’t deny the power of consistently stacking marathon block upon marathon block or just years and years and years of running,” Baxter says. “Endurance running is a long game.”

Matt Rudisill is an Associate Service Editor who has been with Runner's World since 2025. A Nittany Lion through-and-through, Matt graduated from Penn State in 2022 with a degree in journalism and worked in communications for the university's athletic department for three years as the main contact and photographer for its nationally-ranked cross country and track & field teams. Matt was also heavily involved in communications efforts for Penn State football, men's basketball, and women's gymnastics. In his role with Runner's World, Matt has interviewed Olympians, world champions, and countless experts in the field to create service content that helps runners of all ages and experience levels train smarter and race faster. When he’s not out jogging, Matt can be found tweeting bad takes about the Phillies or watching movies.

Stop Hunting for Pricey Antiques—Designers Say These 9 Humble Finds Give a Home So Much Character

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

They’re affordable, widely available, and stylish.

It’s any avid thrifter’s dream to stumble upon an extremely valuable piece of decor while browsing the shelves of their favorite antique store. But while you’re hunting for rare treasures, don’t discount the less-expensive finds—they can be the secret to a home that looks instantly filled with character.

“To me, a collected home is much more interesting when everything isn’t perfect or chosen for its monetary value,” designer Lauren Sullivan of Well by Design tells House Beautiful. “Often, it’s the less precious pieces that give a room its personality.”

Even as older generations are continuing to downsize and offload unwanted things to local thrift stores, it still might be hard to find the diamonds in the rough. Keep reading to find out what Sullivan, and a handful of other designers, say you should be on the lookout for on your next thrifting visit.

The most valuable antiques are the ones that have been passed down through generations. In Sullivan’s own bathroom, the designer keeps her great-grandmother’s vanity set on the countertop, its aged finish providing beautiful contrast with the more contemporary marble and finishes. In her bedroom, the same great-grandmother’s small bedside clock found a place on Sullivan’s nightstand.

“Neither is particularly valuable monetarily, but I love incorporating pieces that have been passed down through generations into a home filled with both old and new,” Sullivan says.

Designers love incorporating artwork into homes to add personality and dimension, but Sullivan, as well as designer Becca Meyer of BB Meyer Design, believe that antique shops are where you can really find some hidden gems.

“Some of my favorite pieces of art have come from flea markets or antique shops for very little money,” Meyer says. “I much prefer these to mass produced prints.” You’re more likely to find pieces with aged textures at these types of shops, and that patinaed quality creates an entirely unique look.

Sullivan explains that she’s actually particularly drawn to prints, as well as smaller paintings and silhouettes. She even has an inexpensive print of The Skating Minister in a vintage frame in one of her guest rooms, pictured above. “These smaller, or sometimes quirky, pieces can bring so much personality to a space without requiring a significant investment,” she adds. They may not be originals, but they’re definitely unique.

Old pottery and ceramics are widely available, and they are usually affordable as well. Designer Brooke Snyder of Design Hutch loves how they make any place feel more curated.

The imperfections these handmade pieces carry, from uneven shapes, variations in glaze, or evidence of years of use, are what help these items bring a warmth to a room, particularly when placed near more refined materials, according to Sullivan. They feel humble, maybe even utilitarian, especially when displayed in a kitchen. Or, better yet, used in a kitchen (as long as they’re safe).

“They're beautiful on their own but also so functional for holding utensils or branches or flowers,” Meyer adds. “They make any space feel a little more lived-in and loved and help tell a story—and they don't break the bank!”

“I love incorporating vintage plates, old copper pots, and woven baskets into a home,” explains designer Joe Waroquier. Though these are run-of-the-mill, everyday objects, they can take on and provide an entirely different life when displayed in the home as decor. Mismatched plates on a wall, a collection of copper pots hanging on a rod or resting on shelves in the kitchen, an old crock filled with branches—these items invite in warmth, texture, and more personality.

“I’m particularly drawn to pieces that show their age, whether it’s a little patina, a worn finish, or an imperfect glaze,” he adds. “I love mixing these finds with more contemporary furnishings because that contrast makes a room feel collected and personal rather than overly designed.”

Mirrors can be intriguing focal points of a room, or they can add dimension to a gallery wall. However you want to style them, Snyder loves finding these reflective pieces at antique malls and vintage shops.

“Purchasing something that you don’t see every day from a retailer allows this unique piece to stand out on its own, without feeling like something you see everywhere or feels too simple,” she explains.

As Snyder puts it, antique table lamps provide high visual impact with low investment. Adding one to your accent table or on a bookshelf is an easy way to make your space feel more curated while inviting in another level of ambience with the lighting.

These vintage lamps in the above space, for instance, were bought from a collector and make the primary bedroom feel much more charming and curated. And unlike antique sconces, there’s no need to hardwire these into the wall.

Besides pottery and lamps, designer Ryann Swan Hackett of Ryann Swan Design loves to find and collect other small tabletop decor for her projects. Think candleholders, vintage coffee table books, and trays—things that can layer a little history and individuality into a room or simple vignette.

“If you’re lucky,” Swan Hackett says of the vintage coffee table books, “you’ll find an old inscription inside, which gives the book a story of its own.”

In terms of midsize accent pieces, Meyer says that she has a soft spot for finding antique stools “because you can pop them into any space to add depth and dimension.” Tuck them beside an armchair in the living room, add them to an empty kitchen corner and place a plant on top, or stick one in a bathroom by the tub for somewhere to keep a drink while relaxing. And if you find an upholstered one that could use new fabric, switching it out for a pattern you love is probably worth it.

“These bring a natural warmth that can be difficult to replicate with something new, and their small size makes them fairly reasonable in price for a vintage furnishing piece,” Meyer says.

A larger piece of furniture is likely always going to be more expensive than a smaller one, but designer Yena Jung of By Yena Designs thinks vintage hutches are the versatile antiques people should pay more attention to. They come in many different shapes, styles, and sizes, and can easily make a home in any room of the house.

“A beautiful vintage hutch can add warmth, charm, and a sense of history to a space,” Jung says. “I especially love how their imperfections and natural patina make them feel collected over time.”

Inside the First Competition Between Historic Vintage Timepieces

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Want more insider watch coverage? Get Box + Papers, GQ's newsletter devoted to the watch world, sent to your inbox every Friday. Sign up here to get it free.

In the automotive world, high-end cars have gone head-to-head since the early 1920s. Judges didn’t evaluate them based on their speed, though—in a Concours d’Élégance, the prize goes to the best-looking vehicles. The tradition continues today in picturesque sites around the world where collectors submit their often very high-end vintage, classic cars. Surprisingly, there has never been an event for vintage timepieces.

Aurel Bacs, the famous auctioneer whose longstanding association with Phillips has shaped the watch collecting world, endeavored to change that. “It began with a meeting where I said, ‘I have a dream,’” he said in a press release. For the very first iteration of this competition, Bacs and Phillips partnered with Swiss heavyweight Vacheron Constantin. They expected to receive maybe 100 to 200 submissions, but the jury was forced to whittle down 550 of them to 34 finalist watches, whose owners were flown out from across the world for the Concours held at the Hôtel Président in Geneva.

“It was the most beautiful evening in the field ever,” Bacs said. At the Hôtel Président, around 200 attendees gathered, along with a jury of 14 watch-industry experts including Bacs himself, Ben Clymer of Hodinkee, writer and historian Nick Foulkes, longtime watch journalist Roberta Naas, and others. Watches could only be submitted for consideration in one category (Chronographs, Métiers d’Art, Chronomètre Royal, Astronomical Complications, Multiple Complications, Design, and Chiming Mechanisms) and were judged based on nine criteria: authenticity, elegance, rarity, impact on the history of Vacheron Constantin and/or on watchmaking in general, provenance, technicality, métiers d’art (artistic crafts), state of preservation, and emotional dimension.

The jurors deliberated over several hours until the winners of the seven categories and a Best of Show were finally selected. Following a cocktail reception, everyone gathered on the lower floor of the Hôtel Président to see the results from the watch world’s first Concours d'Élégance.

The first category featured a smattering of some of the maison’s most important wristwatches, including a highly rare ref. 4072 in stainless steel from 1940. However, it was a yellow gold pocket-watch chronograph from 1929 that won the day. The result was such a surprise that the audience, intimately familiar with the historical importance of pocket watches, audibly gasped inside the room. It made me wonder if these old-fashioned timepieces could regain their lost position as the pinnacle of watch collecting. This piece certainly warranted the love it received: in excellent condition and featuring a white enamel dial with dual registers in an “up-down” configuration, it’s a beautiful timepiece.

This category celebrates handcrafts such as enameling and gem-setting, and it featured a smattering of truly spectacular pieces—mostly sub-40mm wristwatches in precious metals with wild cloisonné or Grand Feu champlevé enamel dials depicting landmasses or animals.

However, a stunning pocket watch set with blue sapphires throughout the bezel and Roman indices won out.

When an older American gentleman stepped on stage to collect his prize, he explained to a stunned crowd that not only had his great-grandfather, Dr. Otto von Frangeš, been given the watch by His Majesty Alexander I of Yugoslavia, its original owner, but that he wore it while sitting in the motorcade during the king’s assassination in France in 1934. Otto’s middle son, Nikola, eventually smuggled the watch out of Yugoslavia by sewing it into the lining of his wife’s jacket as they fled the Iron Curtain in the late 1940s.

In 1907, Vacheron launched the Chronomètre Royal sub-brand, specifically built to endure a life sailing the seas. The model’s parts were anti-magnetic and resisted rusting. The dials were made out of enamel, which could also stand up to the moist atmosphere without rusting. Because they were critical to maritime navigation, timing precision was of the utmost importance, and models from this line frequently won observatory trials (which tested for a watch’s accuracy) that ran from the 1800s to the mid-1900s. Given all that information, the winner is a bit of a shocker: a ref. 2215 from 1976 that exemplifies the qualities of the line but modernizes them in many ways. It doesn’t even feature a full set of numerals, trading them out for a few diamond-set indices. In his acceptance speech, the owner said he wears it on the weekends with jeans.

The Astronomical Complications category is dear to the heart of anyone who appreciates a good moonphase display and also includes timepieces equipped with a triple-calendar, a complete calendar, or a perpetual calendar. Though more modern pieces, such as a skeletonized QP wristwatch from 1987, made it to the final round, it was a wildly neat (and unique) platinum-cased “Cioccolatone” from 1957 that won out. Housed in a 34mm x 43mm rectangular case and equipped with a complete calendar, it’s owned by a dapper Italian collector, who beamed as he collected the prize with his grown son. This would not be this pair’s first time at the podium…

This is a loaded category for Vacheron, which famously makes the most complicated pocket watch and wristwatch in the world, with 57 and 41 complications, respectively. To be eligible in this category, watches were required to have at least two complications—child’s play for VC.

Spanning everything from an enamel-dialed, minute-repeating, split-seconds chronograph pocket watch from 1901 to several Patrimony watches from the 1990s, the winner was the “Don Pancho.” Delivered in 1940, this diminutive yellow-gold timepiece with minute repeater, day, and retrograde date indications was once the world’s most complicated wristwatch. Double-signed by Brooking in Madrid, it sold for roughly $740,000 at auction back in 2019.

A more subjective category, perhaps, than the others, Design included watches “featuring Vacheron Constantin’s signature design, either in the case or through the time display.” One entrant, for example—a wild time-only ref. 4695 dress watch—featured incredibly intricate lugs and a Maltese Cross-type dial. But the winner was nothing short of revelatory. Once again owned by the aforementioned tall Italian gentleman, the possibly unique ref. 6213 is an automatic world timer from 1957 in yellow gold. Based upon Louis Cottier’s famous system, it is so rare that even Clymer admitted he had never heard of it before the competition. The owner’s son described his father returning home with it one day when he was a boy, excited beyond belief that he had finally tracked it down after years of searching.

This category included pocket watches or wristwatches fitted with a minute repeater, quarter repeater, half-quarter repeater, or grand sonnerie—a mechanism that automatically strikes the hours and quarter-hours every 15 minutes. Incredibly, among the entrants was a red gold quarter-repeater pocket watch from the early 1820s with a stunning guilloché dial. However, the winner was another pocket watch dating to 1926 and cased in platinum. Its ultra-thin design, black Breguet numerals, Breguet blued steel handset, and simple silver dial belied the complex mechanism within. The visibly emotional American gentleman who owns it recalled how, as a boy, he would open a chest of drawers, remove his grandfather’s pocket watch, and listen to its minute repeater.

A bonus category, Best of Show went to a truly spectacular wristwatch: a ref. 6448 in platinum with diamond-set indices. Seemingly a simple time-only dress watch, this tremendous piece—which was produced in just three examples in the early 1960s—boasts a hand-wound, 13-ligne minute-repeater movement. Housed in a brushed-finish case with incredible teardrop lugs, it’s the only known example in this metal, and it was notably the last minute-repeating reference produced by the brand for nearly 30 years.

Nominally, the crowd at the awards ceremony was there to judge the merits of a watch, but I left emotionally moved by the stories each watch owner shared about their timepieces. As the very first concours for watches, the format will no doubt be repeated in the future—either by Vacheron, adopted by other brands, or done as a more traditional multi-brand event like the Concours d’Élégance at Pebble Beach.

Betsy James Wyeth’s Placemaking Prowess

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

A wide-ranging book and exhibition reveal the ways the multi-hyphenate’s architectural-environmental projects shaped her husband Andrew Wyeth’s celebrated painting career.

The woman portrayed in Andrew Wyeth’s “Christina’s World” (1948), Christina Olson, has become one of the most iconic figures in American art. Less familiar, perhaps, is the woman who also posed for this painting: his wife, Betsy James Wyeth. Dubbed the “chief architect of the Wyeth mystique,” Betsy shaped the Wyeth brand, Andrew’s life, and art for over seven decades in undeniable ways. Though she rarely occupied the spotlight herself, her influence was everywhere: she handled his business affairs, controlled his public image, wrote books about his art, and maintained his catalogue raisonné. She also served as a creative advisor, critiquing his paintings and giving them their titles.

By Design: The Worlds of Betsy James Wyeth — a hefty and compelling new book (Rizzoli Electa) and a sprawling trio of linked exhibitions shown simultaneously at the Farnsworth Art Museum in Rockland, Maine; Brandywine Museum of Art in Chadds Ford, Pennsylvania; and Colby College Museum of Art in Waterville, Maine — pay tribute to the pair’s enduring partnership and work to credit Betsy as a key figure in her own right.

Michael Manning (He/Him) has been enamored with video games since he first played the Sonic Classic Collection as a child. Music makes many games what they truly are, setting the tone and doubling down on a moment's emotions, whether somber, pulse-pounding, or chilling. Without their OSTs, many games would feel like a shell of themselves, solid without the soul. Many games have used the need for music to get musicians on the map or back into the public spotlight, creating a beautiful loop.

The EU's proposed Digital Fairness Act targets loot boxes, pay-to-win mechanics, unclear in-game prices, and subscription traps.

Final Fantasy is a series absolutely defined by its music, with truly legendary composers and artists stemming from every title. Sadly, Final Fantasy 14’s Twitter announced earlier today that Eikichi Iwai, bassist for the band made for the game THE PRIMALS, has passed away. After recently performing at the most recent Japanese Fan Fest, his passing is a sudden low blow that has staggered fans across the globe.

THE PRIMALS are the standout musicians of Final Fantasy 14, far from the only incredibly talented maestros making the music happen, but definitely the flashiest. Often brought out to live events for both their own original tracks and covers of some of 14's other greatest hits, THE PRIMALS helped forge some of the greatest tracks to grace Final Fantasy 14, and Iwai-san's absence will be deeply felt in future events.

While the rest of the musicians behind FF 14 and his bandmates on THE PRIMALS will feel the most potent loss, fans across the globe are aching as well, already holding in-game mourning vigils throughout Eorzea. Sitting by fires and playing different pieces of music Iwai worked on, fans are lamenting his passing the best way they know how, by celebrating all he gave to them.

THE PRIMALS are the standout musicians of Final Fantasy 14, far from the only incredibly talented maestros making the music happen, but definitely the flashiest.

THE PRIMALS have backed some of the most pivotal moments in Final Fantasy 14’s history, bouncing from raid layers to incredibly crucial story moments to general world traversal. As a band, they’re incredibly flexible and talented, with a good part of that grit coming from Iwai’s incredible bass play. The future of the band is currently uncertain, especially with how sudden Iwai’s loss truly was.

Eikichi Iwai passed at age 57, a titan of his craft and an incredible musician across his pursuits, charming millions who played Final Fantasy 14 and all who followed his long history with the band Chirinuruwowaka, of which he was a founding member. Never one to slow down or stop making music, with his recent showing at the latest Japanese FF14 Fan Fest being cited as one of THE PRIMALS best ever showings.

After suffering a loss like Iwai’s, it’s important to remember just how lucky we are to enjoy such incredibly skilled artists in their primes. Final Fantasy 14 has its issues, but music has never been one of them, carrying many crucial emotional scenes with next-level tracks again and again.

Close in the Distance, A Long Fall, and Rise, all written by Masayoshi Soken, show a mastery of tone and emotion across an incredible range, perfectly putting players in the mindset of the current moment. THE PRIMALS’ own coverage of tracks like Footfalls and Dawntrail gives each song life, fueled by THE PRIMALS’ incredible energy.

I genuinely believe you could find someone who cites almost every song in Final Fantasy 14 as one of their favorites, and that’s amazing. The incredible display of artistry throughout the title is a large reason why it has lasted as long as it has with the massive fanbase it has cultivated.

With live service games and MMOs like Final Fantasy 14, it can be all too easy and lucrative to fall into the trappings of consumerism, focusing on the product rather than the art. FF really doesn’t have that issue. FF14 has always felt like a title guided by the artists who create it, and after the loss of such a powerful artist, it’s up to the rest of us to keep on that path. Rest in peace, Eikichi Iwai.

Garmin LiveTrack Now Alerts Waze Drivers to Nearby Cyclists

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Opt-in LiveTrack sharing puts a moving cyclist icon in Waze and alerts some nearby drivers.

Rearview radars are one of our favorite cycling accessories because they alert riders when a vehicle is approaching from behind. Now Garmin and Waze are flipping that warning around: Their new partnership alerts drivers when a cyclist using Garmin’s LiveTrack is nearby.

Here’s how it works. Start an outdoor cycling activity with LiveTrack on a compatible Garmin Edge computer or smartwatch, and your location will appear in Waze as a moving cyclist icon. Drivers don’t need to be navigating a route to see cyclists; Waze told me the icons appear whenever the app is open, as riders enter the driver’s broader route area or immediate surroundings.

Waze says the alert comes when a cyclist gets closer, but it hasn’t specified the distance. In an email response to my questions, Garmin told me alerts go to “relevant drivers,” usually when the rider and driver are on the same road and traveling in the same direction. A cyclist farther ahead on the driver’s mapped route may also trigger one.

If several riders are together, Waze shows one icon to avoid repeat alerts. If they spread out, multiple icons appear. Garmin describes the grouping feature as “built-in clustering logic.”

For Waze users, cyclist alerts are on by default but can be turned off in the app’s settings. Riders opt in to Waze sharing through Garmin, but only once. After that, sharing starts whenever they begin a cycling LiveTrack, whether they start it manually or set it to start automatically.

Garmin says any Edge computer or smartwatch with LiveTrack is compatible. LiveTrack requires a phone paired with Garmin Connect and cellular coverage; Garmin’s LTE-enabled devices can run LiveTrack without relying on the phone’s connection.

No additional subscription or purchase is required. Waze says drivers see only a temporary alert that a cyclist is nearby, not the rider’s name, identity, or exact route. Sharing stops when the ride ends.

This is not V2X, the connected-vehicle technology that has been simmering in the background. V2X systems let vehicles exchange information with one another, roadside infrastructure, and other road users, including cyclists. Garmin and Waze are taking an app-based route to a similar goal: LiveTrack sends a rider’s location to Waze, which can alert drivers using the app.

It’s a sensible idea, but it requires the latest apps and firmware, a Garmin rider who has opted in, a working connection, and a driver using Waze. It isn’t a substitute for cycling-friendly infrastructure, and it won’t fix distracted drivers or jerks in cars. But reminding drivers that cyclists are on the road is a step in the right direction.

Would you enable Waze sharing for your LiveTrack rides? If you use Waze while driving, would a cyclist icon or alert change how you approach the road? Tell us in the comments, or join me on Discord. I’d especially like to hear from riders who already use LiveTrack or Waze: What would you want to know before turning this on?

A gear editor for his entire career, Matt’s journey to becoming a leading cycling tech journalist started in 1995, and he’s been at it ever since; likely riding more cycling equipment than anyone on the planet along the way. Previous to his time with Bicycling, Matt worked in bike shops as a service manager, mechanic, and sales person. Based in Durango, Colorado, he enjoys riding and testing any and all kinds of bikes, so you’re just as likely to see him on a road bike dressed in Lycra at a Tuesday night worlds ride as you are to find him dressed in a full face helmet and pads riding a bike park on an enduro bike. He doesn’t race often, but he’s game for anything; having entered road races, criteriums, trials competitions, dual slalom, downhill races, enduros, stage races, short track, time trials, and gran fondos. Next up on his to-do list: a multi day bikepacking trip, and an e-bike race.
