Why the 2026 fake foreclosure crisis never happened

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Foreclosure activity in 2026 rose off low levels but did not signal broad credit stress. With 9- to 18-month timelines and LTV at 44.2%, the data points to normalization, not a crisis.  AI Summary

The 2026 foreclosure crisis that some people predicted has failed to materialize, and now those people look silly. We should be thankful for the regulations and laws enacted that laid the foundation for such a sound housing credit market: The 2005 bankruptcy reform law and the 2010 Dodd-Frank laws that established qualified mortgage guidelines were historically positive for the U.S. economy.

Looking at the New York Fed’s Household Debt and Credit Report for Q3, we can clearly see that the anticipated foreclosure crisis hasn’t happened. All the headlines showing a high percentage of year-over-year growth in foreclosure data didn’t amount to anything near a crisis.

For those who expected a situation worse than what we experienced in 2008, it has been an embarrassing year. This highlights the dangers of listening to economic discussions from those without the necessary expertise. So let’s talk about where these doomers get it wrong.

The idea that the FHA foreclosure moratorium, which ended in October 2025, would lead to a surge in distressed listings has been a popular topic this year. It’s gotten a lot of headlines, but has fallen flat on its face, since that isn’t how the foreclosure-to-listing process works in America. At minimum, it takes 9-18 months from the start of the foreclosure process to finally add that supply to the marketplace.And foreclosure data has not, at any point since 2014, shown any material sign of credit stress building up in the pipeline. Always remember, 1%-4% of all mortgages are always going to be in delinquency; it’s when that data breaks above historical norms that it should get everyone’s attention.To illustrate what a true foreclosure crisis might look like, we can consider the historical context: since World War II, we have experienced 13 recessions with an average unemployment rate of 7.8%, but only one significant foreclosure crisis, which was the run-up to the 2008 Great Recession.

It took four years for the foreclosure credit market to build up before the 2008 recession hit. As we finish Q3 of 2026, we still haven’t returned to pre-COVID levels of foreclosure credit stress, as the data below has always shown for anyone who believes in reading.

The New York Fed foreclosure credit stress data line moves slowly, and unless this index is above 200, there isn’t much activity out of the norm. The many posts this year predicting a foreclosure crisis have proven to be exaggerated. The scale of the situation is small, and it’s clear that many commentators lack the experience to accurately discuss the current market conditions.Additionally, I want to highlight HousingWire’s new listing data. During the previous housing crisis, credit foreclosures directly impacted new listing data, pushing weekly listings to between 250,000 and 400,000 — for years. These listings represented distressed sellers who were forced to sell in an underwater market. All those increases from 2005-2008 in foreclosures eventually became distressed sales, and none of that is happening today. Let’s compare the new listings data for last week to the same week from 2009-2011:

The 2008 massive credit boom and bust took many years to create. We have not seen this kind of credit boom in housing from 2010-2026, so it’s impossible to have a credit bust like 2008 — or “worse than 2008,” as many untrained fake housing analysts have talked about for years. A lot of people can’t let go of the past, as they’re still dancing with the ghost of the 2008 housing crisis. They should relax, have a glass of wine, and reflect on their bad takes.In contrast, over the last 16 years, FICO scores in America have remained strong. Most homeowners have 30-year fixed-rate mortgages, and qualification guidelines are much more stringent and effective now. Rising wages and homeowners staying in their properties longer have contributed to the overall financial health of American households.

Despite claims of an impending foreclosure crisis, the reality is quite different. In 2010, over 23% of homes in America were underwater, with loan-to-value ratios reaching nearly 86% in 2011. Currently, the loan-to-value figure is down to 44.2%, as the chart below shows.

It’s crucial to be cautious about who you listen to, as the narrative of a looming crisis has been overstated for many years. If someone tells you it’s housing 2008 or a foreclosure crisis is here, tell them to read a book instead of burning it, and read HousingWire.

AWS gives developers a free local stand-in for its cloud, built from scratch by AI agents

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop on Nov 11, 2026 at 10:51 amNovember 11, 2026 at 10:52 am

AWS says its engineers didn’t write or review a single line of code in its newest developer tool, and the company is touting that as a selling point.

AWS Emulator, released Wednesday, simulates AWS services on a developer’s own machine. It’s built from more than 400 API models covering more than 200 AWS services. Developers and AI coding agents can use it to test code without a network connection and without paying for the real cloud.

The tool addresses a problem that coding agents such as Amazon’s Kiro and Anthropic’s Claude Code have made worse. Agents can write code faster than teams can test it against the real cloud. Agents check their work by running the code and reading the results, over and over, and doing that in the cloud adds delays and costs.

The emulator lets them run that loop on a developer’s own machine.

In an ironic or perhaps appropriate twist, AWS used AI agents to build the emulator. Agents also maintain it, testing it against real AWS and fixing the gaps they find.

“The emulator was written entirely by autonomous agents. Our team hasn’t written or reviewed any of the code,” wrote Matt Goldberg, the AWS senior principal engineer behind the project, in a blog post.

Goldberg compared his agents to himself as a kindergartner, too young to hold a crayon properly, who got yelled at by classmates for ruining a group drawing of dinosaurs. The problem wasn’t bad intent, he wrote, just a lack of competence. The answer, for agents, is clearly defined areas to fill in.

“I think agents also need coloring books,” he wrote.

The agents work from AWS’s public API models, which describe how each service should behave, and check their work against the real AWS cloud.

The agents run on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore in three teams. One writes “fidelity tests” — real-world scenarios such as creating a queue, sending a message and deleting the queue — and checks each against real AWS. A second runs those tests against the emulator and files issues for failures, and a third fixes them, one agent per issue.

Every change passes through an automated gate where other agents check coding standards and security. Goldberg wrote that hundreds of agents now fix thousands of issues a day.

The project started as a weekend experiment, inspired by Anthropic’s demonstration earlier this year in which a team of Claude agents built a C compiler. Goldberg gave Kiro a three-step prompt and got a passable first version of Amazon SQS, AWS’s message queuing service, in 15 minutes.

By the end of the weekend, Goldberg wrote, Kiro had built a rough version of the rest of AWS, with obvious gaps, and his team started using it to test its software that Monday.

Swami Sivasubramanian, AWS vice president of agentic AI and emerging tech, tied the project to a lesson he described to GeekWire in June. He spent four nights babysitting Kiro as it tried to rebuild a replication engine he and Allan Vermeulen built by hand nearly 20 years ago.

Once he gave the agent a spec and a way to test its work, the job took about two hours.

“When agents write the code and check their own work, the engineer’s job is to give them room to work on their own and to build the specs, tests, and merge gates that decide when that work is correct,” Sivasubramanian wrote in a guest post on CTO Werner Vogels’ blog.

It’s the same idea behind Amazon’s recent move to rewrite its engineering playbook for an era when AI writes the code.

The emulator has limits. Devin Pratt, an IDC analyst, told InfoWorld that it doesn’t yet simulate throttling, outages or differences in which services and features are available by region, and that it doesn’t sandbox the code it runs. The agents’ backlog of known gaps is public, in a GitHub folder called “things-that-do-not-work.”

Other AWS emulators exist, most notably LocalStack, which this year put its free version behind an account requirement and limited it to noncommercial use. AWS released its emulator for free under CC0, a public domain dedication, with the full commit history on GitHub.

The company says it will share more at its upcoming re:Invent conference.

If AI can’t understand the business context trapped inside legacy systems, moving them to the cloud just moves the problem. See how Thoughtworks and AWS combine AI/works™ with AWS Transform to modernize for the AI era.

The Horrific-Smelling Piece of Gear No Hiker Needs to Carry in 2026

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

A solid-fuel stove burning a hexamine tablet (Photo: krblokhin / iStock via Getty)

There are smells I’ve experienced that still give me nightmares: The box of mail-order lobsters UPS once delivered to my parents two weeks late. The freezer filled with fish and ground beef that I had to clean out after someone unplugged it and left it outside in the summer sun. And, sliding into third place after those, the time that my hexamine fuel tablets got loose in my pack.

I was competing in the Grand Traverse, a roughly 40-mile ski mountaineering race from Crested Butte to Aspen, Colorado. Because of the ruggedness of the terrain, the organizers required that each team carry a full set of survival gear, including a stove. In an attempt to keep our kit as light as possible, my partner and I packed a tiny solid fuel burner and a few fuel tablets for a total of about 5 ounces—lighter than a 100-gram fuel canister. I double-bagged them and stuffed them in my beanie, which I hoped would stop them from jostling around in my pack.

If you’ve never used hexamine fuel tablets, the first thing to know about them is that they smell bad. And not bad in the way you’d expect: Instead of the petroleum twang of white gas or the skunky smell added to propane-butane mixes, the tablets have a distinct marine odor. Users have compared it to rotten shellfish or dead carp.

Halfway through the race, I started to notice hints of that smell when I opened my pack. But it wasn’t until after we crossed the finish line that I realized what had happened: At some point during the race, one of the bags had jostled open. The beanie I had packed it in was the main casualty; I put it on and gagged. It took multiple cycles in the washing machine and about a month of airing out before it stopped smelling like a Red Lobster during a power outage.

You, too, can have this experience, and you don’t need to spend much: Esbit stoves, the best-known brand of solid-fuel burners, can be had for as little as $12, fuel included. They’re cheap, simple, and at least notionally light. But whether you’re considering one for your ultralight kit or emergency winter use, I would buy literally anything else.

Solid-fuel stoves are old tech: The first hit the market in 1936, when Esbit, a German company, introduced hexamine fuel tablets and the compact burners designed to use them. Demand for them boomed during World War II, when Esbit’s burners became standard issue for the Wehrmacht; the Allies developed their own versions as well. Today, solid-fuel stoves still have more currency among European backpackers than American ones, who are less likely to have used them and, if they live somewhere where fire restrictions are common, often can’t, as land managers generally ban any stove without an on-off switch alongside campfires.

Fire hazard aside, there are concrete reasons why so few hikers nowadays are springing for solids. While the stoves are light, their fuel is not: It takes almost 2 standard tablets, or nearly an ounce, of hexamine fuel to bring a liter of water to a rolling boil, compared to about 0.3 ounces of fuel for a pressure-regulated canister stove like the Soto Windmaster. While someone using a canister stove does have to tote the weight of the metal container the fuel comes in, the difference in efficiency means that for any trip longer than a day or two, the solid-fuel stove’s advantage is questionable at best; at the very least, it means starting out with a heavier pack. They’re difficult, though not impossible, to light in the cold and glacially slow at melting snow, real disadvantages for an emergency stove that you may have to spark up with numb fingers. And then, of course, there’s that pungent smell.

If they’re inefficient and smell bad, why do solid-fuel stoves continue to hang on? I’ll give them this: They’re simple. Like anything with moving parts, a canister stove can break. Filters clog and piezo igniters burn out. There’s essentially no way to break a solid-fuel stove; step on it, and you can just bend it back into shape. Even when it comes to simplicity, though, I’m not convinced it’s the best choice: While they have their downsides, an alcohol burner is as cheap and light as a hexamine stove, smells better, and the fuel is easier to find. (Lest it seem like I’m trashing Esbit, I’ll note that they make a solid alcohol-burning model that doubles as a storage bottle for its fuel.)

I’m not a hater, or at least I try not to be: Every backpacker has their gear quirks, and if you’re an old-schooler who’s been using a solid-fuel stove since your scouting days, I think that’s great. But if you’re one of the newcomers on any given backpacking forum pondering whether you should buy that ultralight titanium solid-fuel burner, the answer is probably no. For about $5 and 12 grams more—that’s about the weight of a AAA battery—you could pick up a BRS-3000T, a canister stove that will give you far less grief. And as a bonus, it won’t make your pack smell like a mermaid with B.O.

Veterans Who Turned to Yoga Are Now Teaching It—And Not Always in the Places You’d Expect

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Veterans yoga has been in existence for decades, with several organizations providing free classes. But what has been changing is the number of veterans teaching yoga, both to their peers and the general public. Writer “Chez” Chesak explores how his former platoonmates and other former military personnel are introducing others to the practice.

When I was deployed to Iraq with the U.S. Army in 2005, me and the rest of my platoon were constantly jumping in and out of Humvees, ascending uneven stairs in buildings, climbing ladders in guard towers, and generally finding ourselves in constant motion—all with more than 80 pounds of armor, ammo, night vision goggles, and other combat load sitting on our shoulders. And then there was the tremendous and relentless emotional and psychological stress. Needless to say, we were all very wired and very, very tight.

One proactive and thoughtful soldier in our platoon, Jake Smith, suggested that yoga could probably help and offered to download some instructions and lead us through a class. For some reason—quite possibly to stay out of sight from other soldiers, who would have likely ridiculed us—we thought it best to climb onto the roof of our building in Kirkuk for an ad hoc yoga session. Jake, who was definitely not a certified instructor but certainly did his best, led us through various poses and we attempted to follow along.

Somewhere in between making shapes with my body and concentrating on my breath, it registered that there were dozens of buildings in plain view of our patrol base. And if we could see the surrounding buildings, anyone in those buildings could see us. Realizing that we could be in the sights of a sniper, we quickly wrapped up class and made our way back inside.

For most of us, it was our first attempt at yoga. Despite the fact the class was well-intentioned and received well, none of us continued practicing during our time overseas. Instead, we succumbed to more shoulder pain and mental duress.

But in the years since, some in my platoon—as well as active military personnel and veterans everywhere—have been getting past our initial biases against such a “hippy” activity and turning to yoga for an array of reasons, including support with lingering physical and mental health challenges. I’ve heard the quote “Yoga saved my life,” from numerous military veterans who expressed the sentiment with zero embellishment, exaggeration, or hyperbole.

With nearly 16 million veterans in the U.S., there are more who could benefit from yoga. A survey of 300,000 veterans and service members registered with the Wounded Warrior Project found that more than half of respondents struggled to manage their pain, with 95 percent experiencing pain in the previous three months and 76 percent acknowledging that pain interfered with their ability to enjoy life. Additionally, more than 50 percent experienced anxiety symptoms in the previous two weeks.

Many of the veterans who proselytize about yoga know firsthand its role in supporting individuals through the repercussions of surviving combat zones. Some proceed to take yoga teacher training and introduce others to the practice, sharing it every conceivable style and practice space, including studios, gyms, and corporate offices. And among them are also those helping fellow soldiers and veterans explore how yoga might help them navigate the complexities of PTSD.

Mike Cappel used to hate yoga. He served 12 years in Military Intelligence in the Army Reserve and three years as a Combat Engineer in the National Guard and readily admits his past loathing for the practice.

Thanks to doctor’s orders, he tried yoga, fell in love with it, and then took a summer class at the University of Cincinnati (U.C.). Cappel now teaches yoga to the Reserve Officers Training Corps cadets there, beginning with cadets who participated in his yoga warm-ups before the Army Combat Fitness Test (ACFT) swearing that they scored higher thanks to the practice.

“They all say they love it now,” says Cappel. “And I can see that they really do. We’ll have a whole battalion of people out there in the stadium first thing in the morning, all doing yoga.” The officers that run the program eventually decided to try his classes as well,and these days everyone does yoga together before taking the ACFT.

His yoga instruction isn’t limited to students in uniform. Cappel also teaches at the offices of corporate clients, local independent yoga studios, and chain gyms, and leads a total of 16 classes a week.

In contrast to exposing those new to the military to yoga, Chris Smith brings it to those who have come back from their tours of duty and struggle to reacclimate to everyday life. After serving as an Engineer in the Army for eight years and being deployed three times, including during the 2003 Iraq invasion, Chris separated from the military and from his wife. He was unhoused and struggling with substance abuse when he was committed to an involuntary five-day psychiatric hold at a hospital.

Eventually, Chris landed in a rental assistance program through Veteran Affairs (VA), used the GI Bill to get a degree from Marywood University, and then started a veteran resource center. In researching benefits, he learned about Warrior Strong, which provides free wellness services, including yoga, to veterans across 33 states.

Chris explains that after taking his first yoga class, he was hooked. He soon signed up for training to become an instructor. He and other Warrior Strong instructors currently deliver nearly 100 classes spanning yoga, fitness, meditation, and breathwork to veterans each month. The classes are conducted online as well as in-studio at the Scranton, Pennsylvania, location as well regional VA medical centers. He also volunteers his teaching time to a weekly breathwork class for the depression group at a local veterans center and a monthly meditation class at the drug and alcohol unit at a VA Medical Center.

“Yoga is what’s really helping me manage my PTSD symptoms,” says Chris. “It’s helped me reconnect with people because for a very long time I was isolating myself a lot. I credit it with helping me continue the journey that I started nine years ago.”

Other veterans take elements of how yoga best supports them and share it with a more generalized student population. Jessica Armendariz spent 21 years in the U.S. Army, serving in the Airborne, completing all four levels of the Amy’s Combatives hand-to-hand combat training, and deploying to Afghanistan. When she retired, her body was experiencing severe physical wear, joint pain, and elevated cortisol levels. She eventually found that practicing rigorous vinyasa yoga helped alleviate chronic pain and quiet her racing mind.

Armendariz currently teaches studio classes at Hot Asana in Southern Pines, North Carolina, and tries to share the same benefits with others. “I am trying to challenge you physically,” says Armendariz. “That’s not to say that the rest of the yoga goes out the window. Of course we do breathing, we do centering, and all those critical things, but for me, for it to work to feel authentic to me as a teacher, there has to be that challenge.” She also has training in yoga nidra as well as yin and restorative yoga.

Sometimes the adaptability of yoga and its built-in community become as much a part of the yoga experience as the poses. After Jeremy McMullen left the Navy, he became severely sedentary. That’s when his wife gifted him a yoga lesson. The retired Chief Petty Officer instantly found that it benefited his sleep as well as his physical and mental health. Now a yoga instructor, McMullen teaches in a studio, performs yoga on mountain passes during long-distance hikes, has led online classes, and teaches “beer yoga” at breweries, where the participants indulge in a beer or two together afterward.

McMullen sees a correlation between his role in the Navy and his role as an instructor. “The chiefs at work [in the Navy] are always trying to teach sailors the right way to do everything. I do the same thing as a yoga teacher, making sure to teach you your left and your right and your breathing and your mindfulness.”

Military veterans are no strangers to working in unexpected and remote places. Jared McKenzie, a medic in my platoon when we deployed, currently teaches yoga at studios, so it wasn’t entirely a surprise when he was inspired, during a recent multi-day whitewater rafting trip, to lead others through early morning stretches along the shore. He’ll be resuming his beach yoga classes on the outfitter’s 2027 River Soul Journeys whitewater rafting and yoga trip in Idaho.

McKenzie is looking forward to teaching yoga within the solitude and serenity of Idaho’s river canyons. “I love the juxtaposition of this trip where everyone will be blasting whitewater through the day and then slowing down in the evenings with riverside yoga and breathwork,” he says.

There are surely more vets out there who are just now trying a class and starting their own experience with yoga journey. Those who take the next step and become instructors can deploy out to studios and VA facilities, teach online, and maybe even lead a class in a river canyon or other backcountry location. And I choose to believe that none of them will be so foolish as to attempt a class on a rooftop in a combat zone.

Veterans interested in trying yoga will find free resources for veterans-focused, in-person and online classes and teacher trainings through the VA’s Whole Health program and various organizations, including Wounded Warrior Project, Veterans Yoga Project, and VEToga.

I Gambled Our Business Class Flight Home On A 65-Minute Connection And Separate Tickets

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

In the interest of maximizing our time on the ground (it was a short trip after all) and avoiding an early morning and long drive to Zürich, I booked a separate ticket from Basel to Copenhagen on Norwegian with a rather tight 65-minute connection to catch our SAS flight to Chicago…

When I booked my tickets for this trip, there was no award space on SAS from Zurich to Copenhagen. I figured it would open (intra-Europe space reliably opens a day or two before travel) and it certainly did, but I wasn’t thrilled about getting up before dawn to drive to Zürich in order to make the flight.

The change fee on a SAS EuroBonus award ticket is about 75USD as well, which caused me to look for options out of Basel, which is 15 minutes away from my in-laws house instead of an hour. I knew easyJet operates service on this route, but I was pleased to see Norwegian Air is now operating and the fares were cheap…cheaper than the change fee on SAS.

It was 177EUR for all four tickets on Norwegian from BSL-CPH, which I found compelling, though it was not without risk. Connecting on separate tickets is always a risk and the connection here was only 1 hour, 5 minutes, which would include walking from the Schengen area to the non-Schengen area (where lines can be very long).

Nevertheless, we opted to take the risk, knowing that if the flight was delayed we would miss our connection and might be stuck in Copenhagen another day or forced to fly back to the U.S. in economy class.

There was another wrinkle. My kids and I are U.S. citizens, but my wife sill is not…she travels to the USA on a Green Card and I figured we would be unable to check her in because the Green Card would need to be verified. That turned out to be an issue…I could not compete her check-in, though I was able to for the kids and me.

Long story short: the gamble worked…but it was very close.

We took off early from Basel and the captain announced we would arrive 15 minutes early into Denmark.

But the weather was poor and we ended up circling and pulled up to the gate late…we know we had less than an hour to go to the transfer counter, collect my wife’s boarding pass, pass through passport control, and board our flight.

We landed at the furthest possible gate and had a long walk ahead of us.

My biggest concern was that even though I had initiated check-in on the SAS app, we might be too late to check-in for Heidi. Thankfully, although we did not reach the transfer desk until 40 minutes before departure, an agent was able to complete check-in and issue the boarding pass.

We saw immigration lines were very long for non-Schengen citizens, but short for us so we ducked into the SAS Lounge for Kanelbullar (Swedish cinnamon rolls) and then headed to the gate. I know, I really do push it.

We reached the gate 26 minutes before departure…the gamble worked. Barely.

MSCI Removes Strategy (MSTR) From Its USA Index, Forcing Index Funds to Sell by Nov. 30

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

MSCI will remove Strategy, the largest corporate bitcoin holder, from the MSCI USA Index as of the close of Nov. 30, the index provider said Wednesday, after a new screen classified the company as a non-operating issuer.

The decision is part of MSCI’s November 2026 Index Review, posted shortly after 5 p.m. ET. Official additions and deletions lists show Strategy (MSTR) out of its USA Index, which also takes it out of the broader MSCI World and ACWI indexes. The same review deletes Japan’s Metaplanet from the MSCI Japan Small Cap Index and uranium holder Yellow Cake from the MSCI United Kingdom Small Cap Index, and it removes Iceland’s Skel Fjárfestingafélag from the MSCI Iceland Frontier Markets Small Cap Index and micro-cap Upexi from the USA Micro Cap Index. All five had already been marked Deleted on MSCI’s potential-impact list, built on Sept. 30 financial data and Oct. 9 market values.

Strategy is the only large-cap name in the group and, on that October list, accounted for more than 90% of the five companies’ combined float-adjusted market value. The company held 850,364 bitcoin as of Nov. 8, acquired at an average price of $75,456 a coin, or $64.16 billion in aggregate.

Strategy shares closed at $122.35, up 1.06%, then slipped 1.51% to $120.50 in after-hours trading as of 5:34 p.m. ET.

The rule does not mention bitcoin. The index first checks whether a company’s operating assets equal at least 50% of total assets. Issuers that fail that core screen face five financial ratios, and a company flagged on four or more of them is ineligible for the MSCI Global Investable Market Indexes.

Buffers limit turnover. A company outside the indexes cannot be added if it fails the ineligibility screen in either of its two most recent annual filing periods, but a current constituent is removed only if it fails in each of them. The index said it may also use the most recent interim filing.

MSCI also said Wednesday that the same non-operating treatment will apply to the MSCI US Index Series.

The screen replaces an approach aimed squarely at crypto. The index first proposed in late 2025 to exclude companies whose digital assets made up 50% or more of total assets, then dropped that plan in January and opened a broader consultation in August. It adopted the new methodology on Oct. 15, with minor modifications, and said it would end the interim measures it had placed on those companies in October 2025, including a freeze on share-count increases and a deferral of additions.

Funds that track an MSCI index have to hold what the index holds, so they will have to sell the deleted stocks by the Nov. 30 close. JPMorgan analysts estimated last November that an exclusion could drive about $2.8 billion of outflows from Strategy, and as much as $8.8 billion more if other index providers followed. That estimate was built on MSCI’s earlier digital-asset proposal, not the October screen, and MSCI itself has published no figure.

A removal does not delist Strategy’s stock, and nothing in the rules forces the company to sell bitcoin. The pressure lands on the shares, and through them on the premium Strategy uses to fund new purchases by selling stock.

In an August letter, the company said a deletion “would have no meaningful impact on Strategy’s business.” Strategy has also said funds tracking MSCI’s global investable indexes hold 3.1% of its basic shares outstanding, and that it reports its bitcoin treasury as an operating segment under U.S. GAAP.

Seventeen other constituents were on the potential watchlist, including SharpLink, TON Strategy and Solana Company. Those names do not appear among Wednesday’s published deletions.

Tokyo-listed Metaplanet is the other bitcoin treasury firm in the deleted group, after it sold bitcoin last quarter to show investors it could tap its holdings for liquidity. Strategy bought 1,036 bitcoin for $81.9 million in the week to Nov. 8, a purchase that left its holdings below that average cost.

From Callback to Confidence: How Engineering an ESS for Field Reduces the Costly Callbacks

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Installer on-site callbacks are the silent margin killer in residential energy management and storage. They consume labor, fuel, new project scheduling capacity, and homeowner trust in ways that rarely show up in the original bid. Across the industry, callbacks come from three sources that often overlap: system designs that are too complex to cleanly install, hardware failures after the system is in service, and challenges that arise in the field during installations. Most contractors only feel the cumulative cost years after an install have rolled past. Conversely highly reliable system hardware allows a contractor to confidently “right-size” the project price bid, helping to bring in more business in a period of industry transition.

Preventing callbacks is not only the installer’s responsibility. How the system is engineered, built, and supported all impact callbacks. FranklinWH has focused on quality products and the simplest install, with hardware held to an independently verified reliability standard. The company provides support before, during and after the installation. All of this helps to minimize callbacks. Service data shows once system complexity and hardware failure are engineered down, the dominant driver of post-install activity that remains is the installation stage itself, the real-world variability of the field. That, too, is a problem to solve at the source, not one to pass to the installer.

This paper walks through the anatomy of a callback, the hidden cost stack it creates, the actual root causes industry-wide and in our own data, and the engineering, support, and service decisions made to reduce callback risk before the truck rolls, and to absorb the cost when it can’t be avoided.

The Best Budget Shotguns of 2026

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

For budget-conscious hunters who need a reliable pump under $500, the Mossberg Maverick 88 or Remington 870 Express are the proven choices. For hunters who want an inertia semi-auto without the Benelli price tag, the Stoeger M3000 is the standout pick. For those who can stretch closer to $1,000, the Beretta A300 Outlander and Winchester SX4 represent the category's best value-to-performance ratio.

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn more

Shotguns digest more shells in a season than most rifles will in a lifetime. They get knocked around in the uplands, and splattered with mud in the marsh, and shot and shot and shot at the range. A lot of people don’t like the idea of spending any more than necessary on a gun that has to take such abuse. This list is for them, the practical among us, who will skip a frill here, an engraved pheasant there, in order to maximize their bang for the buck. What follows are nine shotguns that represent extremely good values in their categories. One of them is most likely the best budget shotgun you can buy for the type of hunting you do.

Made in Mexico, the Maverick 88 is a budget Mossberg 500, which seems redundant. It’s essentially the same gun as the Model 500 but with a crossbolt safety in place of the top safety. The Maverick 88 also starts below $300. For that, you get a lock, stock, barrel, and not much else. Well, you get one choke tube and a middle bead, too. I have shot 25 straight on the skeet field with a Model 88, which suggests they shoot where you point them, and I’ve seen them at work in the field, too. They come in black plastic, 3-inch 12- and 20 gauges, in full-size and scaled-down Bantam models.

Friends don’t let friends shoot cheap gas semiautos. They are a headache, unless they are the Maverick SA. This plain Turkish-made gas gun is slim, it’s light, and it does nothing but work. The design is outdated; you have to switch between two different pistons depending on whether you’re shooting light or heavy loads, but outside of that minor inconvenience, the SA delivers reliable performance so long as you break it in with a box or two of heavy loads, and don’t try to make it shoot super-light target ammo. It’s light, listed at 6 ¾ pounds in 3-inch 12 gauge, but fairly soft-kicking. It comes in walnut and black synthetic models with spacers to adjust stock length. This gun joins the 20-, 28-gauge, and .410 SAs already in the Mossberg line.

If you want an inertia gun but don’t want to shell out for a Benelli, the Spandau S2 offers a clone of the old-style Benelli M2 at a great price. It looks like an M2, works very well, and I can’t bump mine out of battery. I shot the 12-gauge turkey version for the 2026 gun test and loved it enough that I’m taking it to the woods next spring. The Turkish-made S2 accepts M2 parts, which is a bonus, since there are a ton of aftermarket add-ons for M2s. Even without Benelli parts, it’s a good gun on its own. It comes in 3-inch 12- and 20-gauges, with 24- or 28-inch barrels, and starts at a ridiculously low price. There are also combo sets with 24- and 28-inch barrels.

When people ask me what they should buy for a first shotgun, I point them to the A300. It’s based on second-generation technology, but in this case, it’s a budget version of the excellent Beretta 391, simplified, changed a little, and made in the United States. The least expensive model is the synthetic version, which features spacers that let you adjust the stock length up to an inch to fit smaller shooters, plus a version of Beretta’s Kick-Off recoil reducer. The gas system is famously reliable, and the gun shoots all loads without adjustment. It comes in just below $1000, at $999.

The latest in the family of Browning/Winchester semiautos spawned by the Browning Gold back in the ’90s, the X4 is made as a price-point gun, but it still comes packed with features. A redesign of the X3, it features a larger square safety, bolt handle, and bolt release button for easier use in cold weather. It has a bright, TruGlo bead, too, for those who like them, and inside it has the same excellent, reliable, easy-care, soft-shooting gas system of the Gold/X2/X3 family. It comes in 12 gauge, black synthetic at just over $1000.

The ACE represents an excellent buy in an inertia gun. Well-made in a very modern Turkish factory, it features deep-drilled barrels for better patterns, a trigger group that you can remove with the push of a button, and a bolt that can’t be bumped out of battery. The two-piece receiver gives you easy access to the action if you need to maintain it in the field. The “Airy” recoil pad works, and the gun shoots a very flat 50/50 pattern. After a couple of early bobbles, mine has hit its stride and become very reliable. The gun also comes in an ACE version without the square-backed receiver, and both are made in 3 ½-inch 12, 3-inch 20, and 3-inch 28 gauges. They come in wood and synthetic models.

The CZ Drake’s price tag reads like a typo. No O/U should cost as little as it does, yet it’s a solid gun that gives you all the advantages of a break action—two chokes, compact balance—at low semiauto pricing. Made in Turkey, the Drake comes in 12, 20, 28, and .410. It doesn’t have much in the way of engraving or fancy wood, and it has extractors that lift spent shells instead of kicking them out like ejectors. In short, it’s a bare-bones O/U that works, and that’s no small thing for $629 complete with hard case and five choke tubes.

The current version of the Orion, Weatherby’s long-time entry-level O/U, is a solid, dependable Turkish-made O/U. It has a low-profile action and a racy Prince of Wales grip. The Grade A walnut stock of the Matte Blue version is matte-finished to match the matte metal. The gun comes in 12- and 20-gauges, with a choice of 26- or 28-inch barrels in 12-gauge and 28-inch in 20. The barrel has a nicely subdued brass bead, and ventilated top and mid-ribs. In all, it’s an excellent value, and if you want to step up to traditional Weatherby glossiness, you can pay $100 more for the Orion I.

As the price of O/Us climbs, the Instinct holds the line at the sub-$2000 price point. The entry-level Instinct, the L model, features a case-colored receiver and Grade A satin-walnut wood. The guns have Prince of Wales grips, rounded forends, and vent top and mid-ribs. They come with a full set of tubes and a small fiber-optic bead. The 12-gauge weighs in around 7 pounds; the 20 is 6 ½, just right for carrying and shooting in the uplands. If you want a lighter gun, you’ll have to pay more for the alloy-framed Instinct SL.

Used models are the best budget shotguns going. The original buyer takes the depreciation hit, you get a deal, and as a bonus, someone else puts the first ding in the stock, saving you the anguish of worrying about it. If it weren’t for used guns, I’d hardly have any guns at all. Still, you have to be careful. Do your homework. The Blue Book of Gun Values is your guide to how much you should pay. It lists virtually every gun made and its price across a range of conditions.

Buy from a dealer who stands behind what they sell. Be sure you know what the return policy is before you buy. If you buy from an online seller, use only those with good ratings, and make sure there’s an inspection period, typically three days. Find out if inspection includes test-firing.

Check the gun carefully for function. I recently bought a Model 12 Winchester with a slide release that was very difficult to press (it’s a thing with Model 12s), and I negotiated the necessary repairs as part of the price. See if the choke tubes come out. I picked up a Mossberg 835 turkey gun, and when I checked it out, I found the choke tube was rusted in place. The gun was already dirt-cheap, but the store knocked another big chunk off the price when I pointed out the stuck tube.

Look for signs like marred screws that shows someone who didn’t know what they were doing has been poking around inside. Be sure neither the barrel nor the stock has been cut down, and look for signs of refinishing. If you need to, have a gunsmith look the gun over, just as you might take a used car to your mechanic. He can spot problems and keep you from buying what looks like a great budget shotgun but is actually a lemon.

Finally, consider resale value. Someday you may want to move on from the gun even though right now you’re smitten with it. I’m terrible about this, but I’m learning. A little while ago, I passed up a terrific deal on an older Browning Citori skeet gun with great wood and a full set of sub-gauge tubes. But it had 26-inch barrels, and the current trend is toward longer ones. I knew if and when I soured on the gun, the short barrels would make it tough to sell, so I let it go. Whether you are buying new or used, a budget means you have a limited amount to spend. And whether that’s a few hundred dollars or a couple thousand, you want to get the most for your money.

“Never sell a gun,” some sage told me years ago. While it seemed like sound advice at the time, it’s not practical. Guns pile up. You have to thin the herd occasionally. Sometimes you need money right away for emergencies; other times you want to make room for something new. There are several ways to sell a gun, from taking it to a gun store or pawn shop to selling it at auction or through private sales.

The Blue Book of Gun Values is updated annually. It’s the standard most buyers and sellers rely on. You can also search online auction sites to see how much guns like yours have sold for.

If you sell a gun to a gun shop, you get money right away—but you might receive only half the gun’s value or even less. Stores have overhead, and they can’t give you the full value of the gun if they want to make any money on the resale. Scopes and other accessories rarely add much to a gun’s price. If you think you might need them in the future, take them off and sell the gun by itself. You’ll generally get a better deal if you trade the gun toward something at the shop that you want. If you need money right away, sell the gun, take your lumps graciously on the price, and walk out the door with cash in hand.

Ask if your gun shop sells on consignment. They’ll work with you to set the price, display the gun, sell it, and take a commission when the gun sells. Some guns go right away, while I’ve seen others sit on the rack for a year or more until someone meets the seller’s price.

You’ll reach the widest possible market if you sell online through GunBroker.com or a similar site. Take lots of pictures, including any scratches, dents, and dings, and be scrupulously honest in your description to minimize the chance of returns. You’ll be responsible for shipping the gun, and the auction site will take a commission when the gun sells.

Private sales (where legal; see below) will get you the most money for your gun. Ideally, you’ll sell it to a friend who wants it, and you’re done with no hassle. Failing that, advertise the gun locally—the bulletin boards at shooting ranges are good places— and sell it. Only ask top dollar if you’re willing to wait a long time for your money. Again, let the Blue Book be your guide.

Be sure you know your state’s laws. A growing number of states require private sales to go through a Federal Firearms License (FFL) holder who can perform background checks. Laws for selling handguns are often stricter than laws governing long gun sales. Be careful and know the law.

Our writers and editors eat, sleep, and breathe the outdoors, and that passion comes through in our product reviews. You can count on F&S to keep you up to date on the best new gear. And when we write about a product—whether it’s a bass lure or a backpack—we cover the good and the bad, so you know exactly what to expect before you decide to make a purchase.

After being laid off by Microsoft, former Doom devs found new Smash TV-influenced FPS studio that isn't "subject to investor demands"

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Their first project: televised deathgame shooter Danger Pay

Back in July, Doom developers id Software laid off around a hundred people as part of wider Microsoft blood-letting. Now, a few of those devs have founded a new employee-owned studio, Hidden Horses. Their first project is Danger Pay, a single-player FPS influenced by ancient arcade blaster Smash TV (pictured) and dystopian movies such as deadly gameshow fable The Running Man – itself an influence on Smash TV.

Hidden Horses have 20 id Software alumni on the books, including former lead VFX artist Todd Boyce, principal engine programmer Dustin Land, senior AI and gameplay engineer Daniel Young, producer Andrew Willis, senior technical designer Jordan Turner, principal technical designer Tim Locke, and senior skybox artist Allison Dombek. They're looking to make "the kind of old school shooters that we think people want more of," in Land's words.

All members of Hidden Horses own a share of the company, with bosses "democratically" appointed, in the words of a FAQ. "Our structure is quite flat, and we elect leaders when needed," Willis told Vertical Slice in a separate interview. "Financially, this means we are not subject to investor demands and the preservation of the company and the jobs it sustains is paramount over needless growth and our 'capital' doesn't compete for returns with other potential investments."

All of which is obviously gesturing towards the horse elephant in the room: Microsoft, who've made enormous cuts to their Xbox biz in their eagerness to further supercharge their $4 trillion market value.

"We've replicated some things, changed some things, and added others [from our id Software days]," Willis went on. "Overall, I think top-down hierarchy is something we all have bad experiences with—we also don't want to trend chase or make a game by focus group. We want to make the kinds of classic gameplay focused games that we would play ourselves. We think there is a market for that and our experiences with the gaming community in our past work history informs that."

Willis's hope is that the "flat" structure will motivate staff to work harder, because they feel like they have a meaningful say over the company's direction. "It allows all ideas to be considered and means we don't miss out on any contributions from our employees just because they don't sit high up in some arbitrary hierarchy," he said..

I'm cynical about "flat" company structures, myself. I think they are very often just an excuse for the biggest egos in a company to throw their weight around. It can work given a friendly initial mix of founders, but it gets complicated when you start hiring new people who don't have the same chemistry. Mind you, there are no plans to expand the company dramatically for the foreseeable. "We want to create a vehicle that allows us to make games for our livelihoods, not to make crazy profits and get 100x returns," Willis went on. I'm also a bit leery of the fact that Hidden Horses have popped up a Kickstarter, because I'm leery about crowd-funding in general, even when Peter Molyneux isn't involved. The studio founders are currently working on some promotional initiatives to drum up support, including a studio documentary and *squints* a promotional beer? Still, I'm happy to hear that at least a few Cacodemon wranglers jettisoned by the big M have landed on their hooves feet. I wish Hidden Horses the best of luck with it all. As for Danger Pay, proper screens, footage and release details are to follow.

Rock Paper Shotgun is better when you sign in
